proceed with the Magnus breakdown once again before terminating in a free group.
                                                                              t
One can easily generalize the above example (14) with e.g. the relator ra, apa q s “ 1,
etc., in which choosing to break down using a immediately yields a free group, but
breaking down using t can take an arbitrary finite number of steps to reach a free
group. We also here mention in this connection a result of Weinbaum [Wei72] who
proved in 1972 that in a one-relator group xA | r “ 1y, with r cyclically reduced,
no proper non-empty subword of r is equal to 1. Thus even this result, which
seems straightforward, is not immediate to prove using Magnus’ solution to the
word problem. We will discuss the time complexity, and related questions, of the
word problem in one-relator groups in §1.8.3. Before this, however, we must first
cross the barren landscape of inactivity that followed Magnus’ results.


                          1.4. The Lull Phase (1932–1960)
    Following Magnus’ 1932 solution to the word problem in all one-relator groups,
progress on one-relator groups slowed down. Indeed, as for significant theorems
related directly to one-relator groups themselves, in the next three decades or so
only one would be proved: the Identity Theorem of Lyndon. We will discuss this
result in §1.4.2. However, these three decades, which we will refer to as the lull
phase, saw a number of very significant developments in combinatorial group theory
as a subject, many of which would eventually end up being directly relevant to one-
relator group theory. In this section, we will give an overview of this phase of the
development of one-relator group theory, ending it in §1.4.4 by discussing some
reasons for what caused progress to slow down, and subsequently resume.

     1.4.1. Some results on free groups. We begin with some developments on
the theory of free groups that occurred in the 1930s. As we shall see, they would
have rather an important impact on the development of one-relator group theory
in the 1960s, particularly the notion of residual nilpotence. We begin with Magnus’
main result during this time: the representation of free groups in (the group of
units of) a certain ring. This can be fairly easily explained. Let ξ1 , . . . , ξn be n
non-commuting variables, and let Zrrξ1 , . . . , ξn ss be the ring of formal power series
                             1.4. THE LULL PHASE (1932–1960)                              29


in the non-commuting variables. Let ai “ 1`ξi . Then each ai is, in fact, invertible,
with inverse given by
                                                        8
                                                        ÿ
(15)                a´1
                      i “ 1 ´ ξ i ` ξ 2
                                      i ´ ξ 3
                                            i ` ¨ ¨ ¨ “   p´1qj ξij .
                                                         j“0

Thus the ai generate a group; and Magnus proved that the group they generate is
a free group. This has a number of immediate consequences.
     Theorem 1.4.1 (Magnus, 1935 [Mag35]). The group generated by 1 ` ξi (1 ď
i ď n) is free of rank n, freely generated by these generators. As a consequence,
free groups are residually nilpotent.
     Here, a group G is said to be residually nilpotent if the intersection of all
terms in the lower central series of G is trivial; alternatively, we will see a more
general definition of residual properties in §1.4.3. The residual nilpotence of free
groups is a direct consequence in Theorem 1.4.1 as follows: let F be a free group.
Then any element of the kth term of the lower central series of F , considered as
an element of Zrrξ1 , . . . , ξn ss in the above representation, must have the degree
of a certain component be at least k (see [MKS66, p. 313]). This implies, of
course, that the terms of the lower central series intersect trivially. A standard
reference for this representation, now often called the Magnus representation, is
[MKS66, Chapter 5]. See also [Che54, Cha68] for some extensions to other
finitely generated groups; indeed, the representation was in the first place motivated
in part by a 1928 article by Shoda [Sho28] on the automorphism group of abelian
groups.
     The Magnus representation would end up inspiring much research on repre-
senting free groups. While we cannot write the full history here (it shall instead
appear elsewhere), one particularly famous case bears a brief mention. In the late
1930s, a group of three Soviet students working in St Petersburg, supervised by
V. A. Tartakovskii, picked up the subject: these were Kh. A. Doniyakhi, D. I.
Fuchs-Rabinovich, and I. N. Sanov. First, Fuchs-Rabinovich proved results about
“determinators” of free groups, and uses these to recover Magnus’ theorem above
on the residual nilpotence of free groups; this appeared in a 1940 article (written
in English!) [FR40b]21. Doniyakhi proved results about extending these results
to free products of abelian groups [Don40]. Fuchs-Rabinovich [FR40a], in an-
other article, also proved results about representing free groups in SL2 pCq, giving
another proof of Magnus’ result. At this time, Sanov [San40] instead worked on
a different problem, namely the Burnside problem for exponent 4, which he solved
affirmatively in 1940.
     The three students would have rather different fates. When war broke out
in 1941, Fuchs-Rabinovich was drafted into the USSR Baltic Fleet. He died of
dysentery in 1942, during the Siege of Leningrad, at the age of 29. Doniyakhi was
also drafted, and died in battle less than two months later in 1941, at the age of
24. Sanov survived the war.22 After the war, Sanov would carry on the research
his fellow students had initiated, and in 1947 published the famous article [San47].

  21Lyndon [Lyn53] later elaborated on this article and its connection with Magnus’ work, and
we primarily refer the interested reader to consult this latter source.
  22Indeed, Sanov served as Head of Mathematics for one year at the University of Pyongyang
(North Korea) in 1949.
30                                         1. HISTORY


This begins by acknowledging the articles by Fuchs-Rabinovich and Doniyakhi, then
presents his theorem: the (multiplicative) group generated by the two matrices
                            ˆ      ˙          ˆ     ˙
                              1 2               1 0
(16)                                    and
                              0 1               2 1
is a free group of rank 2. This can be used to give yet another proof of the residual
nilpotence of free groups, and would directly inspire many important results in
combinatorial group theory, including the Ping-Pong Lemma [Mac63].
     Sanov and his friends were of course not the only researchers to be affected by
the war; we have already seen in §1.2.2 how Dehn was affected. Magnus, who had
been appointed as an assistant at Königsberg in 1939, was forced to resign from
this position shortly thereafter because he refused to join the NSDAP (the Nazi
Party). Instead, he found employment at Telefunken, a radio company, during the
war.23 This abrupt break in his usual research environment could also explain the
lack of progress on one-relator group theory during this time, cf. §1.4.4.

    Let us now turn back to some further results on free groups pertaining to one-
relator groups. The first concerns the study of primitive elements in free groups, as
discussed in §1.2.3. Whitehead, using topological methods, proved two remarkable
theorems in this direction in a 1936 article [Whi36]. The first states simply that
there is an algorithm for deciding whether or not a given word is a primitive element
or not. That is, one can decide whether a given element w P FA can be part of
a basis or not (cf. Nielsen’s result Proposition 1.2.4, which gives an algorithm for
deciding if a pair of words is a basis for F2 ). Furthermore, Whitehead completely
characterized when a one-relator group is free.
    Theorem 1.4.2 (Whitehead, 1936 [Whi36, Theorem 4]). Let G “ xA | r “ 1y
with r non-trivial. Then G is free if and only if r is a primitive element of FA .
    As we shall see in §1.6, there is also a complete characterization of when a
one-relator group is virtually free (Theorem 1.6.8), but this would not be proved
until 1972.24 Next, a result which can be seen as a generalization of Whitehead’s
result was proved by Magnus in 1939.
    Theorem 1.4.3 (Magnus, 1939 [Mag39]). Let G be a group given by a pre-
sentation with n ` r generators and r defining relations, with n, r ě 0. Then:
     (1) If G can be generated by n elements, then G is free of rank n.
     (2) If Gab – Zn , then some set of n of the generators of G generate a free
         group.
     (3) If G has the same sequence of lower central factors as the free group on
         n ` r generators, then G is free.

    Thus Theorem 1.4.3(2) can also be seen as an extension of the Freiheitssatz to
the case of more than one defining relation. We delay the definition of lower central

   23During this time, he would gain an interest in special functions, which would eventually lead
to him joining a project with Erdélyi, Oberhettinger & Tricomi to publish their famous book
[EMOT53] on transcendental functions. This book remains, by a wide margin, Magnus’ most
cited work.
   24Indeed, the fact that a non-free virtually free one-relator group must have elements of finite
order is not at all obvious, but follows from Stallings’ theorem [Sta70].

     Finally, we remark on a set of results that relate to one-relator groups only by
association. In his 1931 article, his second on one-relator groups, referenced above,
Magnus [Mag31] also proved that the commutator subgroup of the modular group
G “ xa, b | a2 “ b3 “ 1y – C2 ˚ C3 is a free group of rank 2. His argument
is essentially a simplified version of the machinery developed to deal with one-

free products of finite cyclic groups, proving using a geometric argument that that
the commutator subgroup of Cm1 ˚ Cm2 ˚ ¨ ¨ ¨ ˚ Cmr is free of rank

Many more results could be mentioned here, as we now are heading head-first into
the dawn of combinatorial group theory. We have said nothing, for example, of
the automorphism groups of free groups, a topic which interested Magnus, and
many others, around this time. However, these subjects increasingly stray from
one-relator groups, to which we now instead firmly return.
     1.4.2. Lyndon’s Identity Theorem. Recall Dyck’s fundamental result (see
§1.2.1) that in a group G with underlying free group F and given by defining
relations r1 , . . . , rn , an element W is equal to one in G if and only if it can be
written of the form
                           m
                           ź
(17)             W “          Ti rt˘1
                                   i
                                      Ti´1 where Ti P F, and ti “ 1, 2, . . . , n.
                      i“1
One might ask how unique such an expression (17). For example, in the group
xa, b | ra, bs “ 1y, isomorphic to Z2 , we obviously have ra2 , b2 s “ 1, since Z2 is
commutative. This equality is witnessed by the fact that:
                 #              `     ˘     `         ˘ `        ˘
                    ra2 , b2 s “ aRa´1 ¨ R ¨ baRpbaq´1 ¨ bRb´1
(18)                            `     ˘ `          ˘    `        ˘
                    ra2 , b2 s “ aRa´1 ¨ abRpabq´1 ¨ R ¨ bRb´1
where we have let R “ ra, bs “ aba´1 b´1 . How different are the two expressions in
(18)? Up to reordering the terms, we notice that they are identical, except for the
conjugates of R by ab resp. ba. However, ab “ ba in Z2 , and so the two expressions
in (18) are identical up to reordering the terms and equality in G. This is an instance
of Lyndon’s Identity Theorem, which states that this behaviour always occurs in a
torsion-free one-relator group; there are no “relations between the relations”.
     Formally, the problem of determining relations between relations asks: what
are the non-trivial relations holding between the relations r1 , . . . , rn of the form
               źm
(19)               Ti rt˘1
                        i
                           Ti´1 “ 1 where Ti P F, and ti “ 1, 2, . . . , n?
                i“1
There are some trivial relations always holding; for example, if r1 , r2 are two dif-
ferent relations, then pr2 r1 r2´1 q ¨ pr2 q ¨ pr1´1 q ¨ pr2´1 q “ 1, which is a product of the
form (19) but not a particularly interesting one. Such relations are called Peiffer
identities. According to Chandler & Magnus [CM82, p. 118], the idea of studying
identities between relations had been described by Dehn to Magnus in the early
1930s, but this was not pursued further at the time. Instead, this would not be
32                                        1. HISTORY


done until the late 1940s, when it was investigated by Peiffer (a student of Reide-
meister) in 1949 [Pei49b] and, in the same year, by Reidemeister [Rei49]. The
latter proved that the problem of finding all non-trivial relations (19) reduces to
understanding the case when all Ti are taken modulo r1 , . . . , rn , and disregarding
the order of the factors in the product. If we set G “ F {R, i.e. R is the normal
closure of r1 , . . . , rn , then this amounts to stating that we must understand the
group R{rR, Rs. This abelian group is naturally a ZG-module via the conjugation
action of G, and is called the relation module of G (with respect to r1 , . . . , rn , see
below). Furthermore, it can be shown that the action of G on R{rR, Rs is faithful,
i.e. only the identity element fixes all of R{rR, Rs, see [AL55].
     In 1948, Reidemeister visited the Institute for Advanced Study for a year, which
had a great influence on a young postdoc in Princeton at that time: R. Lyndon,
who had been a PhD student of S. Mac Lane’s [App84, p. 3]. Reidemeister,
who took an early interest in one-relator groups (see e.g. his 1932 proof of the
Freiheitssatz in §1.3.1), does not appear to have been the one to introduce Lyndon
to one-relator groups. Instead, Lyndon recalls that after presenting his projective
resolution (see below) in a seminar at the IAS, Reidemeister came up to him and
explained a natural topological interpretation of his resolution [Rat84b, p. 25],
cf. also [Lyn50, p. 651]. Thus in the late 1940s, combinatorial group theory was
certainly on Lyndon’s mind, and in a brief 1949 article [Lyn49] he proved a result
for the cohomology of groups defined by generators and relations, before finally
turning his attention to one-relator groups. He was able to prove the following
theorem, by using the Magnus breakdown (§1.3.1):
    Theorem 1.4.4 (Lyndon’s Identity Theorem, 1950 [Lyn50]). Let  ś G “ xA | r “
1y, with r “ wn where w is not a proper power and n ě 1. Then i Ti rεi Ti´1 “ 1
implies that the indices can be grouped into pairs pi, jq such that ei “ ´ej and
Ti ” Tj wci in G for some 1 ď ci ď n.
     In other words, there are no non-trivial relations holding among the relation
of a one-relator group. We now give a restatement of the theorem in more modern
terminology, using the language of homological algebra. Let us make clear that
at this point, group cohomology was a nascent area of research, and many of the
general statements that are today a standard part of the subject were not yet
formulated.25 We begin with a restatement of Theorem 1.4.4 in the case of n “ 1,
i.e. the torsion-free case (see §1.5.1):
Corollary 1.4.5 (Lyndon [Lyn50, p. 663]). Let G “ xA | r “ 1y be a torsion-free
one-relator group. Then the relation module of G with respect to this presentation
is a free ZG-module.
    The relation module and the second homology group of any group are, as
is well-known (and as we shall see below), closely related; this was observed by
Peiffer [Pei49a] and Miller [Mil52]. We can thus use the Identity Theorem to gain
information about the cohomological dimension of a one-relator group:

   25To highlight this, we remark that group cohomology had essentially only been computed for
finite abelian groups at the time of Lyndon’s 1947 PhD thesis [Lyn47]. The subject of this thesis,
later published as [Lyn48], was the cohomology of group extensions, leading to what is today
known as the Lyndon–Serre-Hochschild spectral sequence. For a lucid account of this early history
of group cohomology, we refer the reader to [ML84].
                            1.4. THE LULL PHASE (1932–1960)                            33


Corollary 1.4.6 (Lyndon [Lyn50, Corollary 11.2]). Let G be a torsion-free one-
relator group. Then G has cohomological dimension at most 2.
     Let us now give a restatement of the Identity Theorem in terms of an explicit
resolution. Let G “ xx1 , . . . , xm | rn “ 1y be a one-relator group with r not a
proper power and n ě 1. By attaching a 1-cell for each generator and a single 2-cell
for the defining relator, we can always find a sequence of free G-modules
                                     B
                                     2     1 B
                                 F2 ÝÑ F1 ÝÑ ZÑ0
where F2 is free of rank 1, and F1 is free of rank m. Lyndon’s Identity Theorem then
states that the kernel of B2 is generated by the element r´1. If n “ 1, then of course
r ´ 1 “ 0 in ZG, so it follows that B2 is injective, and hence any torsion-free one-
relator group G has cohomological dimension 2, and one can explicitly compute
the (co)homology groups. For example, for trivial coefficients it follows almost
immediately from Corollary 1.4.5 that H2 pG, Zq “ Z if and only if r P rF, F s, and
otherwise H2 pG, Zq “ 0. Hughes [Hug66] used the Identity Theorem to compute
the cohomology of G “ F {R with coefficients in the relation module R{R1 . Then,
when r “ wn with n ě 1 and w not a proper power, he proves that H k pG, R{R1 q
is trivial for odd k and cyclic of order n for even k ą 2. Furthermore, he shows
that H 2 pG, R{R1 q is an extension of an easily describable group DpGq by a cyclic
group of order n. For example, in the case of G “ π1 pKg q from (4), Hughes
is able to show that H 2 pπ1 pKg q, R{R1 q – Z. Finally, we remark that in 1984
Ratcliffe [Rat84a] gave an explicit description of the cup product ! : H 1 pG, M q ˆ
H 1 pG, M q Ñ H 1 pG, M q for an arbitrary ZG-module M .
     In 1963, Cohen & Lyndon [CL63] gave a new proof of the Identity Theorem,
and strengthened it by giving an explicit free basis of the relation module. Con-
cretely, if G “ xA | rn “ 1y, with r P FA not a proper power and n ě 1, then
Cohen & Lyndon construct a set X Ă FA of words such that tx´1 rn x | x P Xu
is a free basis of the normal closure xxrn yy in FA . Their results, proved via the
usual Magnus hierarchy but with a stronger inductive hypothesis, also included, as
a byproduct, a new proof of the Freiheitssatz. Two other simpler proofs are due to
Karrass & Solitar [KS72] and [Bur74], in 1972 and 1974 respectively.
     We end with a word of caution regarding the terminology “relation module of
a one-relator group”. Indeed, relation modules depend on the presentation chosen,
although if G – F {R – F {S, then it is known that there is a free ZG-module X
such that R{R1 ‘ X – S{S 1 ‘ X, see [Lyn62]. By combining the Identity Theorem
with the above statement, we find that the relation module of a one-relator group
G – F {S is always ZG-projective. However, if S is not the normal closure of a
single element – even though G is isomorphic to a one-relator group! – then the
relation module S{S 1 need not be ZG-free. Indeed, Dunwoody [Dun72] showed
that in the case of the trefoil knot group G “ xa, b | a2 “ b3 y (see §1.2.2), there is a
presentation G – F {S for which the relation module S{S 1 cannot be generated by
a single element, and indeed cannot contain ZG as a direct summand. Thus S{S 1
is not free, but it is projective.
     1.4.3. Residual properties of groups. The notion of residual properties
goes back rather far, and while we will not attempt to survey it in full here, we will
mention some generalities. Let P be a property of groups (e.g. being finite, free,
etc.). A group G is termed residually P if for every 1 ‰ g P G there exists a group
Hg with property P and a homomorphism φg : G Ñ Hg such that φpgq ‰ 1. The
34                                        1. HISTORY


terminology residually P is originally due to P. Hall [Hal54, p. 349] in 1954, but
the concept can be traced further back. For example, in 1940 Mal’cev [Mal40],
who called residually finite groups finitely approximable, proved that every finitely
generated linear group is residually finite. He also proved, in connection to the Hopf
problem (the connection of which to one-relator groups we shall explore thoroughly
in §1.5.2) that no finitely generated linear group is hopfian; his argument can be
easily extended to show that in fact every finitely generated residually finite group
is hopfian.
     Free groups are the prototypical examples of residually finite groups. Even
before Mal’cev’s result on linear groups – and free groups are linear, using e.g. the
representation by Fuchs-Rabinovich [FR40a], see §1.4.1 – it was well-known that
free groups are residually finite. For example, in 1933 Levi [Lev33, Theorem 1]
gave a proof of the residual finiteness of free groups. Indeed, he proved the stronger
statement that the intersection of any decreasing series of characteristic subgroups
in a free group is trivial, which shows that, in particular, free groups are residually
nilpotent (cf. Magnus’ 1934 proof in §1.4.1). For other early proofs of the residual
finiteness of free groups, see also [Iwa43, Neu61]. Free groups are also known
to satisfy the stronger property of being residually p-groups for any prime p, as
proved by Marshall Hall [Hal50] and Takahasi [Tak51]. Another natural class of
residually finite groups are the finitely generated nilpotent groups, as proved by
Hirsch in 1946 [Hir46]. Thus residually nilpotent groups are also residually finite,
and we will encounter many examples of residually nilpotent groups throughout this
survey. Residual nilpotence, in particular, has a strong connection with the Magnus
representation of free groups (§1.4.1) and many results on residual nilpotence of
particular quotients of free groups and related topics, e.g. a well-known result due
to Gruenberg [Gru62] or Neumann [Neu62], can often be proved by appealing to
the Magnus representation, see [BH66].
     One of the advantages of residual finiteness is that any finitely presented resid-
ually finite group has decidable word problem. The algorithm is simple: given such
a group G “ xA | Ry, to decide whether a given word w P FA is equal to 1 in G
or not we run two algorithms in parallel: (1) enumerate all words equal to 1 in G;
and (2) enumerate all homomorphisms from G onto finite groups. If w “ 1, then
the first algorithm detects this; if w ‰ 1, then by residual finiteness the second
algorithm will detect this. Thus G has decidable word problem.26 Since all one-
relator groups have decidable word problem (§1.3.2, Theorem 1.3.6), this raises the
following very natural question: is every one-relator group residually finite? The
answer is a resounding no, and we will encounter many such counterexamples in
the form of Baumslag–Solitar groups in §1.5.3, which are of central importance due
to giving rise to non-hopfian groups. Finally, we mention that Meskin [Mes74]
    26This algorithm is often attributed to McKinsey, particularly to his 1943 article [McK43,
Theorem 3], but this is not accurate, for several reasons. First, McKinsey works in a considerably
more general context of “finite reducibility” in algebras, and as McKinsey himself points out in a
footnote, “this principle has been known for a long time [...] It was employed by L. Löwenheim
[...] [McKinsey] also made use of it in his article [McK41]” [McK43, p. 69, footnote 6]. The
reference to Löwenheim refers to Hilbert & Ackermann [HA38, p. 95], which in turn refers to a
1915 article by Löwenheim [Löw15], wherein the principle is admittedly well-disguised. The first
to write the principle in its modern form, purely speaking of groups, was Mostowski [Mos66] in
1966 and, independently, V. Dyson [Dys64] (see also [Dys74]). The argument was also known
to G. Higman and A. Turing, but not published (as pointed out by Boone and Schupp in their
MR review of [Mos66]).
                              1.4. THE LULL PHASE (1932–1960)                                  35


proved in 1974 that there exist finitely generated residually finite groups with un-
decidable word problem. Hence, for the above algorithm, the assumption of finite
presentability is crucial.
     1.4.4. Why did progress halt? As we shall see in the next section (§1.5),
the area of one-relator group theory would see a resurgence of interest after the
lull phase ended just before 1960. As described by Chandler & Magnus, Baumslag
proposed the following idea to explain the lull phase: “the Freiheitssatz and the
subsequent solution of the word problem for one-relator groups by Magnus left the
impression that one-relator groups are something almost as easy to deal with as free
groups and that this delayed a more thorough investigation of one-relator groups
for quite a while.” [CM82, p. 119]. While somewhat compelling, we would like
to add two further suggestions to this, which have not appeared elsewhere in the
literature.
     First, given the large number of PhD students supervised by Magnus who
would end up active in the area of one-relator group theory, it also bears noticing
that Magnus only had two PhD students while in Berlin (F. Oberhettinger and
F. Shäfke) with neither working in group theory. As soon as he arrived in New
York in 1950, however, he started supervising a large number of doctoral students,
with the first graduating in 1954; one of the first was Abe Shenitzer27 [She54],
who gave a necessary and sufficient condition for a one-relator group to split as
a free product. This result can thus be seen as Magnus’ return to considering
one-relator groups, following the turbulence of the war. Magnus would end up
supervising 66 PhD students at New York University between 1950 and 1974, with
some ending up as the most important names in the theory of one-relator groups
and combinatorial group theory: to mention a few early names, we have D. Solitar
(1958), M. Greendlinger (1960), B. Chandler (1962), and J. Birman (1968). The
same idea can be proposed for B. H. Neumann, given his early contact with Magnus
while in Berlin. He moved to the University of Manchester in 1948, where he
supervised both J. Britton (1954) and G. Baumslag (1958), both of whom would
be of central importance to the subject (in this line, cf. also [CM82, pp. 197–198]).
     The second reason, and in our view more important, is the general undecidabil-
ity of the word problem. We first give a brief summary of these developments. The
word problem, having originated with Dehn and Thue as outlined in §1.2.1, gave
rise to the first undecidability result in “real” mathematics, following relatively ar-
tificial undecidable problems on Turing machines as given by Turing [Tur36]. This
first “real” undecidability result was the 1947 proof by Post [Pos47] and, indepen-
dently Markov [Mar47b, Mar47a], that there is a finitely presented semi group
with undecidable word problem. This would in short order lead to the undecid-
ability of the word problem for groups. Turing [Tur50] worked on the problem, in


   27Shenitzer and Magnus would develop a life-long friendship, summarized briefly in the touch-
ing tribute [She95]. In particular, Abe Shenitzer (1921–2022) had been imprisoned in several
concentration camps during the war. After the war, he arrived in New York, and studied under
Magnus. Early on in their friendship, Magnus, visibly moved, said “You’ve done more for me
than any person can do for another. [...] You are a Jew who was in German concentration camps,
and I am a German”, cf. also [Sti22]. Magnus choosing to return to his pre-war work on one-
relator groups at this point might thus have been in large part stimulated by his interaction with
Shenitzer.
36                                        1. HISTORY


1950 proving the undecidability of the word problem in cancellative semigroups28,
a class of semigroups in which either of xy “ xz or yx “ zx implies y “ z. This
article would be used29 by Novikov [Nov52, Nov55] to produce a proof of the un-
decidability of the word problem in groups; this was done independently by Boone
[Boo57, Boo59] and Britton [Bri58, Bri63] around the same time. Today, it is
known that there exists a 12-relator group with undecidable word problem [Bor69],
arising from an encoding of a 3-relation semigroup with undecidable word problem
due to Matiyasevich [Mat67].
     In connection with the undecidability of the word problem, in the mid-1950s
a number of further undecidability results for groups would appear, including the
famous Adian–Rabin Theorem, stating that deciding “most” reasonable properties
(e.g. finiteness, triviality, being abelian or free, etc.) about finitely presented groups
is undecidable [Adi55, Rab58, NB22]. Thus, around 1960 the area of combi-
natorial group theory had undergone something of a shock. Indeed, one might
reasonably speculate that a contemporary researcher reading Magnus’ 1932 article
[Mag32] would imagine that perhaps the same method could be extended, at great
combinatorial cost and with little gain, to all finitely presented groups, and that
therefore there was nothing particularly special about one-relator groups per se.
The undecidability results of the 1950s shattered this illusion. Now, research could
be directed to classes of groups where the word problem was decidable in order to
see what was special about them, because something had to be special if the word
problem were to be decidable. One-relator groups certainly turned out to be special
indeed.

              1.5. The resurgence of one-relator groups (1960s)
Topology gave rise to combinatorial group theory. As we have seen in §1.2, the early
articles on one-relator groups were all clearly motivated by topology, with surface
groups and knot groups being particular flagship examples. As the machinery of
one-relator groups and combinatorial group theory grew increasingly powerful, it
also became increasingly useful to reapply these tools back to answer topological
questions. In the late 1950s, an important connection between the Poincaré Con-
jecture and combinatorial group theory was made by C. Papakyriakopoulos. While
this connection would not result in a proof of the Poincaré Conjecture, it did re-
sult, directly and indirectly, in the development of one-relator group theory. Other
topological questions, related directly to the old hopfian problem, would also play
a key role during this time. In this section, we will outline how these topological
considerations led to a resurgence of one-relator group theory, and some of the
subsequent developments in the 1960s. We will also showcase Magnus’ continued
influence on the area by illustrating how one of his PhD students gave rise to a new
school of combinatorial group theory in the USSR.
  28We mention here another connection to B. H. Neumann: around 1950, when he and Turing
were both at the University of Manchester, the two of them had announced a resolution to the
word problem for groups. However, their resolutions were contradictory: Turing announced the
undecidability of the word problem in finitely presented groups, whereas Neumann announced the
general decidability of the same. Both later retracted their claims, but Turing’s work led to the
undecidability for cancellative semigroups in the work [Tur50] cited above [Col85, p. 169].
  29Turing’s article contains a number of gaps, somewhat amended by Boone [Boo58], and
Novikov & Adian [NA58] later produced their own proof of the same result to avoid this depen-
dency.
                1.5. THE RESURGENCE OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS (1960S)                               37


     1.5.1. The Poincaré Conjecture and torsion. Around this time, new stir-
rings of topological methods appeared in combinatorial group theory. An important
stirrer was C. D. Papakyriakopoulos, who rose to prominence by giving a proof of
Dehn’s Lemma in 1957 [Pap57a, Pap57b] (the original “proof” of this result,
given by Dehn [Deh10] in 1910, had a gap, as pointed out by Kneser [Kne29,
p. 260]). Soon thereafter, Papakyriakopoulos [Pap58] became interested in prov-
ing the Poincaré Conjecture30, and in 1963 [Pap63b, Pap63c] he considered the
following construction (with a slight correction provided by Maskit [Mas63]). Let
π1 pΣg q be the surface group of genus g ą 1, defined by (3), let w be any word in the
commutator subgroup of π1 pΣg q, and let Pg pwq be the quotient of π1 pΣg q by the
relation ra1 , b1 ws “ 1. Then Papakyriakopoulos was able to reduce the Poincaré
Conjecture to the following group-theoretic conjectures: (a) Pg pwq is torsion-free;
and (b) the cover of Σg corresponding to the kernel of π1 pΣg q Ñ Pg pwq is pla-
nar. In connection to the first of these two conjectures, Papakyriakopoulos wrote a
letter to Magnus, asking whether the then-known methods of combinatorial group
theory could be applied to solve (a). This letter would become the basis of a 1960
article by Karrass, Magnus & Solitar [KMS60] (D. Solitar was a student of Mag-
nus’, and A. Karrass had been a student of Solitar’s). The three authors did not
solve Papakyriakopolous’ question in full, but provided some partial results indi-
cating it had a positive answer. The groups Pg pwq are two-relator groups, and as
part of their investigations of the elements of finite order in this class, the authors
of [KMS60] were able to completely characterize the elements of finite order in
one-relator groups:
    Theorem 1.5.1 (Karrass, Magnus & Solitar, 1960 [KMS60, Theorem 1]). Let
G “ xA | r “ 1y be a one-relator group with r cyclically reduced. Then G has
elements of finite order if and only if r is a proper power in FA , i.e. r “ wn with
n ą 1 and 1 ‰ w P FA .
    The proof, of course, goes via the classical Magnus hierarchy, by induction on
the relator length (Gruenberg [Gru70] gave a cohomological proof of Theorem 1.5.1
in 1970). The theorem states, in other words, that a one-relator group has torsion
precisely when it obviously has torsion, and the authors of [KMS60] further char-
acterize the elements of finite order as the obvious ones, namely those which are
conjugate to a power of the relator. The authors of [KMS60] proceed to provide
some further insight into one-relator groups with torsion as follows:
    Theorem 1.5.2 (Karrass, Magnus & Solitar [KMS60, Theorem 3]). Consider
the two one-relator groups G1 “ xA | r1n “ 1y and G2 “ xA | r2n “ 1y where n ą 1.
If G1 – G2 , then also xA | r1 “ 1y – xA | r2 “ 1y.
    This shows that isomorphisms for one-relator groups with torsion is, in a sense,
at least as “rigid” as isomorphisms of torsion-free one-relator groups. Note that
the converse of Theorem 1.5.2 fails, but this would not be proved until more than a
decade later, see §1.6.3. Indeed, at this point in the story, one-relator groups with
torsion were still viewed with the same lens as their torsion-free counterparts; there
   30The Poincaré Conjecture, which other than its brief connection to one-relator groups ex-
pounded here lies wholly outside the scope of this survey, states: a closed 3-manifold with trivial
fundamental group is homeomorphic to a 3-sphere. The conjecture was proved by Perelman in
the early 2000s; for more historical details, see Stillwell’s translation and commentary in [Poi10,
pp. 1–12].
38                                   1. HISTORY


was even some indication that the presence of torsion was a complicating factor.
When we return to the topic of one-relator groups with torsion in §1.6, we shall see
that this indication was very far from the truth.
     As mentioned above, Karrass, Magnus & Solitar were not able to completely re-
solve Papakyriakopoulos’ conjecture (a), but provided some partial progress [KMS60,
Theorem 5]. Indeed, their work can be seen as a very early form of “relative one-
relator groups”, an area that would grow in the decades to follow [How81, How84,
How87]. In 1964, another PhD student of Magnus’, E. Rapaport, would provide
a full affirmative solution to conjecture (a). In a beautiful 1964 article, Rapaport
[Rap64b] adapted the Magnus hierarchy to the very particular two-relator setting
of conjecture (a), as suggested to her by G. Baumslag (a name we shall encounter
frequently from this point onwards), and was able to prove conjecture (a). For
further work in this direction, see [Pap63a]. Ultimately, the Poincaré Conjecture
would not be proved by means of Papakyriakopoulos’ reduction, as in 1981, Moran
[Mor81] and independently McCool [McC81] proved that conjecture (b) is false,
using a further reduction due to Swarup [Swa79].
     Nevertheless, topological methods were to remain in combinatorial group the-
ory. Indeed, soon after the new proof of Dehn’s Lemma, J. Stallings, motivated in
part by the old work of Kneser [Kne29] and encouraged by Papakyriakopoulos (see
[Sta65b, p. 1]), gave a new proof of Grushko’s theorem on generators of free prod-
ucts using purely topological methods. His proof also dropped the finite generation
hypothesis appearing in Grushko’s proof, reproving the more general result due
to Wagner [Wag57]. Topological methods in combinatorial group theory would
bloom in the coming decades. Stallings’ 1983 article [Sta83] introducing simple
graph-theoretic methods to the study of free groups marked one turning point in
the history of combinatorial group theory; and similarly the “tower method” of Pa-
pakyriakopoulos used to proved Dehn’s Lemma would become crucial in the study
of locally indicable groups and one-relator groups, as pioneered by Howie [How83]
in 1983. These methods, however, pass outside the scope of this present history.
     Instead, we now return to the state of one-relator group theory in 1960, where
many problems from topology motivated further problems and progress. One prob-
lem of particular importance regarded hopfian groups, and it is here that one-relator
groups would begin to reveal the full extent of their rich, versatile, and often myste-
rious nature. We first give a brief background on hopfian groups, and then expand
on how they led to a resurgence of one-relator group theory.


    1.5.2. Hopfian groups. A group is said to be hopfian if it is not isomorphic
to any proper quotient of itself. The terminology arises from the fact that H.
Hopf [Hop31] in 1931 studied the fundamental groups π1 pΣg q of genus g ą 1,
which we defined in (3) and he denoted by Sg . In particular, he proved by a
rather complicated topological argument that π1 pΣg q is hopfian. In this context,
one is naturally lead to ask: are there finitely generated non-hopfian groups? Hopf
asked this question, although not explicitly in any of his own articles; already by
1934, however, Magnus [Mag35, p. 276] noted that he had been informed by B.
H. Neumann of Hopf asking this question. Of ś     course, there are many non-finitely
generated examples of non-hopfian groups, e.g. iPZ Z, but the assumption of finite
generation makes the problem significantly more difficult, and it would take nearly
20 years to resolve (affirmatively).
                1.5. THE RESURGENCE OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS (1960S)                               39


     It was known already at this point that finitely generated free groups were
hopfian; indeed, it is not difficult to prove using Nielsen’s 1921 results (see §1.2.3).
Magnus [Mag35] explicitly gives a proof of the hopficity of free groups. Further-
more, Mal’cev [Mal40] proved in 1940 that any finitely generated linear group is
hopfian. Indeed, he proved that any such group is residually finite, a sufficient condi-
tion for hopficity as mentioned in §1.4.3, and Fuchs-Rabinovich [FR40b] moreover
proved that the any finite free product of finitely generated abelian groups is hop-
fian. One of the first to consider the hopfian problem explicitly was Baer [Bae44]
in 1944, who extended Fuchs-Rabinovich’s theorem to a more general setting. He
also claimed to have given a construction of a finitely generated non-hopfian group
[Bae44, p. 272]; however, this was later withdrawn due to an error [BW49, Appen-
dix, p. 741–743]. Thus, for some time, the problem of finding a finitely generated
non-hopfian group remained open.31
     The first example of a finitely generated non-hopfian group was finally given in
1950 by B. H. Neumann [Neu50]. This group is not finitely presented. The next
year, Higman [Hig51] constructed a two-relator non-hopfian group, given by the
simple presentation
(20)                            xa, b, c | a´1 ca “ b´1 cb “ c2 y.
A further finitely generated non-hopfian group was constructed in 1961 by Hall
[Hal61]. In 1962, Gilbert Baumslag constructed a new example of finitely pre-
sented non-hopfian groups [Bau62a]: take two integral Heisenberg groups, and
amalgamate them over a common free abelian subgroup of rank 2. Up to that
point, the above examples were essentially the only known non-hopfian groups.
But it would not be long before Baumslag would contribute a remarkable number
of results in this direction.
     Gilbert Baumslag (1933–2014) first heard of Wilhelm Magnus in 1956 while
attending lectures at the University of Manchester given by his PhD supervisor B.
H. Neumann [Bau94, p. 99]. The subject of the lectures was amalgamated free
products, which we have encountered before. Neumann pointed out that although
Nielsen introduced amalgamated free products, it was Magnus who recognised their
value in his work on one-relator groups. These amalgamated free products (or
Schreier products, as Baumslag calls them in e.g. [Bau63b]), would become a cen-
tral part of the toolbox he used to prove a remarkable set of theorems.
     Thus, at this point, in 1962, Baumslag would be part of two remarkable results
relating one-relator groups and hopfian groups. The first was a positive result.
He proved that the amalgamated free product of a free group with a free abelian
group, with amalgamated subgroup maximal cyclic in the free group, is residually
free [Bau62b, Theorem 1] (cf. also [Dye68]). This general result implies many
interesting corollaries. In particular, it implies that the amalgamated free product
F ˚u“u F of two isomorphic free groups, with isomorphism given by x ÞÑ x and u P F
being any word generating its own centralizer, i.e. not a proper power, is residually
free. Thus, many one-relator groups are residually free; and hence, by Magnus’ 1935

the Adian–Rabin Theorem [Adi55, Rab58], as every finitely presented group can be embedded
into a hopfian group [MS71]. However, Collins [Col69b] proved in 1969 that it is nevertheless
undecidable whether a given finitely presented group is hopfian. From these results, of course
proved only long after the results about to be presented, it follows that there exists an abundance
of finitely presented non-hopfian groups.

result (see §1.4.1) also residually nilpotent and residually finite. One example comes

Magnus had asked in a seminar whether all surface groups π1 pΣg q are residually
nilpotent. This question can be answered by using Baumslag’s aforementioned
theorem, by taking a commutator u “ ra1 , a2 s, which makes
                π1 pΣ2 q – xa1 , a2 , a1 , a1 | ra1 , a2 s “ ra1 , a2 sy – F2 ˚Z F2
be residually free (and hence also all π1 pΣg q for g ą 1 by the discussion following
Theorem 1.2.3). Baumslag’s results show that π1 pΣg q is residually nilpotent and
hence also hopfian, thus answering Magnus’ question positively, giving many new
hopfian one-relator groups.32 He also proves that all 2-generator subgroups of
certain one-relator groups are free ([Bau62b, Theorem 2]), comparing this result
to the Freiheitssatz. Indeed, this would be one of the first results about subgroups
of one-relator groups since the Freiheitssatz, cf. §1.7. The second contribution
from 1962 would be of a negative nature, and was joint between Baumslag and
D. Solitar (a former PhD student of Magnus’): the construction of the famous
Baumslag–Solitar groups.
       1.5.3. Baumslag–Solitar groups.
                                            These facts destroy several conjectures long and
                                            strongly held by the reviewer and others, and
                                            some published and unpublished ‘theorems’.

The hopficity of one-relator groups was, for some time, an overlooked question.
In fact, in connection with the construction of his two-relator group (20), Higman
[Hig51, p. 61] states that it can be shown, using an argument due to B. H. Neumann
and H. Neumann, that all one-relator groups are hopfian. This is, as we shall now
see, very far from the truth. In 1962, Baumslag & Solitar [BS62] would consider
very simple examples of one-relator groups:
(21)                        BSpm, nq “ xx, y | x´1 y m x “ y n y,
Their main result, appearing in a bulletin article, would be to prove that many
of the groups BSpm, nq, now called Baumslag–Solitar groups, are not hopfian (see
below for a correction of their initially incorrect statement). One of the main
examples of non-hopfian groups would be the following remarkable statement:
    Theorem 1.5.3 (Baumslag & Solitar, 1962 [BS62]). The group BSp2, 3q is not
hopfian.
    We will not present the proof of this here, but recall that by Mal’cev’s theorem
(§1.4.3) all finitely generated residually finite groups are hopfian. Thus BSp2, 3q is
not residually finite. This, at least, is easy to observe directly. Indeed, let
(22)                      B :“ BSp2, 3q “ xx, y | x´1 y 2 x “ y 3 y,
and let φ : B Ñ F be any homomorphism onto a finite group F . Let α “ φpxq, β “
                                                    n         n
φpyq. A simple inductive argument shows that x´n y 2 xn “ y 3 for all n ě 0 in

   32Baumslag mentions in a footnote [Bau62b, p. 425] that Magnus’ question was also answered,
independently and by a very different method, by K. Frederick [Fre61, Fre63], a PhD student of
Magnus’. Furthermore, G. Higman also communicated a different proof to Baumslag.

B, and hence α´n β 2 αn “ β 3 in F . In particular, if n is the order of α, then we
deduce that the order of β must divide k “ 3n ´ 2n . Since k is obviously coprime
with both 2 and 3, by Bézout’s identity there are integers m1 , m2 P Z such that
1 “ m1 k ` 2m2 , and hence β “ β 2m2 is a power of β 2 . Thus, letting β1 “ φpa´1 baq,
we have that β1 is a power of α´1 β 2 α “ β12 , and β12 “ β 3 . Thus β1 is a power of β,
and so we must have rβ1 , βs “ 1. In other words, since φ was arbitrary, the word
c “ rx´1 yx, ys must vanish in all finite homomorphic images of B. But c ‰ 1 in B,
as Magnus’ breakdown procedure shows that if c “ 1, then in the torus knot group
xy0 , y1 | y02 y13 “ 1y we have ry0 , y1 s “ 1, which is false by standard properties of
amalgamated free products (or indeed since torus knots are not unknots). Thus B
is not residually finite.
      The above argument extends to show that if gcdpm, nq “ 1, and |m|, |n| ě
2, then BSpm, nq is not residually finite. However, characterizing precisely when
BSpm, nq is residually finite is somewhat non-trivial. Indeed, in [BS62, p. 199],
it is claimed that if m or n divides the other, then BSpm, nq is residually finite.
This is not correct, as one can show e.g. that BSp2, 4q is not residually finite. The
correct characterization would only be given by Meskin in 1972, as follows:
    Theorem 1.5.4 (Meskin, 1972 [Mes72, Theorem C]). A Baumslag-Solitar
group BSpm, nq is residually finite if and only if |m| “ 1 or |n| “ 1 or |m| “ |n|.
     Many other complications arise in the literature of Baumslag–Solitar groups,
including for stating precisely when they are hopfian. Baumslag & Solitar them-
selves state that BSpm, nq is hopfian if and only if either m divides n or n divides
m, or if Πpmq “ Πpnq, where Πpkq denotes the set of prime divisors of k. This is
not correct, as e.g. BSp2, 6q can be shown to not be hopfian. The correct charac-
terization is as follows.
    Theorem 1.5.5 (Collins & Levin [CL83]). A Baumslag–Solitar group BSpm, nq
is hopfian if and only if it is residually finite, or if Πpmq “ Πpnq.
     Thus, for example, BSp12, 18q is hopfian, but it is not residually finite. Indeed,
as stated (but not proved) by Baumslag & Solitar [BS62, Theorem 2], it has a
finite index subgroup which is not hopfian (and therefore also not residually finite).
The earliest complete published proof of Theorem 1.5.5 that we are aware of only
appeared in 1992, see [ARV92, Corollary 1]. There, the authors attribute this
result to Collins & Levin [CL83], who do not give a full proof of the result, but
prove that for all |m|, |s| ‰ 1, if Πpmsq “ Πpmq, then BSpm, msq is hopfian. This
is one of the more difficult parts of proving Theorem 1.5.5, and it seems very
likely that Collins & Levin were aware of, and could have written down a proof of,
Theorem 1.5.5. The above characterization also leads to some curious results: for
example, it is not difficult to show that in BSp12, 18q “ xx, y | x´1 y 12 x “ x18 y the
group generated by xx, y 6 y is isomorphic to BSp2, 3q. Thus BSp2, 3q ď BSp12, 18q,
even though the former is non-hopfian, but the latter is hopfian because Πp12q “
Πp18q. Thus the Baumslag–Solitar groups also serve to illustrate just how elusive
a property hopficity can be from a group-theoretic point of view.33
     Baumslag–Solitar groups have come to play a central role in the theory of one-
relator groups since their introduction (indeed, recall that some examples of them

   33Even understanding when direct products of hopfian groups is hopfian is rather complicated,
see e.g. [Cor65, Hir69].

appeared already in Magnus’ 1930 article, §1.3.1). They act as counterexamples,
as we have seen, to many natural conjectures about one-relator groups, and have a
rich structural theory. We now give an overview of some of this structure to finish
this section. We first remark that the groups BSp1, mq, with m P Z, which above
are distinguished as residually finite as well as hopfian, are in fact solvable. Indeed,
                                             1
the derived subgroup is isomorphic to Zr m     s, and the groups split as a semidirect
            1
product Zr m s ¸ Z, with obvious conjugating action. Indeed, they are linear, arising
as subgroups of GL2 pRq, via the representation
                           ˆ 1            ˙              ˆ      ˙
                            m2        0                    1 1
(23)                  x ÞÑ              1     and y ÞÑ
                              0     m´ 2                   0 1

In fact, a Baumslag–Solitar group is linear if and only if it is residually finite. One
direction is Mal’cev’s famous theorem, and the other is obtained by constructing
linear representations for all groups in Theorem 1.5.4. Thus, in view of the linear
representation (23), it suffices to show that BSpm, mq is linear for all m P Z. How-
ever, it is not hard to prove that BSpm, mq has a finite index subgroup isomorphic
to a direct product Fn ˆ Z, where Fn is a free group of rank n. Since such direct
products are obviously linear, so too is BSpm, mq.
     The results by Baumslag & Solitar were quickly picked up by Rapaport, who
in 1964 used them to prove certain results on free products of groups. In a rather
obscure article [Rap64a], she proves among other results two which are relevant to
our story: first, the minimal number of defining relations of a free product of two
finitely presented groups may be less than the sum of that of the two factors (later
rediscovered by Hog, Lustig & Metzler [HLM85]). Second, she proved a result
on the deficiency of a one-relator group. Here, the deficiency of an n-generator k-
relator presentation is defined to be n ´ k, and the deficiency of a finitely presented
group is the maximum, taken over all presentations of the group, such deficiency.
Thus the deficiency of an n-generator one-relator group is at least n ´ 1, and
Rapaport proved that it is no larger:
    Theorem 1.5.6 (Rapaport, 1964 [Rap64a, Corollary 2]). Let G “ xA | r “ 1y
be a one-relator group. Then G has deficiency |A| ´ 1.
     Baumslag–Solitar groups continue to be a rich area of research, and although
we cannot possibly mention all results regarding them, we will mention a few which
are naturally connected to hopficity and the above considerations. First, the en-
domorphisms of BSpm, nq with gcdpm, nq “ 1 and m, n ‰ ˘1 were described by
Anshel [Ans71, Ans72, Ans73] (see [Ans76c] for corrections). Using this, he
proceeded to prove that in this case, G “ BSpm, nq has a fully invariant subgroup
N such that G{N – G, a very strong form of non-hopficity. We mention also
that Hirshon [Hir75] has given a full description of the intersection of the sub-
groups of finite index in Baumslag–Solitar groups. Finally, regarding the quotients
of Baumslag–Solitar groups, Moldavanskii & Sibyakova [MS95] proved (with clas-
sical techniques) that the solvable Baumslag–Solitar groups BSp1, kq are profinitely
rigid among residually finite one-relator groups, i.e. for any residually finite one-
relator group G, the set of finite quotients of G is the same as that of BSp1, kq if
and only if G – BSp1, kq, cf. also [BS89]. We remark finally that a natural line
of investigation beyond Baumslag–Solitar groups comes from considering relations
of the type x´1 wm x “ wn , where w is some arbitrary word (possibly with more
                1.5. THE RESURGENCE OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS (1960S)                            43


generators). Hopficity in this case has been investigated e.g. by Collins [Col78b],
Rosenberger [Ros80], and Collins & Levin [CL83].
     1.5.4. The Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy. One of Magnus’ most promi-
nent students was M. Greendlinger (b. 1932), who completed his PhD in 1960.34
The subject matter of his thesis [Gre60b] was extending Dehn’s algorithm from sur-
face groups π1 pΣg q with g ą 1 (see §1.2.2) to broader classes. This class of groups,
which lie somewhat outside the scope of this survey, is now called small cancella-
tion groups, as they satisfy the “small cancellation condition” C 1 p1{6q (historically,
groups satisfying this condition were also called sixth groups). This condition, which
traces back to the 1940s in work by Tartakovskii [Tar49] (and independently Brit-
ton [Bri56, Bri57]), is intuitively speaking a condition on how much the relators
of a given group presentation overlap. It can be used to guarantee a solution to
the word problem and the conjugacy problem, using a well-known result known
as Greendlinger’s lemma, which gives rise to a similar algorithm to Dehn’s in the
setting of π1 pΣg q. Greendlinger would expand on his work in a series of articles
throughout the 1960s [Gre60c, Gre60d, Gri], which would be generalized by Lyn-
don [Lyn66, Lyn72] and his student Schupp [Sch68, Sch70]. Today, a classical
reference for small cancellation theory is [LS77, Chapter V].
     Greendlinger himself did not work directly with one-relator groups, even though
his results were all directly inspired by this subject. After he moved from New
York to the USSR (forgoing an NSF Grant for working with B. H. Neumann in
Manchester), Greendlinger would end up as one of the core members of the algebraic
school of mathematics in Ivanovo State University, and then in Tula, where he began
working in 1967 [BDTU17]. He would bring many of the results and methods
from Magnus to the USSR, and many of Greendlinger’s students would end up
proving significant results on one-relator groups. One of his students in Ivanovo
was D. I. Moldavanskii, who became a graduate student of Greendlinger’s in 1964,
but ”almost immediately declared that small cancellation theory did not appeal
to him” [Aza16, p. 4]. Greendlinger, very familiar with, and having access to,
Western literature on the subject, gave Moldavanskii a copy of Baumslag’s 1964
survey [Bau64] on one-relator groups, which ends with a question on the abelian
subgroups of one-relator groups. Moldavanskii began working on this question,
which would turn out to be very fortuitous for the development of one-relator
groups. His approach to the subject would pass (more or less explicitly) via HNN-
extensions, and would lead to a simplification of the Magnus hierarchy. This, in
turn, leads to simplifications of the classical results, and for deeper results to be
proved, heralding in a new era of one-relator group theory.
     Before presenting Moldavanskii’s method, we give a brief summary of HNN-
extensions. Such extensions were first introduced by G. Higman, B. H. Neumann,
and H. Neumann [HNN49] in 1949, and carries their initials. The main idea of
the extensions is that if G is a group with two isomorphic subgroups H1 , H2 ď G,
then such an isomorphism can be extended to an inner automorphism of a larger

  34Martin Greendlinger was born in New York in 1932. In 1957, he met Elena Ivanovna in
Moscow while visiting for a conference, and they married the next year. Because Elena was un-
able to get a US citizenship, Martin moved to the USSR in 1960, renounced his US citizenship,
and became a Soviet citizen in 1961, eventually settling in Tula. Thus Magnus’ school of combi-
natorial group theory also made its way to the USSR. For more details, see [VD22]. Occasionally,
Greendlinger’s name is incorrectly transliterated (via Russian) in English as Grindlinger.

group G˚ into which G embeds. Concretely, if G “ xA | Ry, and φ : H1 Ñ H2 is an
isomorphism between two subgroups of G, then the HNN-extension G˚φ of G with
associated subgroups H1 and H2 is the group with the presentation
(24)                   G˚φ “ xA, t | R, th1 t´1 “ φph2 q p@h1 P H1 qy
where t is some new symbol, called the stable letter. A basic result proved in
[HNN49] is that the natural map G Ñ G˚φ is injective, and one original motiva-
tion for introducing these extensions was to prove that every countable group G is
isomorphic to a subgroup of a two-generator group H. However, 10 years after its
introduction, the construction would find its key use by J. L. Britton (a student of
B. H. Neumann’s) in his proof of the undecidability of the word problem for groups.
    Let G˚φ be the group in (24), and let w be a word written as
(25)                                 u0 tε1 u1 tε2 ¨ ¨ ¨ tεn un
where ui P FA and εi “ ˘1 for all 0 ď i ď n. Suppose for simplicity of formulation
that n ą 0. Then Britton realized that deciding that the word problem in G˚φ can
be formulated in terms of H1 , H2 , and G in the following way:
Lemma 1.5.7 (Britton35 [Bri57, Lemma 4]). If w “ 1 in G˚φ , then w contains a
subword of the form th1 t´1 or t´1 h2 t, where h1 P H1 and h2 P H2 .
      Thus, via this “spelling theorem”, one can immediately get a solution to the
word problem as soon as one can solve membership in H1 , H2 P G. A stronger form
of Britton’s Lemma, called Theorem A by Britton [Bri63], has a direct proof via
normal forms in amalgamated free products, see [Mil68]. A direct consequence of
Britton’s Lemma is, of course, the aforementioned result that the map G Ñ G˚φ is
injective. But, more generally speaking, the lemma allows encodings into groups
of “combinatorial sequences”, particularly those associated to the instructions of
a (universal) Turing machine. In finitely presented semi groups, such encodings
are rather straightforward (cf. §1.4.4), but for groups the first known encodings,
by Boone [Boo57] and Novikov [Nov55], were messy and full of combinatorial
intricacies. The simplicity of Britton’s formulation permitted the explosion of new
undecidability results in the 1950s (again see §1.4.4).
      This simplicity of formulation would also turn out to be applicable to one-
relator group theory. In 1967, Moldavanskii [Mol67] would be the first to notice
this, and used some of the properties of HNN-extensions in the study of the Magnus
hierarchy. First, we remark that if G “ xA | r “ 1y is a one-relator group in which
some generator t, actually appearing in r, has exponent sum zero in r, then it
is not hard to see that G is an HNN-extension of a one-relator group G1 , where
the associated subgroups are Magnus subgroups of G1 and t is the stable letter
(for a proof see [MS73, Theorem 1]). Indeed, continuing the example (10) and
(11), the stable letter is b, the first associated subgroup is xa0 , c0 y, the second is
xa1 , c1 y, and the isomorphism is induced by a0 ÞÑ a1 , c0 ÞÑ c1 . Furthermore, by
using Nielsen transformations and Euclidean division in the obvious way, we can
always assume that at least one generator of G has exponent sum zero in r and
appears in r. Let us call this the Euclidean trick. For example, in the torus knot
group xa, b | a2 “ b3 y, we can apply the Nielsen transformation a ÞÑ ba and fixing b,
   35HNN-extensions, and an essentially equivalent form of Britton’s Lemma, also appear inde-
pendently in Novikov’s proof of the undecidability of the word problem in groups, where they play
a key role [Nov52, Nov55].
                1.5. THE RESURGENCE OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS (1960S)                            45


followed by the Nielsen transformation fixing a and mapping b ÞÑ a2 b, which results
in the isomorphism
                     T2,3 “ xa, b | a2 “ b3 y – xa, b | ababa´2 b “ 1y.
In this new presentation, a has exponent sum zero. Hence, our group is an HNN-
extension with stable letter a, and applying the Magnus method (from §1.3.1) we
see that it is an HNN-extension of xb0 , b1 , b2 | b1 b2 b0 “ 1y, a free group.
     The Euclidean trick is sufficient for proving certain results about one-relator
groups, and Moldavanskii uses it e.g. to give a necessary and sufficient criterion
for a one-relator group to be (f.g. free)-by-cyclic (see §1.5.5 below). However, in
general the Euclidean trick will not lend itself easily to inductive arguments, since
it can increase the length of the relator.36 Instead, we need another trick. Suppose
(for simplicity) that G1 “ xa, b | rpa, bq “ 1y, where the exponent sums of both a
and b are non-zero, say α resp. β. Then we can map G1 into the group
                    G11 “ xy, x, b | rpy, bq “ 1, b “ xα y – G1 ˚b“xα Z
where the exponent sum of x is zero. When rewriting the relator via the Magnus
process, the relator length does indeed decrease compared to that of G1 , allowing
the use of induction. Let us call this the Magnus trick. Using this trick, we can
then prove the following:
    Theorem 1.5.8 (Moldavanskii, 1967 [Mol67]). Every one-relator group G1 “
xA1 | r1 “ 1y can be embedded in a one-relator group G11 which is the HNN-extension
of a one-relator group G2 “ xA2 | r2 “ 1y, in which the associated subgroups are
Magnus subgroups and |r2 | ă |r1 |.
     The finite hierarchy of one-relator groups G1 , G2 , . . . , Gn that this gives rise
to is called the Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy. Notice that the groups of this
hierarchy are the same as the original Magnus hierarchy, with the only conceptual
difference being the addition of the HNN-extensions. Furthermore, Theorem 1.5.8
as stated does not appear in Moldavanskii’s article, but is implicit in his reasoning
(e.g. [Mol67, p. 1379]). The main result of Moldavanskii’s article is the following:
     Theorem 1.5.9 (Moldavanskii, 1967 [Mol67]). Any abelian subgroup of a one-
relator group is cyclic, free abelian of rank 2, or locally cyclic.
    This almost resolved Baumslag’s 1964 question of the abelian subgroups of one-
relator groups. However, it leaves unanswered the question of which locally cyclic
groups can appear as subgroups of a one-relator group, and in particular whether
pQ, `q can appear as a subgroup of some one-relator group. This has a negative
answer, and we shall see its full resolution, given by B. B. Newman in 1968, later
in §1.7.3.
    Moldavanskii continued to use this new hierarchy, and other tools, to prove
more results about one-relator groups and combinatorial group theory in general.
For example, in 1969 he gave an algorithm for deciding whether two subgroups of
a free group are conjugate [Mol69b] (cf. also [Gre70]), and an extension of the
Nielsen method to free products of groups, resulting in a new proof of Grushko’s
theorem [Mol69c] (for more on extensions of Nielsen’s methods in the context of

   36Moldavanskii seemingly missed this fact at first, since he only in a footnote [Mol67, Proof
of Theorem 3] remarks that the Magnus trick is needed to make a proof of his correct.
46                                        1. HISTORY


one-relator groups, see §1.6.3). The same year, he also gave a complete proof of the
following claim made by Magnus [Mag32, p. 297] in 1932:
    Theorem 1.5.10 (Magnus / Moldavanskii [Mol69a]). Except for Z and BSp1, kq
for k P Z, every one-relator group contains a free group of rank 2
    Note that the class of exceptional groups in Theorem 1.5.10 has a number of
other characterizations, and appears as a frequent class of “easy” one-relator groups.
They are, among other things precisely the amenable one-relator groups, and as
proved by Gildenhuys [Gil79], the groups in Theorem 1.5.10 also form precisely
the class of solvable groups of cohomological dimension ď 2. We shall see some
extensions by other students of Greendlinger of Theorem 1.5.10 in §1.7.
    For a final note on the Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy, we note that a modern
and simple proof of its validity was given by McCool & Schupp [MS73], which
continues to be an excellent resource for understanding the elements of one-relator
group theory. Indeed, therein they use the well-developed machinery of HNN-
extensions to give simple proofs of the Freiheitssatz (§1.3.1), the decidability of the
word problem (§1.3.2), and the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem (which we will
encounter later in §1.6.1).
    1.5.5. Free-by-cyclic one-relator groups. One important class of one-relator
groups are those which are free-by-cyclic. Recall that a group G is said to be free-
by-cyclic if there exists a short exact sequence
(26)                            1 ÝÑ F ÝÑ G ÝÑ C ÝÑ 1,
where F is a free group and C is cyclic. We do not a priori assume that F is
finitely generated, nor non-trivial; similarly, we do not require C to be non-trivial.
However, in essentially all cases we will encounter in this brief section F will be
finitely generated, and C will be infinite cyclic. Note that in the case that C “ Z,
G splits as a semidirect product G “ F ¸φ Z, where Z acts by F via conjugation
by some automorphism φ P AutpF q.
      Let now G be a one-relator group. In 1967, Moldavanskii [Mol67] proved that
if ϕ : G Ñ Z is any surjective homomorphism, then it is possible to find a generating
set a11 , a12 , . . . , a1n of G, with a defining relator r1 “ 1, such that kerpϕq is precisely
the normal closure of a12 , . . . , a1n . In particular, a11 has exponent sum zero in r1 .
Using this result, he proved that [Mol67, Corollary 2] the commutator subgroup
of G is finitely generated if and only if the following holds: n “ 2, and the normal
closure of a12 is a finitely generated free group. Combining his results, we obtain
the following characterization of precisely when this happens:
    Theorem 1.5.11 (Moldavanskii, 1967 [Mol67]). Let G “ xa, b | r “ 1y be a
non-abelian37 one-relator group with the exponent sum of a in r equal to zero. Let
bm resp. bM be such that m resp. M is the smallest resp. largest index occurring
when rewriting r over bi “ ai ba´i . Then the following are equivalent:
     (1) G is (finitely generated free)-by-cyclic;
     (2) The commutator subgroup G1 is finitely generated;
     (3) m ă M , and am resp. aM occur exactly once in the rewritten form of r.


   37In the statement of [Mol67, Corollary 2], this assumption is inadvertently omitted, but it is
there in the proof, cf. the statement of [Mol67, Theorem 1].
                1.6. ONE-RELATOR GROUPS WITH TORSION (1960–1980)                          47


Remark 1.5.12. The equivalence (1) ðñ (3) is often called “Brown’s criterion”
for a one-relator group to be (finitely generated free)-by-cyclic, as it was indepen-
dently rediscovered in 1987 by Brown [Bro87, §4].
    Now, (finitely generated free)-by-cyclic groups are residually finite, as proved
by Mal’cev [Mal58, Theorem 1, p. 50] in 1958, see also [Bau71a] (also extended by
Wong [Won87]). Thus one consequence of Moldavanskii’s results is the following:
Corollary 1.5.13 ([Mol67, Corollary 1]). If the commutator subgroup of a one-
relator group G is finitely generated, then G is residually finite.
     Given a one-relator group G and a surjective homomorphism ϕ : G Ñ Z, Molda-
vanskii also notes that his criterion gives an effective procedure for deciding whether
or not kerpϕq is finitely generated. In particular, one corollary of his methods is the
following.
Corollary 1.5.14 (Moldavanskii, 1967 [Mol67, p. 1375]). There is an algorithm
for deciding if a one-relator group is (finitely generated free)-by-cyclic.
     Residual finiteness is, in general, a significantly weaker property than being
free-by-cyclic, even in the class of one-relator groups. It remains an open problem
whether residual finiteness is decidable for one-relator groups.

             1.6. One-relator groups with torsion (1960–1980)


                              It seems to be commonly believed that the presence of
                              elements of finite order in a group with a single defining
                              relation is a complicating rather than a simplifying factor.
                              This note is in support of the opposite point of view.

                                                         —G. Baumslag, 1967 [Bau67c]

                              [...] apart from the complications introduced by having
                              elements of finite order, one-relator groups with torsion
                              behave much like free groups.

                                                            —S. J. Pride, 1977 [Pri77b]
This far into the story, none of the theorems about one-relator groups have singled
out one-relator groups with torsion as forming a particularly simple class compared
to their torsion-free counterparts. Indeed, sometimes the opposite seems true: for
example, Lyndon’s Identity Theorem (Theorem 1.4.4, §1.4.2) is significantly neater
to state in the torsion-free case. Nevertheless, in the 1960s and 1970s a number of
remarkable results would be proved for one-relator groups with torsion which, in full
accordance with the above quotations. The crowning achievement of such theorems
was the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem, which in modern terminology states that
one-relator groups with torsion are hyperbolic. Before stating this result, we state
a number of important conjectures made by G. Baumslag around this time:
Conjecture 1.6.1 (Baumslag’s Conjectures). Let G “ xA | rn “ 1y, where n ą 1,
be a one-relator group with torsion. Then the following hold:
     (1) G is virtually free-by-cyclic [Bau86, Problem 6]
     (2) G is coherent [Bau86, Problem 1]
48                                   1. HISTORY


     (3) G is residually finite [Bau67c, Conjecture A],
     (4) G is hopfian [Bau67c, Conjecture A5],

     We make some remarks on these conjectures. First, even for arbitrary groups
G, (1) is the strongest of these properties, and implies all others: (1) ùñ (3),
and (3) ùñ (4) are both classical results. The implication (1) ùñ (2) is
due to Feighn & Handel [FH99] in 1999. Since BSp2, 3q is non-hopfian, it follows
that the only one of the four conjectures that can hold in the torsion-free case
is (2). In fact, that is precisely the conjecture that Baumslag made in [Bau86,
Problem 1]: all one-relator groups are coherent. We note that already in 1974,
Baumslag [Bau71b] had stated that it remained an open problem whether all one-
relator groups are coherent. The above conjectures would be driving forces behind
many of the developments of one-relator group theory in the 1970s until the present
day. Today, we know that they all have affirmative answers, including (2) in the
general case. In this section, we will present some of the partial progress made on
these conjectures before their full resolution, and present some general theorems
indicating why one-relator groups with torsion are significantly simpler than their
torsion-free counterparts.

     1.6.1. The B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem.
                                     I don’t believe it! I don’t believe it!

                                     —W. Magnus in 1968, upon seeing B. B. Newman
                                       present his Spelling Theorem [NB21a, p. 119]

                                            He could not believe that an unheard-of
                                            mathematician, from some unknown
                                            university in outback Australia, could have
                                            come up with these results.

                                                —B. B. Newman, remarking on Magnus’
                                                       reaction above [NB21a, p. 120]
Spelling theorems, being theorems which provide information on the spelling of
words equal to 1 in a group, date back to the very beginning of one-relator group
theory, the prime example being Dehn’s Spelling Theorem (Theorem 1.2.2) for the
fundamental groups of 2-surfaces. Britton’s Lemma (see Lemma 1.5.7), with its
importance for the Magnus–Moldavanskii, is another. And in 1968, B. B. Newman,
a PhD student of Baumslag’s, provided perhaps the most important one. We will
be brief on the biographical details of this story, since this is expanded on at length
by the author in [NB21a]. We mention only that the starting point for Newman’s
work on the subject was Baumslag giving him a draft of the chapter on one-relator
groups from the now famous book by Magnus, Karrass & Solitar [MKS66]. After
some work, he was able to prove the following remarkable theorem:

    Theorem 1.6.2 (The B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem, 1968). Let G “ xA |
rn “ 1y be a one-relator group with torsion, with n ą 1, and r cyclically reduced
and not a proper power. Let w P FA be a non-trivial word such that w “ 1 in G.
Then w contains a subword u such that either u or u´1 is a subword of rn , and
such that the length of u is strictly more than n´1                     n
                                                 n times the length of r .
                1.6. ONE-RELATOR GROUPS WITH TORSION (1960–1980)                        49


    The proof of this theorem goes, as expected, by the Magnus hierarchy. However,
the 1968 article in which the theorem was first announced was only a bulletin article
[New68a], and no proofs appeared therein. The proof only appeared in Newman’s
PhD thesis [New68b], which was until recently completely inaccessible: indeed,
the author’s (eventually successful) hunt for this thesis is the subject of [NB21a].
A simpler proof of the Spelling Theorem was given by McCool & Schupp [MS73]
in 1973, using the full power of HNN-extensions (see §1.5.4).
    We now give some immediate consequences of the Spelling Theorem, all of
which appear in B. B. Newman’s thesis. First, and most obviously, it gives a
linear-time solution to the word problem in all one-relator groups with torsion.
This is significantly better than the time-complexity of Magnus’ solution to the
word problem in general one-relator groups (see §1.3.3). Next, Newman realized
that his Spelling Theorem gave an easy solution also to the conjugacy problem:
    Theorem 1.6.3 (B. B. Newman, 1968 [New68b, Theorem 3.2.3]). The conju-
gacy problem is decidable in any one-relator group with torsion.
    Newman also solved (see [New68b, Theorem 3.3.1]) the power problem in one-
relator groups with torsion, i.e. the problem of deciding whether a given element in
a group is a proper power or not. This problem, which we will not discuss further,
appeared in a similar setting of small cancellation groups also in work by Lipschutz
[Lip65, Lip68] and Reinhart [Rei62]. The next corollary of the Spelling Theorem
was a stronger form of the Freiheitssatz.
      Theorem 1.6.4 (B. B. Newman, 1968 [New68b, Corollary 2.1.6]). Let G “
xa1 , a2 , . . . , ak | rn “ 1y with n ą 1 be a one-relator group with torsion, where r is
cyclically reduced involving the letters a1 , a2 P A non-trivially. Let β be any integer
which does not divide the exponent sum of a1 in rn . Then taβ1 , a2 , . . . , ak u freely
generates a free subgroup of G.
     Thus, for example, in xa, b | pa2 b3 q2 “ 1y, the subgroup generated by ta3 , bu is
free on that basis. Note that the ordinary Freiheitssatz only gives information about
cyclic subgroups for this example. Thus Theorem 1.6.4 gives an abundance of free
subgroups of one-relator groups with torsion. The same year, Ree & Mendelsohn
[RM68] had proved a weaker version of this result, for the two-generator case, by
using representations for some one-relator groups with torsion in SLp2, Cq. Magnus
[Mag75], however, has shown that their technique cannot yield the full strength of
Theorem 1.6.4. As a final application of the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem, we
mention the following subgroup result.
    Theorem 1.6.5 (Newman, 1968 [New68b, Corollary 2.3.4]). The centralizer
of every non-trivial element of a one-relator group with torsion is cyclic.
     This theorem was also proved and extended by Karrass & Solitar [KS71a,
p. 956], who elaborated on the properties of free products with a malnormal amal-
gamated subgroup. B. B. Newman had made central use of some of the properties
of such amalgams, particularly using the malnormality of Magnus subgroups in one-
relator groups to prove his results. This property of Magnus subgroups remains also
a key theme in the modern setting. We elaborate on this theme in §1.7.1.
     These were only some the initial applications of the B. B. Newman Spelling
Theorem, all of which appeared already in his thesis. Newman also used it to study
abelian and solvable subgroups of one-relator groups with torsion; we detail this
50                                    1. HISTORY


further in §1.7.3. Furthermore, it would come to play a particularly prominent role
in the subsequent work by S. J. Pride on one-relator groups with torsion, which
we will detail more in §1.6.3. We mention one of his results here, as its proof
heavily relies on the Spelling Theorem. B. B. Newman and Pride once thought
that every finitely generated subgroup of a one-relator group would either be free
or have torsion [Pri77b, p. 483]. That this is not true can be seen either from
Theorem 1.6.8 together with the fact that not every one-relator group with torsion
is free, or else from the example (27) below. Nevertheless, much can be said of the
two-generator subgroups of one-relator groups with torsion:
     Theorem 1.6.6 (Pride, 1977 [Pri77b]). Every two-generator subgroup of a
one-relator group with torsion is either a free product of cyclic groups, or a one-
relator group with torsion.
    Further results on subgroups of one-relator groups is the subject of §1.7. We
now conclude this present section with some brief remarks on the Spelling Theorem.
First, a strengthening of the theorem was obtained by Gurevich [Gur72, Gur73],
see also Schupp [Sch76a] for a complete proof and further strengthening. As noted
