by Pride [Pri74, p. 57], a proof of this stronger result can be obtained by the
same method as the proof of Newman’s result given by McCool & Schupp [MS73].
Furthermore, one of the consequences, indeed one of the reformulations, of the
Spelling Theorem is that one-relator groups with torsion admit Dehn presentations.
This is known to be equivalent to Gromov hyperbolicity, and hence all one-relator
groups with torsion are hyperbolic. This places many of the above theorems in their
proper context: for example, in any hyperbolic group the centralizer is virtually
cyclic, yielding in particular Theorem 1.6.5 for one-relator groups with torsion.
However, the proof of Theorem 1.6.5 predates the definition of hyperbolic groups
by two decades.

     1.6.2. Residual and virtual properties. As mentioned before, today we
know that all of Baumslag’s conjectures (Conjecture 1.6.1) are true. However,
there had been some partial progress on these before this resolution, including for
residual finiteness. We now present some of these results. We remark first that a
natural conjecture might be that all one-relator groups with torsion are residually
(torsion-free) nilpotent. But this is not so, as indicated by the following example
due to B. B. Newman [New68b, p. 72]: let
(27)                        G “ xa, b | pa´1 b´1 ab2 q2 “ 1y
Then the index 2 subgroup of G generated by xa, b2 , bab´1 y admits the presentation
xx, y, z | ry, zsry, xs “ yy, and clearly y lies in the intersection of every term in the
lower central series. Hence G is not residually torsion-free nilpotent. One of the
first theorems on residual properties of one-relator groups with torsion was also due
to B. B. Newman, in his thesis:
   Theorem 1.6.7 ([New68b, Corollary 2.1.7]). Let G “ xa1 , a2 , . . . , ak | rn “ 1y
where n ą 1. Then G is residually a two-generator one-relator group with torsion.
     The proof is not difficult, but uses the Spelling Theorem. A few years later,
Fischer, Karrass & Solitar [FKS72] wrote a short but influential note giving struc-
tural information about one-relator groups with torsion. Two of their results are
as follows:
                1.6. ONE-RELATOR GROUPS WITH TORSION (1960–1980)                     51


    Theorem 1.6.8 (Fischer, Karrass & Solitar, 1972 [FKS72]). Let G “ xA |
rn “ 1y with n ą 1 be a one-relator group with torsion. Then: (1) G is virtually
torsion-free; and (2) G is virtually free if and only if r is a primitive word in FA .
     The authors also prove that the elements of finite order in G generate a sub-
group that is the free product of conjugates of the cyclic group generated by r, a
result that appears implicitly already in work of Cohen & Lyndon [CL63]. Part
(2) of Theorem 1.6.8 was reproved, among other results, by Karrass, Solitar &
Pietrowski [KPS73] the next year. Part (1) of Theorem 1.6.8 was expanded on
by Fischer [Fis77], who proved that (with the same notation as in Theorem 1.6.8)
if H is a subgroup of G with rG : Hs “ k ă 8 and H is torsion-free, then n
divides k. Furthermore, if some generator in r has exponent sum s ‰ 0, then G
has a torsion-free |s|-relator subgroup of finite index; if further gcdps, nq “ 1 then
G has a one-relator subgroup H of finite index. As a corollary, he deduced that
if G contains a torsion-free subgroup H with rG : Hs “ kn, then H is a k-relator
group. Finally, while we cannot pass into the details of ends of groups here, instead
referring the reader to [Sta68], we note that another important result proved in
[FKS72] is that any freely indecomposable one-relator group with torsion has at
most one end. Thus, in this case, it follows from Theorem 1.6.8 that if the defining
word is non-trivial and not a power of a primitive word, then the group has exactly
one end.
     We now list some families of one-relator groups with torsion which were known
to be residually finite before the full resolution of Baumslag’s conjecture:
      (1) (Fischer, 1977 [Fis77]) The groups xA Y B | puvqn “ 1y, n ą 1, where
          u P FA and v P FB are non-trivial elements, and A X B “ ∅.
      (2) (Fischer, 1977 [Fis77]) The groups xa, b | pabk a´1 y r qn “ 1y, where n ą 1,
          n does not divide r or k, and gcdpr ` k, nq “ 1.
      (3) (B. Baumslag & Tretkoff, 1978 [BT78]) The groups xa, b | pa´1 bl abl qn “
          1y where n ą 1 and l P Z.
      (4) (B. Baumslag & Levin, 1979 [BL79]) The groups xA, x | pxux´1 vqn “ 1y
          where n ą 1 and u, v P FA and either (i) u, v are words over disjoint sets
          of generators; or (ii) u and v are non-trivial powers of a single element,
          e.g. xx, y | pxy k x´1 y l qn “ 1y with k, l P Z.
      (5) (Allenby & Tang, 1981 [AT81a, AT81b]) Many different sporadic classes
          of one-relator groups with torsion; a typical example is [AT81a, Theo-
          rem 4.4], which states that xa, b | praα , bβ sra´γ , b´δ sqt “ 1y, n ą 1 and
          α, β, γ, δ P N.
     After these sporadic results, Egorov [Ego81] (a student of Moldavanskii) proved
the following remarkable result on positive one-relator groups with torsion:
    Theorem 1.6.9 (Egorov, 1981 [Ego81]). Let G “ xA | rn “ 1y with n ą 1
and r a positive word in FA , i.e. including no inverse of any generator. Then G is
residually finite.
    For extensions of this result, we refer the reader to Wise [Wis01]; for compar-
isons with positive torsion-free one-relator groups, see §1.7.5. Finally, we mention
a connection with another “residual-like” property. Tang [Tan82] proved that the
groups xa, b | pam bn qk “ 1y where k ą 1 are all conjugacy separable, a stronger prop-
erty than residual finiteness. Recall that a group G is conjugacy separable if for
any g, h P G which are not conjugate, there exists some finite quotient Q of G such
52                                           1. HISTORY


that the images of g and h are not conjugate in Q. Thus conjugacy separability is
to the conjugacy problem what residual finiteness is to the word problem. Further
results on conjugacy separability in some classes of one-relator groups with torsion
were obtained by Allenby & Tang [AT86] a few years later, see also [KMT95].
Today, like residual finiteness, we know that one-relator groups with torsion are
conjugacy separable, a result due to Minasyan & Zalesski [MZ13].

     1.6.3. The isomorphism problem for one-relator groups with torsion.
By deep results of Dahmani & Guirardel [DG11], we know today that the iso-
morphism problem for all hyperbolic groups is decidable. As mentioned in §1.6.1
(Theorem 1.6.2), all one-relator groups with torsion are hyperbolic. Hence, the
isomorphism problem is decidable for one-relator groups with torsion. However,
this problem was by no means a mystery before this point, and it was known to be
decidable in a large number of cases. Thus, we will now go through some of this
earlier literature on the subject, focussing particularly on the remarkable results by
Pride.
     First, in line with asking about isomorphisms of one-relator groups, it is natural
to ask whether there is a converse to the isomorphism result from Theorem 1.5.2.
Such a converse would show that the isomorphism problem for one-relator groups
would be somewhat more tractable, since it would show that it would be sufficient
to solve it in the torsion case. However, B. B. Newman suggested to Pride that a
counterexample to this converse might exist [Pri74, p. 71], and Pride was able to
construct such a counterexample:
    Theorem 1.6.10 (Pride, 1974 [Pri74, Theorem 4.7]). Let n ě 1 and consider
the groups
        Bn “ xa, t | pa´3 t´1 a2 tqn “ 1y         and     Cn “ xa, t | pa´1 rt, as2 qn “ 1y.
Then Bn – Cn if and only if n “ 1.
    Thus, this theorem provided a counterexample to the converse of Theorem 1.5.2.
Indeed, it also provided another counterexample to an old conjecture by Magnus
(Conjecture 1.3.5), since it proves that there is an isomorphism
                   B1 “ xa, t | t´1 a2 t “ a3 y – xa, t | rt, as2 “ ay “ C1
but it is readily checked that t´1 a2 ta´3 and rt, as2 a´1 are not related by a free group
automorphism (this example was also obtained by Brunner [Bru74], cf. [Bru76]).
     The above negative result is, in hindsight, not surprising: the isomorphism
problem for one-relator groups with torsion turns out to be significantly easier than
in the torsion-free case. To state some of the positive results, we recall the notion of
a T -system (Ger. Transitivitätssystem, i.e. transitivity system) of a group, following
[Pri75]. Let G be an n-generated group, and let
                        g “ pg1 , . . . , gn q,   and g1 “ pg11 , . . . , gn1 q
be two generating tuples for G. We say that g and g1 are Nielsen equivalent if there
is some automorphism
                           xi ÞÑ Yi px1 , . . . , xn q,   i “ 1, 2, . . . , n
of the free group Fn on x1 , . . . , xn such that gi1 “ Yi pg1 , . . . , gn q. Thus, for example,
the tuples pg1 , g2 q and pg2 g1´1 , g1 q are always Nielsen equivalent. We say that g and
                1.6. ONE-RELATOR GROUPS WITH TORSION (1960–1980)                             53


g1 lie in the same T -system if there is some automorphism ϱ P AutpGq such that g1
is Nielsen equivalent to ϱpgq, where
                                  ϱpgq “ pϱpg1 q, . . . , ϱpgn qq.
This equivalence relation was defined by B. H. Neumann & H. Neumann [NN51] in
1951. For our purposes, the importance of T -systems comes via Magnus’ Conjugacy
Theorem (Theorem 1.3.3). First, continuing the above notation, let
                             xx1 , . . . , xn | r1 “ 1, . . . , rm “ 1y
is a presentation of G with respect to the generating tuple g (i.e. the kernel of the
map xi ÞÑ gi coincides with the normal closure of the rj ). Then g1 lies in the same
T -system as g if and only if there is some ϕ P AutpFn q such that
                         xx1 , . . . , xn | ϕpr1 q “ 1, . . . , ϕprm q “ 1y
is a presentation of G with respect to the generating tuple g1 . In general, G may
have many different m-relator presentations with respect to g1 , and one of the
difficulties (indeed undecidability) of the isomorphism problem in full generality
arises from this issue. However, when m “ 1, i.e. the one-relator case, Magnus’
Conjugacy Theorem (Theorem 1.3.3) tells us that two generating sets lying in the
same T -system gives very strict control over the defining relator:
Proposition 1.6.11. Two one-relator presentations xA | r “ 1y and xA | s “ 1y
of a group G are associated with elements from the same T -system in G if and only
if s is equal in FA to the image of r˘1 under some automorphism of FA .
    In particular, if a one-relator group G only has one T -system of generating
tuples, then the isomorphism problem is decidable for the class of one-relator groups
with respect to that one-relator group; in other words, it is decidable whether a
given one-relator group is isomorphic to G or not. Brunner’s example of BSp2, 3q
above shows that in general a one-relator group may have infinitely many T -systems
of generating tuples. In the case of one-relator groups with torsion, however, more
can be said.
    First, for example, consider the class of groups Sn “ xa, b | ra, bsn “ 1y, where
n ą 1. These groups Sn are virtually surface groups π1 pΣg q, indeed it is not hard
to show that kerpSn ↠ D2n q is isomorphic to π1 pΣ2n´1 q (see e.g. [BT08]). The
fact that Sn is Fuchsian was observed also by Rosenberger [Ros73], who studied
these groups in 1973. He furthermore proved that the only generating sets tu, vu of
Sn are those of the form where ru, vs is conjugate to ra, bsε , where ε “ ˘1 (see also
Purzitsky & Rosenberger [PR72, PR73] and Pride [Pri73], cf. [Pri74, p. 72]).
By Nielsen’s result (Proposition 1.2.4), this implies that tu, vu is a basis of the
underlying free group, so in particular Sn has only one T -system of generators. In
1972, Rosenberger [Ros72] also considered the “higher genus” Sn,g variants of the
groups Sn , defined by
(28)            xa1 , b1 , . . . , ag , bg | pra1 , b1 sra2 , b2 s ¨ ¨ ¨ rag , bg sqn “ 1y
with n, g ě 1, and proved that the isomorphism problem for one-relator groups
relative to this class is decidable. For other related examples, and a slight general-
ization, see [RK76, Ros76].
     However, a few years later, in 1977 Pride [Pri77a] was able to clarify the
situation of generating tuples of all two-generator one-relator groups with torsion.
He proved that if G “ xA | rn “ 1y is a one-relator group with torsion, with
54                                   1. HISTORY


n ą 1 and r not a proper power, then G has only one T -system of generating tuples
(when n “ 2) or 21 ϕpnq such systems when n ą 2. Here ϕ denotes Euler’s totient
function. He moreover showed that these T -systems can be effectively found, and
hence deduced the following remarkable theorem:
    Theorem 1.6.12 (Pride, 1977 [Pri77a]). The isomorphism problem is decid-
able for two-generator one-relator groups with torsion.
    The method of proof involves much use of the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem
and, of course, induction on the length of the Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy.
Beyond the isomorphism problem, other structural information can also be deduced
from a one-relator group having only one T -system of generating tuples.
    Theorem 1.6.13 (Pride, 1977 [Pri77a]). Let G be a two-generator one-relator
group with torsion. Then:
     (1) G is a hopfian group.
     (2) The automorphism group of G is finitely generated;

     We remark that Rosenberger was able to extend these methods to a slightly
more general setting; we do not recount all such extensions here, but mention a typi-
cal example. Let X1 X X2 ‰ ∅, let wi P Fi for i P t1, 2u be two words. Then Rosen-
berger [Ros77] gave conditions under which the group xX1 , X2 | pw1 w2 qn “ 1y
with n ą 1 only has one T -system of generating tuples, and hence the isomorphism
problem for one-relator groups is decidable with respect to this class.

                     1.7. Subgroups of one-relator groups
Subgroups of zero-relator groups, i.e. free groups, are themselves zero-relator groups;
this is the Nielsen–Schreier Theorem, as presented in §1.2.3. Similarly, many sub-
groups of one-relator groups are free, as the Freiheitssatz tells us. Thus, it seems
natural to ask: what are the subgroups of one-relator groups? In particular, is
every subgroup of a one-relator group a one-relator group? The answer to this
latter question is emphatically no. The subgroup structure of one-relator group
carries a great richness, and although we today know that all one-relator groups
are coherent, much was known already before this was proved. In this section, we
will present several different flavours of such results about subgroups of one-relator
groups.
     We first remark that there is, in general, an abundance of subgroups in one-
relator groups. Baumslag & Miller [BM86] prove that certain one-relator groups
contain many non-isomorphic subgroups. Indeed, they prove, using a simple ar-
gument that as soon as a group G contains a countably infinite number of non-
isomorphic, freely indecomposable subgroups, then G ˚ Z contains continuously
many non-isomorphic subgroups. For example, one can take the solvable Baumslag–
Solitar group
                             BSp1, 2q “ xa, b | b´1 ab “ a2 y
                                      i
in which the subgroup Ei “ xa, b2 y is solvable, and Ei fl Ej if i ‰ j since
H1 pEi , Zq clearly grows in i. Thus BSp1, 2q ˚ Z contains continuously many non-
isomorphic subgroups. Note also that every one-relator group can be embedded
into a 2-generator one-relator group (see e.g. [MKS66, p. 259]), and hence there
                        1.7. SUBGROUPS OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS                                    55


are 2-generator one-relator groups containing continuously many non-isomorphic
subgroups, e.g. the Baumslag–Gersten group
(29)                         BG “ xa, t | ta´1 t´1 atat´1 “ a2 y
which is clearly an HNN-extension of BSp1, 2q. By the above results, it contains
continuously many non-isomorphic subgroups. Thus it also contains countable sub-
groups which are not recursively presentable. Nevertheless, in general, much can be
said about particular classes of subgroups of one-relator groups, particularly their
finitely generated subgroups. In this section, we will see many examples of this.
     We caution the reader that because of the often simultaneous and overlapping
developments at this time, unlike previous sections, the subsections of this section
(and the next) can be read more or less independently of one another, and will
frequently pass from one subject to another with only some connective tissue.
     1.7.1. Magnus subgroups and malnormality. Let G “ xA | r “ 1y, with
r cyclically reduced. Then recall (from §1.3.1) that a Magnus subgroup of G is
any subgroup generated by a proper subset of A. The Freiheitssatz, being the
first result about one-relator groups, was a result about these subgroups: they are
free. More can be said about Magnus subgroups, however. One particular classical
theme which we now expand on is that of malnormality. For an arbitrary group
G, a non-trivial subgroup H ď G is said to be malnormal if gHg ´1 X H “ t1u
for all g P GzH. The terminology malnormal for this property was introduced
by B. Baumslag in his 1965 PhD thesis [Bau65a], see also [Bau68, p. 601]. The
structure of a finite group with a malnormal subgroup is rather restricted. A finite
group G has a (non-trivial) malnormal subgroup H if and only if it is a Frobenius
group, i.e. it acts transitively on some finite set where no non-trivial element fixes
more than one point, and some non-trivial element fixes some point. Then H being
malnormal is a restatement of H being a Frobenius complement, and G splits as a
semidirect product G “ K ¸ H where K is the (normal) subgroup consisting of all
elements not in any conjugate of H [Fro01].
     In general, in infinite groups malnormal subgroups are more complicated. In-
deed, even the problem of deciding whether a subgroup of a free group is malnormal
or not is non-trivial, albeit decidable [BMR99]. Early work on malnormal sub-
groups, other than that by B. Baumslag [Bau65a, Bau68], are four other PhD
theses, all of which were by students of G. Baumslag: the first is by Driscoll [Dri67],
who used it in her study of the conjugacy problem is some amalgamated free prod-
ucts; the second was Whittemore [Whi67], who used malnormal subgroups in her
study of the Frattini subgroups of amalgamated free products; and third by Taylor
[Tay63, Lew67]38 in her study of groups with unique roots. The fourth, and for us
the most important, was by B. B. Newman, who proved the following remarkable
result in his thesis:



  38Here, a possible source of confusion regarding surnames arises. Tekla Taylor and Jacques
Lewin were the first two PhD students of G. Baumslag’s, and they married in 1965. Tekla
took her husband’s surname, and the two would publish together frequently, including the article
containing their famous Lewin–Lewin construction for the group ring of a torsion-free one-relator
group [LL78]. Nevertheless, possibly because of delays in publishing, Tekla’s surname still appears
as Taylor in 1968 in her joint article with Baumslag on the centre of one-relator groups [BT68],
see §1.7.2.
56                                       1. HISTORY


    Theorem 1.7.1 (B. B. Newman, 1968 [New68b, Lemma 2.3.1(I)]). Any Mag-
nus subgroup of a one-relator group with torsion is malnormal.
     This result, and a strengthening of it using the notion of strong malnormality,
was used by Newman in his solution to the conjugacy problem in all one-relator
groups with torsion. Newman’s result would also, many decades later, come to play
a key role in Wise’s recent proof of the residual finiteness of one-relator groups with
torsion [Wis09]. We remark that Moldavanskii [Mol68] further proved that the
intersection of Magnus subgroups generated by disjoint subsets of the generators is
always trivial in a one-relator group with torsion.
     Outside the torsion case, Bagherzadeh [Bag76b, Bag76a] proved in 1976 that
for any one-relator group G, if H ă G is a Magnus subgroup, then H X g ´1 Hg
is cyclic for g R H. Thus any Magnus subgroup of a one-relator group is what
Bagherzadeh calls cyclonormal (a term which later appears also in work by Wise
[Wis01]). It is somewhat surprising that new results are still proved about Mag-
nus subgroups in general one-relator groups, even using mostly classical methods.
Moldavanskii [Mol68] proved that if G is a one-relator group xA | r “ 1y with
X1 , X2 Ď A and X1 X X2 “ ∅, then xX1 y X xX2 y is either trivial or isomorphic
to Z. Collins [Col04] extended this result in 2004 by proving that if X1 , X2 Ă A
generate two Magnus subgroups H1 “ xX1 y and H2 “ xX2 y, then H1 X H2 is either
free on X1 X X2 , or else is a free product of Z with this group.
     1.7.2. The centre of one-relator groups. For any group G, it is natural
to ask about its centre ZpGq. This is also true of one-relator groups. Indeed, Levi,
in his 1933 investigation [Lev33] on free groups 1933, includes as an example the
group BSp2, 2q “ xx, y | x´1 y 2 x “ y 2 y (see §1.5.3), and notes that its centre is
infinite cyclic, and generated by the element y 2 . Generalizing such examples, in
1935 Kalashnikov & Kurosh [KK35] studied subgroups of amalgamated free prod-
ucts in which the amalgamated subgroups are all subgroups of the centres of the
factors, a precursor to the tree products which we shall encounter below. Comput-
ing the centre of finitely presented groups is in general an undecidable problem39,
and Remeslennikov proved that there are finitely presented groups whose centre
is not finitely generated [Rem74] (answering a question of Baumslag [Bau67a,
Question 1]). However, it turns out that for one-relator groups, there is a complete
answer. We give a brief exposition of these results in this section.
     Perhaps the first general result on the centres of one-relator groups is that
the centre of a one-relator group with torsion is necessarily trivial, proved in 1960
[KMS60, Corollary]. The real impetus for the study of the centre of one-relator
groups, however, came in 1964, when Murasugi [Mur64] proved several beautiful
theorems. Let G “ xa1 , . . . , an | R “ 1y be a one-relator group. Then Murasugi
proved: first, if n ą 2, then ZpGq “ 1.40 Furthermore, if we suppose that n “ 2, G
is non-abelian, and ZpGq is non-trivial, then ZpGq is infinite cyclic. Thus the centre
of any one-relator group G is either trivial, infinite cyclic, or Z2 – and the last case
happens if and only if G – Z2 (and there is only one cyclically reduced one-relator
presentation for Z2 , see §1.3.2). The proofs of this pass via the Alexander polynomial

   39This is not a direct consequence of the Adian–Rabin theorem [Adi55, Rab58], but follows
from a 1959 extension of it by Baumslag, Boone & Neumann [BBN59].
   40In a sequel article, Murasugi [Mur65] conjectured that any group with deficiency d ě 2 has
trivial centre, which remains an open problem.
                     1.7. SUBGROUPS OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS                           57


of G, as defined by Fox [Fox54], and the proofs also give sufficient conditions for
ZpGq to be trivial. However, the question of completely characterizing when ZpGq
is trivial was left open. In 1968, this problem was solved by Baumslag & Taylor
[BT68], who proved that there exists an algorithm for deciding whether a one-
relator group has trivial centre or not. This was the starting point for a natural
line of investigation: what is the structure of one-relator groups with a non-trivial
centre?
     Some partial results in this line were obtained by Meskin, Pietrowski & Stein-
berg [MPS73]. The question was fully answered by Pietrowski in 1974 (although
the results already appear in his 1972 PhD thesis [Pie72]) using subgroup theorems
of Karrass & Solitar. Recall that a tree product of groups is the fundamental group
of a graph of groups, where the underlying graph is a simple tree. A stem product
is a tree product in which the underlying tree is a path, introduced in [KS70].
     Theorem 1.7.2 (Pietrowski, 1974 [Pie74]). Any one-relator group with non-
trivial centre is a (finite) stem product of cyclic groups or an HNN-extension of
such a group with cyclic associated subgroups.
     Associated to any finite stem product of cyclic groups is a finite sequence of
pairs of integers, corresponding to the amalgamated subgroups. This sequence can,
it turns out, be used to essentially uniquely determine the one-relator group up to
isomorphism. As a consequence, we have the following corollary:
Corollary 1.7.3 (Pietrowski, 1974 [Pie74]). The isomorphism problem is decid-
able for one-relator groups with non-trivial centre.
     The problem of deciding precisely when a stem product of cyclic groups is a one-
relator group is non-trivial and was left open by Pietrowski. Some partial results
were obtained by, among others, McCool [McC91], and it was eventually solved in
1999 by Metaftsis [Met99]. As pointed out by Meskin, the above structural result
(Theorem 1.7.2) makes no essential use of the fact that the group is a one-relator
group, but applies equally well to any two-generator infinite (f.g. free)-by-cyclic
groups with a non-trivial center. This was expanded on by Karrass, Pietrowski &
Solitar [KPS73], where they completely characterize the structure of infinite (f.g.
free)-by-cyclic groups with a non-trivial centre as a tree product of infinite cyclic
groups.
     We mention another decidability result for one-relator groups with a non-trivial
centre, proved by Moldavanskii & Timofeeva in 1987. Recall that a finitely gener-
ated subgroup H ď G is said to be separable if for every g P GzH, there is some
subgroup Kg P G such that H ď Kg and g R Kg . In particular, separability of the
trivial subgroup is precisely residual finiteness. We say that G is subgroup separable
if all its finitely generated subgroups are separable.
    Theorem 1.7.4 (Moldavanskii & Timofeeva, 1987 [MT87]). All one-relator
groups with non-trivial centre are subgroup separable. In particular, the subgroup
membership problem is decidable in one-relator groups with non-trivial centre.
    We conclude our discussion on the centre by mentioning the centre of subgroups
of one-relator groups. The subgroups of one-relator groups can be complicated in
general, but their centres can be completely understood, analogously to Murasugi’s
theorem.
58                                  1. HISTORY


    Theorem 1.7.5 (Mahimovski, 1971 [Mah71] / Karrass, Pietrowski & Solitar,
1974 [KPS74]). Let G be a torsion-free one-relator group and H ă G be a subgroup
with non-trivial centre 1 ę ZpHq ę H. Then ZpHq is infinite cyclic.
     Related to this theorem is a question posed by Baumslag [Bau71b] in 1974: if
G is a one-relator group with at least 3 generators, and N Ÿ G is such that G{N
is infinite, does G{N 1 have trivial centre? Here N 1 is the derived subgroup of N .
Fischer [Fis76] gave a negative answer to this question shortly thereafter.
     Just as for many other theorems about subgroups of one-relator groups, there
is an important (co)homological point of view. Swan [Swa69, p. 156] proved
in 1969 that for any finitely generated non-abelian group G with cdZ pGq ă 8,
we have cdZ pZpGqq ă cdZ pZpGqq. In particular, Swan’s theorem can be used to
prove Theorem 1.7.5, as the cohomological dimension of any torsion-free one-relator
group is ď 2 (see §1.4.2), and any abelian group of cohomological dimension ď 1
is of course either 1 or Z. In 1976, Bieri [Bie76, Theorem C] extended Swan’s
theorem by dropping the hypothesis of finite generation. In the same article, Bieri
[Bie76, Theorem D] also gave a homological generalization of many of the above
results on the centre, by proving the following: let G be a group of type FP8 pZq
with cdZ pGq “ n with ZpGq free abelian of rank n ´ 1. Then G is a treed HNN-
extension over a finite tree with all edges and all vertices – ZpGq. In particular,
when G is a torsion-free one-relator group with non-trivial centre, we obtain the
following consequences:
       (1) every finitely generated subgroup of G is finitely presented and of type
           FP8 pZq,
       (2) rG, Gs X ZpGq “ 1,
       (3) rG, Gs is free.
We refer the reader to [Bie76, Corollary 6.6] for more details. In this line there
are also earlier results by Baumslag & Gruenberg [BG67], e.g. that for any two-
generated subgroup H ď G of a one-relator group, if ZpHq is cyclic and H{ZpHq
is periodic, then H is itself cyclic.
     For a final note on one-relator groups with non-trivial centre, we mention that
their (outer) automorphism groups are also known. As a classical example, recall
that Schreier (see §1.2.2) determined the automorphism group of Gm,n “ xa, b |
am bn “ 1y, and it is easy to see that the corresponding group is virtually free
(note that Pettet [Pet95] has also proved a structural result for all finitely gen-
erated groups with virtually free automorphism group). Extending this result,
Gilbert, Howie, Metaftsis & Raptis [GHMR00] proved that the outer automor-
phism group of a one-relator group G with non-trivial centre falls into two cases:
first, if rkZ Gab “ 1, then either Z{2Z, or else G – xa, b | am “ bm y for some
m ě 2, and in the latter case OutpGq – pZ{2Zq2 , as proved by Schreier in 1924,
see §1.2.2. If instead rkZ Gab “ 2, then either G – Z2 , or OutpGq – D8 , or
OutpGq – D8 ˆ pZ{2Zq.

    1.7.3. Commutativity in one-relator groups. As we mentioned earlier
(in §1.5.4), in his 1964 survey article Baumslag asked which abelian groups arise
as subgroups of one-relator groups. In particular, he [Bau64, p. 391] conjectured
that the additive group of rationals is not a subgroup of any one-relator group.
He also says “one might however ask just which abelian groups are subgroups
of a group with one defining relator”. We have already seen that Moldavanskii
                     1.7. SUBGROUPS OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS                          59


[Mol67] gave a nearly complete answer to this question in 1967, see §1.5.4, but
that he left unresolved the question of whether pQ, `q embeds into any one-relator
group. This would be done by B. B. Newman in 1968, again in his thesis, who gave
an independent proof of Moldavanskii’s result (Theorem 1.5.9), and clarified the
situation of the locally cyclic subgroups as follows:
     Theorem 1.7.6 (B. B. Newman, 1968 [New68b, Corollary 1.2.4]). Let G be
a torsion-free one-relator group. Then no non-trivial element of G has more than
finitely many prime divisors. Moreover, a non-trivial element is not divisible by
more than finitely many powers of a prime p if p is greater than the length of the
relator.
    As an immediate consequence of this theorem, we see that:
Corollary 1.7.7 (B. B. Newman, 1968 [New68b, Corollary 1.2.4]). The additive
group of rationals is not a subgroup of any one-relator group.
    This completed the classification of the abelian subgroups of one-relator groups.
New proofs of these results, particularly characterizing the abelian subgroups of
amalgamated free products, were given by Wright [Wri79] using Bass–Serre theory,
and Nagata [Nag80].
    We now turn to solvable subgroups of one-relator groups. This is a more difficult
question, but again it becomes easier to answer in the case of one-relator groups
with torsion. Unlike the proof of the Spelling Theorem, the proof of this result
(which was also announced in [New68a]) was published in the literature, and the
proof of Theorem 1.7.8 is the subject of the 1973 article [New73].
    Theorem 1.7.8 (B. B. Newman, 1968 [New68b, Corollary 2.3.3]). The solv-
able subgroups of a one-relator group with torsion are cyclic.
    Karrass & Solitar [KS69] determined which one-relator groups contain abelian
normal subgroups. In particular, the only one-relator group, other than Z2 itself,
containing a normal subgroup isomorphic to Z2 is xa, b | abab´1 “ 1y, the funda-
mental group of the Klein bottle. Another student of Greendlinger in Tula was
A. A. Chebotar, who proved several remarkable theorems in the 1970s, extending
Moldavanskii’s work. First, in 1971 [Che71], he extended Theorem 1.5.10 to all
subgroups of one-relator groups, proving that if H ď xA | r “ 1y is non-abelian and
such that H does not contain a free subgroup of rank 2, then either H – C2 ˆ C2
or G – BSp1, kq for k P Z. Thus, we also get a full classification of the solvable
subgroups of one-relator groups, and a “Tits alternative” for one-relator groups.
Next, in 1975 he extended Karrass & Solitar’s aforementioned result on abelian
normal subgroups to all subgroups of one-relator groups [Che75]. But perhaps the
most important result proved by Chebotar was the following, which was also given
a proof by Bagherzadeh around the same time:
    Theorem 1.7.9 (Bagherzadeh, 1976 [Bag76b] / Chebotar, 1978 [Che78]).
Let G “ xA | r “ 1y be a one-relator group, and let H1 ˆ H2 ď G be a non-trivial
direct product. Then one of H1 and H2 is isomorphic to Z, and the other is locally
cyclic.
     In particular, the direct product F2 ˆF2 of two non-abelian free groups does not
embed into any one-relator group. This direct product has a range of bad properties,
e.g. undecidable membership problem [Mik58] and incoherence [Sta77]. Today,
60                                    1. HISTORY


we know that one-relator groups are coherent, giving another (significantly more
difficult) proof of Chebotar’s theorem. Finally, Chebotar notes that his results
give an answer to Question 6 from Baumslag’s 1974 survey [Bau71b], published
only four years earlier. This showcases the efficiency of transferring results and
open problems across the Iron Curtain even during this time, and particularly the
remarkable work by Greendlinger at making Tula a leading centre of combinatorial
group theory.
    1.7.4. Coherence and subgroup separability. The first property we will
consider is coherence. Recall that a group is said to be coherent if all of its finitely
generated subgroups are finitely presented. As mentioned at the beginning of §1.6,
Baumslag conjectured that all one-relator groups are coherent, which we today
know is the case. The coherence of all one-relator groups was also said to be
“conceivable” by Bieri [Bie76, p. 36]. Some classical results yield coherence in
small classes of one-relator groups: for example, it follows from Theorem 1.2.3 that
surface groups π1 pΣg q are coherent for all g ě 1. Perhaps the first large contribution
to the coherence of one-relator groups came in 1976. As we have seen in §1.7.2,
one-relator groups with non-trivial centre are in general significantly better behaved
than their centreless counterparts, and general theorems easier to come by. This is
true also for coherence:
   Theorem 1.7.10 (Bieri, 1976 [Bie76, Corollary 6.6]). One-relator groups with
non-trivial centre are coherent.
    Beyond this result, however, few truly general results were known regarding
coherence. More was known on the structure of particular finitely presented sub-
groups of one-relator groups. For example, one can ask what the finitely presented
normal subgroups of one-relator groups are. In this line, we have the following
theorem, which can be obtained by combining two closely related results due to
Bieri and Karrass & Solitar.
    Theorem 1.7.11 (Bieri [Bie76, Theorem B] / Karrass & Solitar [KS78]). Let
G be a one-relator group and let H Ĳ G be a non-trivial finitely presented normal
subgroup. If H has infinite index in G, then H is free, and G is torsion-free and has
two generators. Furthermore, G is an infinite cyclic or infinite dihedral extension
of a finitely generated free group.
     In fact, Bieri proved a stronger statement: the above theorem holds for all
finitely generated groups of cohomological dimension ď 2, which includes all torsion-
free one-relator groups (see §1.4.2). Notably, the proof of both the results of Bieri
and Karrass & Solitar use cohomological methods, including the rational Euler
characteristic, cf. [Wal61, Chi76].
     Similarly, one can also ask about when finitely generated normal subgroups have
infinite index in one-relator groups. Recall that Schreier (§1.2.3) proved that any
finitely generated normal subgroup of a free group has finite index. The analogous
theorem fails already for some very basic one-relator groups: for example, in the
torus knot group G “ xa, b | a2 “ b3 y the commutator subgroup is finitely generated
and free (cf. Theorem 1.5.11), but and hence has infinite index in G as the defining
word a2 b´3 is not primitive (see Theorem 1.4.2). Note that G – Z ˚Z Z, where the
amalgamated subgroup of course has finite index in both factors; this turns out to
one source of obstructions to extending Schreier’s result. Karrass & Solitar [KS73]
                     1.7. SUBGROUPS OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS                          61


proved a number of results on amalgams with no such obstructions, and we state
one of the relevant corollaries of their results:
     Theorem 1.7.12 (Karrass & Solitar [KPS73, p. 23]). Let G “ A ˚U B be an
amalgamated free product where U has infinite index in A, the subgroups of U are
all finitely generated, and every finitely generated normal subgroup of A has finite
index in A. Then every finitely generated normal subgroup of G has finite index in
G.
    Thus, for example, the group G1 “ xa, b, c | a2 “ b3 y does have the property
that every finitely generated normal subgroup of G1 has finite index in G1 .
    Other than coherence, another finiteness property for groups in relation to
their finitely generated subgroups is subgroup separability, which we have mentioned
above (see Theorem 1.7.4). Since not every one-relator group is residually finite,
neither is every one-relator group subgroup separable. However, in general subgroup
separability is a much stronger condition even for one-relator groups. Indeed, Burns,
Karrass & Solitar [BKS87] proved that the one-relator group defined by
(30)                xa, b | ra, bab´1 s “ 1y – xa, b | rab, bas “ 1y
is not subgroup separable. However, the group (30) is residually finite, indeed (f.g.
free)-by-cyclic, by Theorem 1.7.15.
     Finally, we mention the Howson property. We say that a group G has the
Howson property if the intersection of any two finitely generated subgroups of G is
again finitely generated. Howson [How54] proved that free groups have the Howson
property (see [Imr77] for a simple proof), and it is known that free products
of Howson groups are Howson [Bau66], also reproved by Bezverkhnii & Rollov
[BR74]; cf. also [Bur72, Kap97]. It is thus natural to ask to what extent the
Howson property holds in one-relator groups. Greenberg [Gre60a, Theorem 2]
proved in 1960 that all surface groups are Howson. Moldavanskii [Mol68] proved
that BSp1, nq for n P Z have the Howson property. However, Moldavanskii [Mol68]
also proved that any one-relator group with a non-trivial centre fails to have the
Howson property. Kapovich [Kap99] gave an example of a hyperbolic torsion-free
one-relator group which is not Howson. Kapovich [Kap96] also proved that if
G “ xA | rn “ 1y is a finitely generated one-relator group with torsion such that
n ě 5, then the intersection of any two 2-generated subgroups of G is again finitely
generated.

     1.7.5. Quotients and residual properties of one-relator groups. We
discuss some results on quotients of one-relator groups, beginning with their fi-
nite quotients and some results on residual solvability and nilpotency. Of course,
Baumslag–Solitar groups (§1.5.3) give examples of non-residually finite one-relator
groups, but many other examples of residually finite one-relator groups are known.
     In 1963, G. Baumslag [Bau63b] proved several fundamental results regard-
ing residual finiteness and nilpotent groups: for example, he proved that the free
product of two residually finite groups, amalgamated in a finite subgroup, is al-
ways residually finite (see also [Tre73] for a simple proof of this fact). One of his
results concerns one-relator groups directly, and we have already seen it in §1.5.2:
the free product of two free groups, amalgamated in a cyclic subgroup, is residu-
ally finite [Bau63b, Theorem 7] (cf. also [Eva73, Weh73, Shi88] in this line).
Such amalgams, called cyclically pinched one-relator groups, provide a rich source of
62                                    1. HISTORY


residually finite one-relator groups (including the surface group π1 pΣ2 q, and hence
also all π1 pΣg q for g ą 1, see §1.2.2).
    Theorem 1.7.13 (Baumslag, 1963 [Bau63b]). Cyclically pinched one-relator
groups are residually finite.
     Strengthening this, in 1981 Allenby [All81] proved that cyclically pinched one-
relator groups are potent, a stronger form of residual finiteness (for every non-trivial
element g and every n P N, there is a homomorphism onto a finite group which maps
g onto an element of order exactly n).
     We turn to positive one-relator groups and related classes of groups. A one-
relator group G is said to be positive if it admits some presentation xA | r “ 1y
where r P FA contains no inverse letters. Note that this condition has some subtlety:
for example G “ xa, b | bab´1 a “ 1y is, in spite of its disguise, a positive one-relator
group, since G – xx, y | x2 y 2 “ 1y. Indeed, the isomorphism problem of one-
relator groups with respect to the class of positive one-relator groups is still an
open problem, i.e. it is unknown whether we can decide if a given one-relator group
is positive or not. Note that e.g. the group Z2 “ xa, b | ra, bs “ 1y is not positive,
by Proposition 1.3.4. Positive one-relator groups enjoy some particular properties.
The first, and perhaps most striking, theorem in this line is the following:
    Theorem 1.7.14 (Baumslag, 1971 [Bau]). Positive one-relator groups are
residually solvable.
     In particular, every perfect subgroup of a positive one-relator group is trivial.
It follows essentially from the local indicability of one-relator groups (see below)
that all finitely generated perfect subgroups of one-relator groups are trivial; if one
drops the assumption of finite generation, then the answer is negative, as evidenced
by the Baumslag–Gersten group BGp1, 2q defined in (31).
     When the defining relator is positive and a proper power, i.e. we are consid-
ering a positive one-relator group with torsion G, then G is residually finite by
Theorem 1.6.9 in §1.6.2. Of course, solvable groups are not in general residually fi-
nite, indeed they can have undecidable word problem, by Kharlampovich [Kha81].
Furthermore, positive one-relator groups are not residually finite in general. Indeed,
any Baumslag–Solitar group BSpm, ´nq with m, n ě 0 admits a positive presen-
tation, as noticed by Higman [Bau, p. 166] and, independently, Perrin & Schupp
[PS84].
     One-relator groups are, in general, not residually solvable, as e.g. the Baumslag–
Gersten group G “ xa, b | ra, bab´1 s “ ay has a lie in every term of the derived
series. It is not hard to see that G – xx, y | x2 yxy “ yxy, and so in general one-
relator groups with a defining relation of the form uv ´1 “ 1 for two positive words
is not residually solvable. However, with some additional assumptions, more can
be said also in this case. This was the subject of a 1983 article by Baumslag, some
of the results of which we now present. First, recall that for a word w P FA and a
letter a P A, we let σa pwq be the exponent sum of a in w.
     Theorem 1.7.15 (Baumslag, 1983 [Bau83]). Let G “ xA | u “ vy where
u, v P FA are positive words, and where σa puq “ σa pvq for all a P A. Then G is
free-by-cyclic.
    Note that one particular case of groups in the class from Theorem 1.7.15 is the
case of a commutator ru, vs “ 1, where u, v P FA are positive words. Not every
                      1.7. SUBGROUPS OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS                              63


one-relator group of the form ru, vs “ 1 with general u, v P FA is free-by-cyclic (see
[Bau83, §4.3]), but it is possible to add further conditions on u and v, other than
positivity, to ensure residual finiteness.
    Theorem 1.7.16 (Baumslag, 1983 [Bau83]). Let G “ xA Y B | ru, vs “ 1y
where A X B “ ∅ and u P FA , v P FB . Then some term in the lower central series
of G is free; and G is residually finite.
     Thus, for example, the group xa, b, c | ram , cbn c´1 s “ 1y is residually finite for
all m, n P Z. For extensions of Theorem 1.7.16 to a slightly more general setting,
see Loginova [Log99].
     On the other hand, some one-relator groups have residual finiteness fail in a
rather dramatic fashion. By basic linear algebra, every infinite one-relator group
obviously surjects Z, and hence also has all cyclic groups as a quotient. Baumslag
[Bau69b] proved, remarkably, that there also exist non-cyclic one-relator groups
all of whose finite quotients are cyclic. This can be seen as a very strong failure
of residual finiteness. The group in question was generalized to a family of groups
known as the Baumslag–Gersten groups
(31)                  BGpm, nq “ xa, b | bab´1 am ba´1 b´1 “ an y
and the original example in [Bau69b] is the case of pm, nq “ p1, 2q. The groups
have seen some study. Gersten [Ger92] proved that the Dehn function of BGp1, 2q
grows faster than any finite tower of exponentials, later sharpened by Platonov
[Pla04]. Brunner [Bru80] investigated the automorphism group of BGpm, nq, and
Kavutskii & Moldavanskii [KM88] proved that BGpm, nq is residually finite if and
only if |m| “ |n|, and it is not hard to see that all of the finite quotients of BGpm, nq
are cyclic if and only if m “ n ˘ 1.
     Recall that finitely generated nilpotent groups are residually finite (§1.4.3). In
1964, A. Steinberg [Ste63, Ste64] studied free nilpotent quotients of one-relator
groups. Specifically, let G “ xA | R “ 1y where R P rFA , FA s, i.e. the exponent sum
of every generator in R is zero. Steinberg then proved that there exists an algorithm
for deciding whether or not G has a rank 2 free nilpotent quotient. Shapiro &
Sonn [SS74b] reproved his theorem in a cohomological setting and extended it
to a somewhat broader class of one-relator groups, see also [Son74]. One of the
corollaries of Steinberg’s results in [Ste64] is that the one-relator group xa, b, c |
a2 b2 c3 “ 1y has free nilpotent quotient groups of rank 2 and all classes; however,
it does not surject the free group of rank 2 (this follows e.g. by a theorem of
Schützenberger [Sch59]). We shall see more of such groups in §1.7.6. Finally,
we remark that although automorphism groups of one-relator groups remain in
general somewhat mysterious, a very elegant and short argument due to Baumslag
[Bau63a] shows that the automorphism group of any finitely generated residually
finite group is again residually finite. Thus, automorphism groups of residually
finite one-relator groups provide a rich source of residually finite groups.
     Free groups are one-relator groups, and studying the residual properties of
free groups often leads to interesting questions related to one-relator groups more
general. First, note that by a simple argument, Fn is residually F2 for all n ě 2, see
[Pel66]. Peluso [Pel66] also proved that the free group of rank n ě 2 is residually
PSLp2, pq for any p ą 3. In relation to this result, we mention briefly that an old
conjecture by Magnus was that for any infinite family of finite non-abelian simple
groups G, the free group F2 is residually G. This led to many significant articles,
64                                    1. HISTORY


e.g. [KM68, GL70, Pos70, Pri72, Wie77, Lub86, Wil91], leading finally to a
full resolution [Wei92b, Wei92a, Wei93], cf. also [Val93]. Magnus [Mag69, p.
309] raises the point that non-abelian free groups are residually xa, b | an y for n ą 1,
as proved by [KM68], and also “possibly residually S for a large number of other
one-relator groups S”. This line of investigation was taken up by Pride [Pri72],
and especially in [Pri74, Chapter 3], where he investigates when free groups are
residually one-relator groups with torsion, making heavy use of the B. B. Newman
Spelling Theorem. In particular, he proves:
    Theorem 1.7.17 (Pride, 1974 [Pri74, §3.2]). Let G “ xx1 , x2 , . . . , xk | rn “ 1y
with n ą 1 be an arbitrary one-relator group with torsion.
     (1) If k ą 3, then Fk is residually G.
     (2) If k “ 2, and r ‰ rx1 , x2 s, then F2 is residually G.
     (3) If k “ 2, and r “ rx1 , x2 s, then F2 is not residually G.
    For more on the class of groups Sn “ xa, b | ra, bsn “ 1y with n ą 1, see §1.6.3.
    We now turn to more general quotients of one-relator groups. There is in
general no shortage of quotients of one-relator groups. Recall that a countable
group G is SQ-universal if every countable group embeds in some quotient of G.
In 1973, P. Neumann [Neu73] conjectured that any one-relator group is either
solvable (and hence either BSp1, nq or cyclic) or else is SQ-universal, which remains
an open problem. In 1974, Sacerdote & Schupp [SS74a] proved that every one-
relator group with at least three generators is SQ-universal. B. Baumslag & Pride
[BP78] generalized this to prove that if G is a group with n generators and r
relators, such that n ´ r ě 2, then G has a finite index subgroup which surjects F2 .
Finally, Stöhr proved the following general result in 1983:
    Theorem 1.7.18 (Stöhr, 1983 [Stö83]). Every one-relator group with torsion
is SQ-universal.
     In looking at quotients of one-relator groups, it is natural to ask the follow-
ing question: given two one-relator groups which are homomorphic images of one
another, are they necessarily isomorphic? This question was first posed by Mol-
davanskii in 1969 (see Question 3.33 in the Kourovka Notebook [KM24]). The
question was given a negative answer only 40 years later by Borshchev & Molda-
vanskii [BM06], indicating the difficulty of even such simple-sounding problems.
     Finally, we briefly mention a very important result on quotients of one-relator
groups. A group is said to be indicable if it surjects Z, and locally indicable if
all of its finitely generated subgroups also surject Z. The notion of an indicable
group was introduced by Higman [Hig40, p. 242], who proved that if G is locally
indicable and R is an integral domain, then RG has no zero divisors and no non-
trivial units [Hig40, Theorem 12]. Baumslag [Bau71b] conjectured that every
torsion-free one-relator group is locally indicable. Brodskii [Bro80] announced in
1980 the theorem that this conjecture is true, i.e. all torsion-free one-relator groups
are locally indicable. Brodskii [Bro84] only published the full proofs of these facts
four years later; in the meantime, the theorem was also given topological proof by
Howie [How82]. The subject of local indicability and its many fascinating links
with many broader topics reaches far outside the scope of this survey, however.
   1.7.6. Lower central series and parafree groups. Let G be any group.
Recall that the lower central series γ1 pGq, γ2 pGq, . . . of G is defined inductively by
                     1.7. SUBGROUPS OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS                            65


γ1 pGq “ G Àand γn`1 pGq “ rG, γn pGqs. This determines a Z-graded Lie (Z´)algebra
grpGq :“ ně1 γn pGq{γn`1 pGq, where the factor γn pGq{γn`1 pGq is termed the nth
lower central factor of G. The sequence of lower central factors is sometimes called
the lower central sequence of G. The lower central factors of any finitely presented
group are finitely generated abelian groups, and are furthermore computable from
the presentation (by a commutator collection process [Hal34], see e.g. [AP90,
Bau99]) and thus form a particularly convenient set of invariants of groups. This
is in stark contrast to the derived series of a group: if G2 denotes the second derived
subgroup of G, then G{G2 need not be finitely presented, even if G is a one-relator
group. Indeed, as proved by Baumslag & Strebel [BS76a]: if Gm,n,r “ xa, t |
tr am t´r “ an y, where mnr ‰ 0 and gcdpm, nq ą 1 or |r| ą 1 and mn “ 1, then
H2 pG{G2 , Zq is not finitely generated. On the other hand, if gcdpm, nq “ 1 and
|m| ‰ 1 ‰ |n|, then G{G2 is not finitely presented, even though H2 pG{G2 , Zq is
finitely generated.
     The structure of the Lie algebra grpGq has seen some study in the case of one-
relator groups. We mention some results briefly. First, if G “ Fn , a free group
on n generators, then grpFn q is the free Lie algebra on n generators (see [MKS66,
Chapter 5]). If G “ F {R, then there is a canonical surjection grpF q ↠ grpGq. This
kernel is quite non-trivial already for some very simple examples of one-relator
groups. For example, it was determined when G “ xx, y | xp “ 1y with p a prime
by Labute [Lab77]. In the case that the defining word is not a proper power,
more can be said. Indeed, Labute [Lab70] found an explicit formula for the rank
of γn pGq{γn`1 pGq when G “ xA | r “ 1y, as long as r is not an nth power
modulo certain terms of the lower central series of the free group F “ F pAq, and
furthermore that in this case the associated Lie algebra grpGq is itself definable by a
single defining relation (as a Lie algebra), cf. also [Lab67]. This, for example, gives
a closed formula for the rank of γn pπ1 pΣ2 qq, where π1 pΣ2 q is the fundamental group
of a genus 2 surface. If gn denotes the Z-rank of the free abelian group γn pπ1 pΣ2 qq,
then
                                  ¨                                     ˛
                                                          ˆ      ˙
                       1 ÿ     n       ÿ              d     d ´ i d´2i ‚
(32)             gn “       µp q ˝            p´1qi                4
                       n       d                    d´i       i
                        d|n        0ďiďtd{2u

where µ?denotes the Möbius function. One can use (32) to show that gn grows
as p2 ` 3qn , and more generally the exponential   agrowth rate of the ranks in the
case of π1 pΣg q with genus g ą 1 is given by g ` g 2 ´ 1. It also follows from the
aforementioned result by Labute that grpπ1 pΣg qq is a one-relator Lie algebra (in
particular, it has decidable word problem [Shi62]). By contrast, it is not hard to
show that grpBSp1, 3qq is not finitely presented as a Lie algebra.
     We now turn to a very important aspect of the lower central series. This goes
back to the 1939 theorem by Magnus (see Theorem 1.4.3(3)) that any n-generator
group with the same sequence of lower central factors as the n-generator free group
is itself free. This prompted H. Neumann to ask whether any residually nilpotent
group with the same sequence of lower central factors as a free group is necessarily
free. This leads to the following definition. A group G is said to be parafree if it is
residually nilpotent and has the same sequence of lower central factors as some free
group. Thus H. Neumann’s conjecture simply asked: do non-free parafree groups
exist? G. Baumslag [Bau64, Bau65b, Bau67b, Bau69a] would approach this
question in a series of papers (cf. also [BS64]), and prove:
66                                     1. HISTORY


     Theorem 1.7.19 (G. Baumslag, 1964 [Bau64]). Non-free parafree groups exist.
     One such example given in [Bau64], with proof going via a careful study of
amalgamated free products to be found in [Bau65b], is xa, b, c | a2 b3 c5 “ 1y. We
note the reader that the terminology used in [Bau64] is pseudofree, rather than
parafree. Other examples of non-free parafree groups given in 1969 by Baumslag
[Bau69a] are the groups xa, b, c | a “ rci , asrcj , bsy which are parafree, but not free,
for all i, j P Z such that ij ‰ 0. Of course, the free group with which they share their
sequence of lower central factors is the free group of rank 2. This family belongs to
the broader class of parafree-by-cyclic groups, studied by Wong [Won78, Won80]
in his PhD thesis, supervised by G. Baumslag. Parafree groups share a great deal
of properties with free groups. Recall (§1.7.5) that any non-cyclic free group is
residually free of rank two. Similarly, one can show that any non-cyclic parafree
group is also residually parafree of rank two [Bau69a, Theorem 5.2]. We also have
the following curious result, showing that the local behaviour of parafree groups is
remarkably like that of free groups:
    Theorem 1.7.20 (G. Baumslag [Bau69a, Theorem 4.2]). Any two-generator
subgroup of a parafree group is free.
     Baumslag & Stammbach [BS76b] have also given an example of a non-free
parafree group all of whose countable subgroups are free. We remark that for a
residually nilpotent group G, we have that G is a parafree group if and only if grpGq
is a free Lie algebra. Using the Nielsen–Schreier theorem for free Lie algebras due
to Witt [Wit37], it is possible to give another proof of Theorem 1.7.20. This final
part of the section is only intended to serve as a small sampler of parafree groups.
Very much more can be written, particularly on parafree one-relator groups. Many
of the techniques and results involved in the study of such groups go far beyond
the classical, and is better left for a separate survey.
     We conclude this section by noting that one can go beyond residual nilpotence
in the following way. Let G be any group. Then its transfinite lower central series
is
Ş defined as in the finite case by γ1 pGq “ G, γα`1 “ rG, γα pGqs, and γλ pGq “
   αăλ γα pGq for limit ordinals λ. Then the nilpotency class of G is the minimal
α such that γ1`α pGq “ 1. Thus, for example, a residually nilpotent group has
nilpotency class ď ω. Baumslag [Bau71b] asked whether every one-relator group
has nilpotency class ă ω 2 . Indeed it was open for some time whether there were
finitely presented groups of nilpotency class ą ω, until Levine [Lev91] gave an
example of such groups in 1991. Baumslag’s question was only answered in 2016,
when Mikhailov [Mik16] proved that xa, b | ab´1 a3 “ b´2 aby has nilpotency class
ω2 .

                         1.8. More on decision problems
    Even though the word problem was solved for one-relator groups almost a
century ago, as presented in §1.3.3, remarkably both the conjugacy problem and
isomorphism problem – the other two of Dehn’s three fundamental problems –
remain open for one-relator groups. In this section, we will discuss some partial
progress made on these problems, and the general approaches to the problems.
Finally, we will discuss the word problem in some greater depth, including some
open problems, and the possibility of extending the solution of the word problem
to all two-relator groups.
                         1.8. MORE ON DECISION PROBLEMS                              67


     1.8.1. The conjugacy problem. We begin with a general remark regarding
the difficulty of the conjugacy problem. Decidability of the conjugacy problem
implies decidability of the word problem, and hence the conjugacy problem is for
general finitely presented groups undecidable. However, P. S. Novikov [Nov54]
proved in 1955 the existence of finitely presented groups Ap1 ,p2 with undecidable
conjugacy problem, the construction of which is significantly simpler than his group
with undecidable word problem (see §1.4.4). He left the question of the decidability
of the word problem in Ap1 ,p2 open. In 1960, A. A. Fridman (a student of Novikov)
proved that the groups Ap1 ,p2 have decidable word problem, thus showing that the
conjugacy problem is in general strictly harder than the word problem. We note
that in 1967, Bokut [Bok67] simplified both Novikov’s argument and Fridman’s
argument, and Collins [Col69c] in 1969, proved that the conjugacy problem can
be arbitrarily difficult – i.e. of any recursively enumerable degree of unsolvability –
even in groups with decidable word problem.
     More specifically to one-relator groups, recall that Magnus’ solution to the
word problem (§1.3.3) in all one-relator groups depends, as clarified by Moldavan-
skii (§1.5.4), in large part on properties of HNN-extensions and amalgamated free
products. Summarizing this argument, heavy appeal is made to the fact that if
the word problem is decidable in two groups G1 , G2 , and H ď G1 , G2 is a common
subgroup in which membership is decidable for both G1 and G2 , then the amalga-
mated free product G1 ˚H G2 has decidable word problem; the analogous statement
also holds for HNN-extensions, mutatis mutandis. Thus the word problem (more
specifically membership in Magnus subgroups) can be passed down from a one-
relator group to the next one-relator group in the Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy,
possibly embedding the group into a larger one-relator group along the way, until
a free group is reached, and the word problem is decidable in all one-relator groups
by induction. In seeking to extend this line of argument to the conjugacy problem,
we are faced with two problems:

     (1) Decidability of the conjugacy problem is a much more delicate property
         to preserve when taking HNN-extensions or amalgamated free products;
         and
     (2) Decidability of the conjugacy problem is not, in general, preserved by
         taking subgroups, even of finite index.

     We elaborate on these two points. For the first part, there is the following gen-
eral undecidability result which, a priori, shows that additional ingredients must be
added to the Magnus–Moldavanskii hierarchy to adapt it to the conjugacy problem:

    Theorem 1.8.1 (Miller, 1971 [Mil71, Theorem III.10]). There exists a finitely
presented HNN-extension of a free group with undecidable conjugacy problem. There
exists an amalgam Fn ˚Fk Fm of two finitely generated free groups over a finitely
generated free subgroup with undecidable conjugacy problem.

    Note that the groups in Theorem 1.8.1 all have decidable word problem, since
the subgroup membership problem is decidable in free groups. We refer the reader
to [BMR07a, BMR07b] for more in-depth investigations into the complexity of
the conjugacy problem in such amalgams of free groups. We also note that the
conjugacy problem in HNN-extensions, and thus in some one-relator groups, has a
68                                   1. HISTORY


very combinatorial flavour, and can thus often be phrased in terms of semigroup-
theoretic problems. For example, for certain HNN-extensions the conjugacy prob-
lem reduces to reachability problems of vector addition systems, see [Ans76c,
Ans76b, Ans78], and sometimes further reduced to the word problem for commu-
tative semigroups; see [Ans76a]. Similarly, Horadam & Farr [HF94] have reduced
the conjugacy problem in some HNN-extensions to membership problems in inverse
semigroups.
     For the second part, i.e. closure under taking subgroups, it was probably first
recognized by Collins [Col69a] that there exists a finitely presented group G with
a finitely presented subgroup H ď G such that G has decidable conjugacy problem,
but H has undecidable conjugacy problem. In 1971, C. F. Miller [Mil71] proved
that there exist subgroups of F2 ˆ F2 with undecidable conjugacy problem, even
though the conjugacy problem in F2 ˆ F2 is almost trivial. His example is based on
the undecidability of the membership problem in F2 ˆ F2 due to K. A. Mikhailova
[Mik58] (a PhD student of P. S. Novikov). In 1977, Collins & Miller [CM77]
proved that in general neither passing to index 2 subgroups nor index 2 extensions
preserve decidability of the conjugacy problem. In this line, see also [GK75]. A
natural question that arises in this context is the following:
Problem 1.8.2. Do subgroups of one-relator groups have decidable conjugacy prob-
lem?
     We emphasize that it is possible that Problem 1.8.2 has a negative answer, even
if all one-relator groups have decidable conjugacy problem.
     In spite of all the above negative results, in the setting of one-relator groups
there is nevertheless a case to be made for optimism. Collins [Col69d] proved that
the conjugacy problem can be undecidable in 11-relator groups, but the problem
remains open for k-relator groups for all 1 ď k ď 10. Furthermore, as mentioned in
§1.2, Dehn gave already in 1911 a solution for the conjugacy problem in the funda-
mental group π1 pΣg q of surface groups of genus g ą 1. In 1966, Lipschutz [Lip66]
generalized this to all cyclically pinched one-relator groups, i.e. amalgamated free
products Fn ˚Z Fm of free groups amalgamating a cyclic subgroup (of which surface
groups are central examples), to give the following theorem:
    Theorem 1.8.3 (Lipschutz, 1966 [Lip66]). Cyclically pinched one-relator groups
have decidable conjugacy problem.
     Lipschutz later extended these results to other cyclic amalgams of non-free
groups, see [Lip69, Lip75]. For a different solution to the conjugacy problem
in BSpm, nq with gcdpm, nq “ 1, see [AS74]. In 1980, Dyer [Dye80] extended
Theorem 1.8.3 by proving that cyclically pinched one-relator groups are conjugacy
separable. Larsen [Lar77] also proved that any one-relator group with non-trivial
centre has decidable conjugacy problem, using the structure theorem for one-relator
groups (see §1.7.2) with non-trivial centre. In this line, see also [Hur80]. Finally,
we have already mentioned that B. B. Newman proved (see §1.6.1) that every one-
relator group with torsion has decidable conjugacy problem.
     We remark that it might be tempting to consider the well-behaved class of
residually nilpotent one-relator groups as natural targets, since finitely generated
nilpotent groups have decidable conjugacy problem; indeed they are even conjugacy
separable, as proved in 1965 by Blackburn [Bla65]. However, unlike for the word
problem, it turns out that residually nilpotent groups need not have decidable
                         1.8. MORE ON DECISION PROBLEMS                               69


conjugacy problem, as proved in 1999 by G. Baumslag [Bau99]. Whether residually
nilpotent one-relator groups have decidable conjugacy problem also seems to be an
open problem.

       1.8.2. The isomorphism problem for torsion-free one-relator groups.
                                       The isomorphism problem is perhaps the most
                                       important as well as the most intractable
                                       problem in the theory of one-relator groups.

                                                 —G. Baumslag, 1971 [Bau71b, p. 75]
We have already outlined some of the results about the isomorphism problem for
one-relator groups with torsion in §1.6.3. In particular, we know that in this case,
the problem is decidable. By contrast, the isomorphism problem for torsion-free
one-relator groups remains wide open. In this section, we will discuss some par-
tial results, as well as some particularly difficult instances of the problem. Before
beginning, we mention that by far the two largest classes of one-relator groups for
which the isomorphism problem is decidable are one-relator groups with torsion (see
§1.6.3) and one-relator groups with non-trivial centre (see §1.7.2). Beyond this, few
truly general results are known: the quotation by G. Baumslag above remains as
true today as it was 50 years ago.
     Nevertheless, we can say some generalities. First, recall that two one-relator
groups xA | r “ 1y and xA | s “ 1y are said to be (Nielsen) equivalent if there
is an automorphism of FA mapping r to s. Of course, any two Nielsen equivalent
one-relator groups are isomorphic, but whether the converse holds was for some
time an open problem (Conjecture 1.3.5, sometimes called the Magnus Conjecture,
cf. e.g. [MKS66, p. 401]). We have seen that Whitehead proved the conjecture
true in 1936 in the case that the one-relator group is free (Theorem 1.4.2. However,
for general one-relator groups the conjecture was disproved in 1970 by Zieschang
[Zie70] and independently in 1971 by McCool & Pietrowski [MP71]. The examples
by McCool & Pietrowski come from torus knot groups. Let p, q P Z with |p|, |q| ą 1,
and let Tp,q “ xx, y | xp “ y q y. If p “ st ` 1 for some s, t P N with t ‰ 1, then the
authors proved that Tp,q – xa, b | b “ pap b´s qt y, but that these presentations are
not equivalent. Zieschang’s examples also come from Tp,q . Such counterexamples
produce finite classes of isomorphic but non-equivalent one-relator groups. This
was extended in 1976 by Brunner [Bru76] (cf. also [Bru80]), who proved that if
for r ě 0 we define
                                           r          r
(33)                   Gr “ xx, y | y ´1 x´2 yxy ´1 x2 y “ x2 y,
then Gr – Gs for all r, s ě 0, but no two such groups are Nielsen equivalent for
r ‰ s. This gives an even stronger failure of the Magnus Conjecture. Thus, it
would be interesting to know whether it is decidable if a given one-relator group
has infinitely many pairwise non-Nielsen-equivalent one-relator presentations. In
principle this can be decidable even if the isomorphism problem is undecidable,
and vice versa.
    The isomorphism problem, and the above failure of Magnus’ Conjecture, is
closely related to finding non-equivalent generating sets of one-relator groups, i.e.
generating sets lying in different T -systems. We discussed this subject in §1.6.3,
and note that there is also some literature on this subject in the torsion-free case
(beyond what is cited above). For example, Zieschang’s article [Zie70] is primarily
70                                      1. HISTORY


concerned with extending Nielsen’s method of finding a basis for a subgroup in
free groups to amalgamated free products. This is not possible in general, and
Zieschang was able to point out several obstructions to this together with sufficient
conditions on the amalgam for the method to work; such obstructions are also
the source of the failure of Magnus’ Conjecture. Similar conditions were given in
1978 by Peczynski & Reiwer [PR78] for HNN-extensions. The Nielsen method in
this broader context of amalgams and HNN-extensions was surveyed in [Ros79],
in which it is among other things used to give a proof of the Tits alternative for
SL2 pRq. Nielsen’s method was extended to all Bass–Serre theory in [Wei02]; see
also [FRS95] for a survey. We note that even Baumslag–Solitar groups BSpm, nq
(see §1.5.3) can also be elusive from the point of view of their generating sets.
In particular, the group BSp2, 3q “ xx, y | x´1 y 2 x “ y 3 y requires more than one
relation to define it in terms of the generators x and y 4 (in this connection, see also
[Pic71]). Dunwoody & Pietrowski [DP73] gave infinitely many non-equivalent
generating sets of the trefoil knot group xa, b | a2 “ b3 y. These generating sets do
not, however, have one-relator presentations.
     One can also consider the isomorphism problem with respect to particular
classes. For example, Whitehead’s Theorem (Theorem 1.4.2) implies that it is de-
cidable whether a one-relator group is free or not; thus, the isomorphism problem
is decidable for one-relator groups relative to free groups. Similarly, one can prove
using classical methods that the isomorphism problem is decidable for one-relator
groups relative to the class of cyclically pinched one-relator groups, i.e. cyclic amal-
gams of free groups Fn ˚Z Fm . This was proved for n “ m “ 2 and some constraints
on the amalgam by Rosenberger [Ros82], and finally in general by Rosenberger
[Ros94a] in 1994 (cf. also [Zie77] and [Col78a]). The strategy was to prove that
such groups have only finitely many T -systems of generators (as in §1.6.3), yielding
in particular the following result:
    Theorem 1.8.4 (Rosenberger 1994, [Ros82, Ros94a]). The isomorphism prob-
lem for one-relator groups is decidable relative to the class of cyclically pinched
one-relator groups.
    Another reason for the difficulty of the isomorphism problem in torsion-free one-
relator groups comes from parafree groups (discussed in §1.7.6). In this direction,
Chandler & Magnus [CM82] mention a particular family of parafree one-relator
groups:
(34)                 Gi,j “ xa, b, c | a “ rci , asrcj , bsy   pi, j P Nq
as being particularly difficult. Finding necessary and sufficient conditions on the
parameters i, j P Z for pairs of groups in the family Gi,j to be isomorphic is partic-
ularly difficult, and for decades, only sporadic isomorphism results were proved for
these groups (see e.g. [FRS95, FRS95], and [BCH04] for an overview). Finally,
the isomorphism problem for the family of groups tGi,j | i, j P Nu was solved in
2021 by Chen [Che21], using character varieties: it turns out that the group Gi,j
is uniquely determined by the pair pi, jq when i, j ą 0. In a similar vein, Me-
skin [Mes68, Mes69] in his PhD thesis studied the isomorphism problem for the
one-relator groups
                          xx1 , x2 , . . . , xt | xm1 m2       mt
                                                   1 x2 ¨ ¨ ¨ xn “ 1y
where t ě 2 and m1 , . . . , mt are arbitrary integers. These groups are parafree when
t ą 3 and gcdpm1 , . . . , mt q “ 1. Already within this class of one-relator groups
                            1.8. MORE ON DECISION PROBLEMS                               71


the isomorphism problem is difficult, but was eventually fully solved in 1975 by
Meskin [Mes75]: the integers m1 , . . . , mt , up to permutation, determine the group
uniquely.
    In practice, there are many useful group invariants that can distinguish a given
pair of torsion-free one-relator groups. The most basic invariant comes from the
abelianization. The second homology group also provides some information: it is a
consequence of Lyndon’s Identity Theorem (§1.4.2) that if G “ xA | r “ 1y, then
H2 pG, Zq “ Z if and only if r P rFA , FA s. In this case, when the second homology
group is non-trivial, there is much more to be said. This was explored by Horadam
[Hor81a, Hor81b] in 1981, who provides a simple and straightforward test for
non-isomorphism (later expanded on in [Hor82a, Hor82b]). For example, as in
[Hor81b, p. 197], if we let A “ tx1 , . . . , x4 u, and take
                 r1 “ rx3 , x4 srx1 , x2 srx1 , x24 srx3 , x2 srx2 , x4 srx3 , x4 s3 ,
                 r2 “ rx3 , x4 srx1 , x2 srx1 , x24 srx3 , x2 srx2 , x4 srx3 , x4 s2 ,
the only difference being the final exponent, and set Gi “ xA | ri “ 1y for i “ 1, 2,
then her methods prove that G1 fl G2 . The technique can also be used to show
that there exist infinitely many pairwise non-isomorphic one-relator groups of the
form xa, b | r “ 1y where r P rF2 , F2 s, which is not trivial to demonstrate otherwise.
We note that her methods were also strengthened later by Gromadzki [Gro85].
     There is more to be said on using finite quotients, and whether the set of all
finite quotients of a residually finite one-relator group uniquely determines the group
up to isomorphism (profinite rigidity of one-relator groups). In this direction, we
mention only [MS95], who prove that the groups BSp1, kq for k P Z are profinitely
rigid among all one-relator groups, i.e. if a residually finite one-relator group has
the same finite images as BSp1, kq for some k P Z, then G – BSp1, kq.
     1.8.3. More on the word problem. The first major result on one-relator
groups was the Freiheitssatz, and one of the first application of this result was the
decidability of the word problem in all one-relator groups. It is therefore remarkable
that since these results, proved almost a century ago, few general results on the
word problem in one-relator groups have appeared. For example, it remains an open
problem whether it can be decided in polynomial time. There also exist examples
of one-relator groups with very rapidly growing Dehn functions, but nevertheless
having word problem decidable in polynomial time [MUW11]. In this section, we
will discuss some open problems closely related to the word problem in one-relator
groups.
     One approach to better understand time complexity of the word problem in
one-relator groups is via finite complete rewriting systems. We give brief definitions
here, referring the reader to the monographs [Jan88, OZ91] for significantly more
thorough treatments. Let A be a finite set, A˚ the free monoid on A, and let
R Ď A˚ ˆ A˚ be a set of pairs of words, which we call rules. The set R of rules
induces a relation Ñ  Ý R on A˚ , where w1 Ñ Ý R w2 if and only if there exist words
         ˚
x, y P A and some rule r “ pu, vq P R such that w1 ” xuy and w2 ” xvy. The
reflexive and transitive closure of ÑÝ R is denoted ÑÝ ˚R . If there is no infinite chain
u1 ÑÝ R u2 ÑÝ R ¨ ¨ ¨ , then we say that R isterminating. For u P A˚ , if there exists
some v P A˚ such that u Ñ     Ý R v, then we say that u is reducible, and otherwise
irreducible. If R is a terminating rewriting system in which for every word w P A˚
there exists a unique irreducible w1 P A˚ such that w Ñ      Ý ˚R w1 , then we say that
72                                       1. HISTORY


R is complete (cf. [Jan88, Prop. 1.1.25]). Every rewriting system R induces an
equivalence relation Ø˚R , being the least equivalence relation containing R. In
fact, this is a congruence, and A˚ { Ø˚R , which we call the monoid associated to
R, is simply the monoid presented by MonxA | Ry. If a group G is isomorphic to
the monoid associated to a finite complete rewriting system, then we say that G
admits a finite complete rewriting system. Of course, any group admitting a finite
complete rewriting system has decidable word problem: simply rewrite any word
to an irreducible one by successively applying the rules of the system.
     Thus a finite complete rewriting system for a group is a particularly nice type
of monoid presentation for the group, giving rise to a simple solution to the word
problem. This leads to the following natural question:
Problem 1.8.5 (Otto & Kobayashi, 1997 [OK97, Q3]). Does every one-relator
group admit a finite complete rewriting system?
     If Problem 1.8.5 has a positive answer, then if the rules are concrete enough,
it may be possible to gain upper bounds on the complexity of the word problem
in any one-relator group. On the other hand, the property of admitting a finite
complete rewriting system does not, in general, entail any upper bound (beyond re-
cursiveness) on the complexity of the word problem [BO84]. Regarding a negative
answer to Problem 1.8.5, we first note that there exists a finitely presented monoid
xA | Ry which does not admit a finite complete rewriting system R Ď A˚ ˆ A˚ ,
but which does admit a finite complete rewriting system over some other gener-
ating set [Ott84]. Thus Problem 1.8.5 is intricately linked with the problem of
generating sets and alternate presentations of one-relator groups. Second, it seems
difficult to even approach the question of how one might give a negative answer
to Problem 1.8.5. However, it turns out that finite complete rewriting systems are
closely related to homological finiteness properties. Squier [Squ87] proved in 1987
that every monoid admitting a finite complete rewriting system has the homological
finiteness property FP3 pZq, later strengthened to FP8 pZq by Kobayashi [Kob90],
cf. also [Ani86, Gro90, SOK94]. Since there are finitely presented groups with
decidable word problem but which are not FP3 pZq [Sta63], there are finitely pre-
sented groups with decidable word problem but not admitting any finite complete
rewriting system. Whether the same is true for one-relator groups is thus the crux
of Problem 1.8.5. Lyndon’s Identity Theorem (Theorem 1.4.4) shows that all one-
relator groups are FP8 pZq, showing that any negative answer to Problem 1.8.5
would have to involve some fundamentally new technique. We also remark here
