that there exist finitely presented groups of cohomological dimension 2 with unde-
cidable word problem [CM99], and thus not admitting a finite complete rewriting
system.
     Another angle on the word problem comes from embedding one-relator groups
into finitely presented simple groups: any finitely presented simple group has de-
cidable word problem by Kuznetsov’s elegant algorithm [Kuz58].41 Recall that the
classical Boone–Higman Theorem [BH74] from 1974 states that a finitely gener-
ated group G has decidable word problem if and only if there exists a simple group
S and a finitely presented group K such that G ď K ď S. The Boone–Higman
   41If G “ xA | Ry is a finitely presented simple group and w P F , then to decide if w “ 1
                                                                     A
in G, run the following two procedures in parallel: (1) enumerate all words equal to 1 in G; (2)
enumerate the elements of Gw “ xA | R, w “ 1y. Then (1) terminates if w “ 1, and (2) terminates
if w ‰ 1 in G, since G being simple then implies that Gw is trivial.
                          1.8. MORE ON DECISION PROBLEMS                                73


Conjecture asks whether there is furthermore some such S and K exist with K “ S,
i.e. whether every finitely generated group with decidable word problem embeds in
some finitely presented simple group. This conjecture remains open, but it has
recently seen remarkable progress: Belk & Bleak [BB63] proved in 2023 that every
finitely generated hyperbolic group embeds in some finitely presented simple group.
Thus, for example, by combining this with the B. B. Newman Spelling Theorem
(§1.6.1) we have that every one-relator group with torsion embeds into a finitely
presented simple group. Whether this can be extended to all one-relator groups
seems a next natural step for the Boone–Higman Conjecture.
Problem 1.8.6. Can every one-relator group be embedded into a finitely presented
simple group?
     Extensions of the Boone–Higman Theorem to other decision problems also
exist, including for the order problem [BH75] and the conjugacy problem [Sac77],
giving rise to analogous questions for one-relator groups. Dual to the above problem
is the following classical problem:
Problem 1.8.7 (Baumslag, 1964 [Bau64, p. 391]). Can a one-relator group con-
tain a non-abelian simple subgroup?
    Finally, we make a brief remark on membership problems, being in some ways
a natural step following the word problem. The subgroup membership problem for
a group G “ xA | Ry asks, on input a set of words w1 , . . . , wn P FA and a word
w P FA , whether or not w belongs to the subgroup of G generated by w1 , . . . , wn .
Thus the word problem is simply the membership problem for the trivial subgroup.
Recall that Magnus (§1.3.3) proved that membership in Magnus subgroups of one-
relator groups is decidable. However, for arbitrary finitely generated subgroups,
deciding membership remains an unsolved problem in general:
Problem 1.8.8. Is the subgroup membership problem decidable in every one-relator
group?
     Any subgroup separable one-relator group has decidable subgroup membership
problem. Thus a large class of one-relator groups with decidable subgroup mem-
bership problem is formed by the one-relator groups with non-trivial centre (see
§1.7.2 and particularly Theorem 1.7.4). However, there are non-subgroup sepa-
rable one-relator groups which nevertheless have decidable subgroup membership
problem. e.g. the groups Hm,k “ xa, b | a´m bam “ bk y, with m, n P N, of Novikova
[Nov98]. Problem 1.8.8 can also be extended to instances of the submonoid mem-
bership problem, defined analogously. Here undecidability appears rather quickly,
and many one-relator groups have undecidable submonoid membership problem,
e.g. xa, b | rab, bas “ 1y or xa, b | ra2 , b2 s “ 1y. There are also positive one-relator
groups with undecidable submonoid membership problem [FGNB24]. However,
all currently known such examples arise from embedding the right-angled Artin
group ApP4 q, which has undecidable submonoid membership problem (see [LS08]).
Such subgroups obviously cannot appear as subgroups of hyperbolic groups. Thus
whether there is some hyperbolic one-relator group with undecidable submonoid
membership problem, and whether there is a one-relator group with torsion with
undecidable submonoid membership problem, both remain open problems, being
Problems 20.68 & 20.69, respectively, in the Kourovka Notebook [KM24].
74                                    1. HISTORY


     1.8.4. Two-relator groups.
                                       So my survey ends fittingly, as it began, with the
                                       work of Wilhelm Magnus.

                                                         —G. Baumslag [Bau86, p. 55]
This survey has told the story of one defining relation. What question could now be
more natural than: what about two-relator groups? Unfortunately, here much less is
known. The question of extending the methods of one-relator groups to two relators
dates back to the very foundations of the former subject, being present already in
an appendix to Magnus’ 1930 article [Mag30, §8]. Very little has been produced in
way of results for two-relator groups qua two-relator groups. Even the problem of
classifying all finite two-relator groups is still a mystery. Certainly, any finite two-
relator group must by basic linear algebra also be generated by two elements, and
thus admit a balanced (i.e. deficiency zero) presentation. It follows that any finite
two-relator group G must satisfy H2 pG, Zq “ 0. Beyond such generalities, the class
of finite two-relator groups seems, at present, far too broad to understand in its
entirety, and contains groups such as SLp2, 5q ˆ SLp2, 25q, as proved in [CRK` 86],
and much computational work has been directed towards finding two-generator,
two-relator presentations for similar groups, see e.g. [CRK` 86, RW89]. Even the
finiteness problem for two-relator groups, i.e. the isomorphism problem for two-
relator groups with respect to the class of finite two-relator groups, is at present
wide open.
     The word problem, conjugacy problem, and isomorphism problem are all open
for two-relator groups. The latter two are less surprising, since they also remain
open for one-relator group, and currently known methods for encoding undecidable
problems in groups tend to produce a relatively large number of relators. Indeed, as
for the word problem, as discussed in §1.4.4 the currently smallest known number
of defining relators for a group with undecidable word problem is 12, as proved
by Borisov [Bor69]. His proof (simplified by Collins [Col72]) uses the fact that
the word problem in any n-relation monoid can be encoded into an pn ` 9q-relator
group, and Matiyasevich [Mat67] gave an example of a three-relation monoid with
undecidable word problem. It seems very plausible, but remains an open problem,
that there is a two-relation monoid with undecidable word problem; and recent
trends (see e.g. [Gra20b, FGNB24]) have indicated that there may even be a one-
relation monoid with undecidable word problem (see [NB21b] for an introduction
to this problem). Nevertheless, we see that Borisov’s method would only yield a 11
resp. 10-relator group with undecidable word problem if these undecidability results
would hold, respectively. Thus, if the word problem is undecidable in two-relator
groups, then to prove it would require some fundamentally new way of encoding
undecidability into groups. If it is decidable, then some fundamentally new way
of solving word problems must be developed, perhaps by extending the Magnus
hierarchy to this setting. In either case, much exciting mathematics will surely
follow!
                                   CHAPTER 2


                             Recent progress

                                2.1. Introduction
    The origins of combinatorial group theory are often identified with Dehn’s
solutions to the word and conjugacy problem for fundamental groups of closed
hyperbolic surfaces. Dehn presented two approaches, one utilising the geometry of
the hyperbolic plane H2 [Deh11] and another abstracting the geometric properties
to purely combinatorial properties of their one-relator presentations [Deh12]:
                       C                         ˇ           G
                                                 ˇź g
                  Σg “ a1 , b1 , . . . , ag , bg ˇ rai , bi s , g ě 2.
                                                 ˇ
                                                 ˇ i“1

Generalisations of Dehn’s ideas would eventually lead to the very fruitful study of
small cancellation groups and, later, hyperbolic groups.
    The theory of amalgamated free products, developed by Schreier in the decade
that followed [Sch27], provided a third method of solving the word problem for
surface groups. This algebraic method was picked up by Dehn’s doctoral student
Magnus who used it to investigate the much more general class of one-relator groups;
that is, groups admitting a presentation
                                    G “ xS | wy
with a single defining relation. Magnus made two fundamental initial contributions
to the theory by proving the Frieheitssatz in 1930 [Mag30] and solving the word
problem for all one-relator groups in 1932 [Mag32]. These results initiated the
systematic study of one-relator groups which has continued to develop and mature
over the last century. After Magnus’ foundational work, one-relator groups quickly
became a test-piece for new conjectures in group theory. Indeed, one-relator groups
exhibit a wide variety of behaviours so that few properties hold for the whole class
and characterising one-relator groups with a certain property is often a nuanced
problem. This has the corollary that even fundamental problems like Dehn’s conju-
gacy and isomorphism problems are very difficult to solve for all one-relator groups
and in fact remain wide open. On top of this, not a single theorem is known to
hold for all two-relator groups that does not already hold for all finitely presented
groups. It is therefore remarkable that one-relator groups admit a cohesive theory
at all and even more so that it is sufficiently deep and developed that one can hope
to approach most difficult problems.
     Until the 80s, group theory remained a mostly algebraic and combinatorial sub-
ject. The focus in group theory then shifted towards more geometrically flavoured
problems when Gromov introduced hyperbolic groups in [Gro87]. By the very
nature of their definition, one-relator groups do not admit any obvious intrinsic
geometry and so this shift initially had little impact on the theory of one-relator
                                         75
76                              2. RECENT PROGRESS


groups. Indeed, the geometry of one-relator groups has proven to be surprisingly
complicated, resisting any form of decisive classification for some time.
     On the other hand, one-relator groups have turned out to be very amenable for
study via topological and homological means. The recent developments in CATp0q
cube complexes, 2-complexes with non-positive immersions and L2 -invariants have
all had a significant impact on one-relator groups, resulting in new insights and
resolutions of old conjectures that had seen little progress for many years. This
includes, but is not limited to, the residual finiteness conjecture (posed by Baumslag
in [Bau67c] and solved by Wise in [Wis21]), the coherence conjecture (posed
by Baumslag in [Bau74b] and solved by Jaikin-Zapirain and the first author in
[JZL23]) and the virtually free-by-cyclic conjecture (posed by Baumslag in [Bau86]
and solved by Kielak and the first author in [KL24]).
     The purpose of this chapter is to survey the current state of the art in the
theory of one-relator groups with a particular emphasis on homological, topological
and geometric aspects of one-relator groups, topics which, as mentioned above,
have seen considerable advancement in the last decade. Some of the content of this
survey is based on work of the first author, but the majority is based on recent
work of Andrei Jaikin-Zapirain, Dawid Kielak, Lars Louder, Henry Wilton, Daniel
Wise and many others, without whom this article would not warrant writing.
     We aim to complement other pre-existing treatments of one-relator groups
which are more classical and combinatorial in nature, see [Bau64, Bau74b, Bau86,
LS01, MKS04, BFR19] for example. We include proofs of a few selected results,
but mostly content ourselves with sketch proofs and ideas. Throughout, we also
present open problems and directions for future research. As such, we hope this
document can serve both as an invitation to the theory of one-relator groups and
as a point of reference for anyone wishing to contribute and expand on the topics
presented here.
     Many results and questions we discuss here in reality apply (or can be gener-
alised) to broader classes of groups which include:
       ‚ One-relator products of groups (possibly with extra conditions on the
         factors),
       ‚ Fundamental groups of 2-complexes with non-positive immersions,
       ‚ Groups of cohomological dimension two with vanishing second L2 -Betti
         number.
Indeed, often results for these classes of groups arise from direct generalisations
of arguments used to prove the same result for the simplest non-trivial case in the
corresponding class, one-relator groups. For the sake of simplicity, in this article we
mostly only state the versions of results for one-relator groups, pointing the reader
towards references for further information.

    2.1.1. A brief description of the content. In Section 2.2 we begin by dis-
cussing Magnus’ main tool for studying one-relator groups, the Magnus hierarchy.
We discuss subsequent improvements to the hierarchy and cover a topological ver-
sion using two-complexes in detail, as developed by the first author in [Lin22a].
The treatment includes several examples and a somewhat complete proof of the
Freiheitssatz.
    In Section 2.3 we cover what is known about splittings of one-relator groups.
We begin with an in depth analysis of the action of a one-relator group on the
                2.2. ONE-RELATOR SPLITTINGS AND THE HIERARCHY                         77


Bass–Serre tree associated with the splittings arising from its hierarchy. We then
provide a sufficient condition for when a one-relator group is free-by-cyclic which
we conjecture to also be necessary. We then consider the more specific case of
when a one-relator group is {finitely generated free}-by-cyclic, which was com-
pletely determined by Moldavanskiı̆ and later by Brown. Finally, we cover the
work of Friedl–Tillmann and Henneke–Kielak on polytopes and splitting complexi-
ties for one-relator groups, finishing with some open problems on JSJ and amalgam
decompositions.
     In Section 2.4 we cover (co)homological topics and properties of the group ring
of one-relator groups. We discuss Lyndon’s identity theorem and the implications
this has for the (co)homology of a one-relator group. We then cover recent re-
sults on groups with cyclic relation module and connections with the relation gap
and relation lifting problems. We also cover L2 -invariants, the resolution of the
strong Atiyah conjecture for one-relator groups by Jaikin-Zapirain–López-Álvarez
and embeddings of group rings of torsion-free one-relator groups into division rings.
Finally, we sketch a proof of the fact that the rational group ring of a one-relator
group is coherent.
     In Section 2.5 we cover properties of subgroups of one-relator groups. We
begin by covering the non-positive immersions property as introduced by Wise. We
then cover work of Louder–Wilton who characterise when a one-relator group has
the stronger property of negative immersions. This then leads us to connections
between the k-freeness property and primitivity rank of elements of the free group.
Combining these results with some homological properties, we then sketch a proof
of the fact that all one-relator groups are coherent, covering also a strengthening of
this theorem for the subclass of 2-free one-relator groups. Finally, we cover what
is known about when a one-relator group is virtually free-by-cyclic, Mel’nikov’s
conjecture, surface subgroups and subgroups of infinite index.
     In Section 2.6 we cover geometric properties of one-relator groups. We discuss
Gersten’s conjecture on the hyperbolicity of one-relator groups, discuss a method
of proving a one-relator group is hyperbolic which leads to a reduction of Ger-
sten’s conjecture to the case of primitive extension groups and mention results
that are known to hold for hyperbolic one-relator groups. Then, we cover CATp0q
and cubulated one-relator groups, demonstrating further the difficulty of geomet-
ric characterisations of one-relator groups with some pathological examples due to
Gardam–Woodhouse. Finally, we discuss Wise’s quasi-convex hierarchies, Wise’s
solution of the residual finiteness conjecture and an extension to the class of 2-free
one-relator groups.
     In Section 2.7 we cover residual properties such as residual solvability, residual
nilpotence, parafree groups and profinite completions.
     In Section 2.8 we cover the work that has been carried out on the complexity
of the word problem for one-relator groups, the conjugacy problem, various mem-
bership problems, the normal root problem, generating sets of one-relator groups
and the isomorphism problem.

                2.2. One-relator splittings and the hierarchy
    2.2.1. The Magnus–Moldavanskiı̆–Masters hierarchy. If S is a set, we
shall write S ˘1 to denote S \ S ´1 , the union of S with the set of its formal inverses
and by F pSq the free group freely generated by the set S. A one-relator group is a
78                              2. RECENT PROGRESS


group of the form
                                   G “ F {xxwyy
where F is a free group and where xxwyy denotes the normal closure of the single
element w P F . We will always be assuming that w ‰ 1. The most general tool
used to study one relator groups is known as the Magnus hierarchy, developed by
Magnus in his thesis [Mag30] in order to prove the Freiheitssatz.
    Theorem 2.2.1 (Freiheitssatz). Let F be a free group over a generating set
S and let w P F be a cyclically reduced element. If T Ă S is a subset such that
w R xT y, then the natural map
                                F pT q Ñ G “ F {xxwyy
is injective.
     A free subgroup of a one-relator group arising from Theorem 2.2.1 is called
a Magnus subgroup. The reader is directed to Section 1.3 for more on the Frei-
heitssatz.
     The idea of the original proof of Theorem 2.2.1 is to find a suitable epimorphism
G Ñ Z in which the kernel can be decomposed as an infinite amalgamated free
product of one-relator groups with strictly shorter relator length and then use the
explicit structure of this decomposition, together with induction, to prove that the
subset of generators we chose freely generate a free subgroup. There is a caveat
with this sketch; such an epimorphism exists only when a generator appears with
exponent sum zero in the relator, and this cannot always be the case. To get
around this, Magnus embeds the one-relator group into another suitably chosen
one-relator group in which some generator does appear with exponent sum zero.
Then some technical arguments allows one to show that if the Freiheitssatz holds
for the larger one-relator group, then it holds for the original one-relator group.
Several proofs of Theorem 2.2.1 have appeared that do not (explicitly) make use of
Magnus’ hierarchy. See, for instance, [Lyn72] and [LW22].
     The ideas in Magnus’ proof are extremely powerful as they subsequently allowed
Magnus to prove several strong results on the structure of one-relator groups, such
as the decidability of the word problem [Mag32]. As is often the case, in order to
make an inductive argument work, one needs to prove something stronger than the
desired statement. Indeed, in order to solve the word problem in HNN-extensions
or amalgamated free products, one needs both decidability of the word problem in
the base groups as well as decidability of the membership problem in the associated
subgroups. Thus, the actual statement that Magnus proved inductively in order to
solve the word problem for one-relator groups was that the membership problem
for any Magnus subgroup is decidable. Most other results on one-relator groups
follow a similar structure, usually one proves that the result is true ‘relative’ (in an
appropriate sense) to Magnus subgroups by induction on the length of a hierarchy,
highlighting the importance of Magnus subgroups.
     Over the many years that have passed since Magnus’ first paper, there have
been several improvements to Magnus’ original version of the hierarchy, the most
well known being due to Moldavanskiı̆ [Mol67] which utilises HNN-extensions. Ex-
plicitly, Moldavanskiı̆ noticed that the amalgamated product decompositions of the
kernel of some epimorphism G Ñ Z that arose in Magnus’ work could be bundled
together to obtain a HNN-decomposition G – H˚ψ , with the original epimorphism
being recoverd by quotienting by the normal closure of H. In particular, if G is a
                2.2. ONE-RELATOR SPLITTINGS AND THE HIERARCHY                        79


one-relator group, then Moldavanskiı̆ proved that there is a diagram of one-relator
groups
                               G “ G0          G10


                                    G1            G11


                                    ¨¨¨           ¨¨¨


                                    GN
where GN is a free product of a free group and a (possibly trivial) finite cyclic
group and where G1i´1 – Gi ˚ψi where ψi identifies a pair of Magnus subgroups of
Gi . All embeddings and splittings can also be described very explicitly in terms
of the presentations. We shall not do this here, we direct the reader to [LS01] for
more details. We remark that HNN-extensions had been introduced almost two
decades after [Mag32]. A variation of this version of the hierarchy was also proven
by Mihalik–Tschantz [MT92] who proved that all the embeddings above can be
replaced with embeddings as (proper) free factors.
     Expressing the Magnus hierarchy with HNN-extensions simplifies many of Mag-
nus’ original arguments; indeed, McCool–Schupp then used Moldavanskiı̆’s improve-
ment to reprove many of Magnus’ original results on one-relator groups in a short
paper [MS73].
     Another version of the hierarchy that is worth mentioning is due to Masters.
In an unpublished prepreint, Masters [Mas06] showed that one can do away with
the embedding step in the hierarchy and obtained the following theorem.
    Theorem 2.2.2 (The Algebraic Hierarchy). Let F be a finitely free generated
group, w P F cyclically reduced and consider the one-relator group G “ F {xxwyy.
There is sequence of finitely generated subgroups FN ď . . . ď F1 ď F0 “ F contain-
ing w and isomorphisms ψi : Ai Ñ Bi between Magnus subgroups of Gi “ Fi {xxwi yy
for each 1 ď i ď N , such that FN – Z and
                                    Gi´1 – Gi ˚ψi
for each 0 ă i ď N .
    The splittings that arise in this version are the same as those from the Magnus–
Moldavanskiı̆ hierarchy. However, in order to find them, Masters modifies the
original presentation via Nielsen moves until a presentation with relator having
exponent sum zero on some generator appears. This idea was already present in
previous articles, the issue in using it to obtain a hierarchy had previously been
that there was no argument to overcome the fact that the relator might get longer
under this modification. The novelty of Masters proof is to show that, regardless,
continuing to split one eventually reaches a free product of a free group with a finite
cyclic group.
    Some classical results follow directly from this version of the Magnus hierarchy,
combined with general theory of HNN-extensions. However, the splittings are not
given so explicitly in terms of the original presentation of the one-relator group
making it more difficult to apply than the Magnus–Moldavanskiı̆ hierarchy.
80                              2. RECENT PROGRESS


     Yet another version of the hierarchy is provided by the first author in [Lin22a].
This hierarchy is phrased and proven in terms of 2-complexes. It both eliminates the
embedding step from the Magnus–Moldavanskiı̆ hierarchy and expresses the HNN-
splittings very explicitly. Given that in this article we shall mostly be studying
one-relator groups via 2-complexes, we shall cover this hierarchy in much more
detail now. Moreover, this version implies Theorem 2.2.2.
      2.2.2. The topological hierarchy and some classical results. Before
reaching the topological hierarchy statement, let us first set up some notation and
definitions.
      A graph is a 1-dimensional CW-complex. A combinatorial map of graphs is
one that sends 0-cells to 0-cells and open 1-cells homeomorphically to open 1-
cells. An immersion of graphs is a combinatorial map that is locally injective,
which we denote by í. If λ : S 1 í Λ is an immersion of a cycle, denote by
degpλq the maximal degree of a covering map S 1 í S 1 that λ factors through.
Note that degpλq “ 1 if and only if λ˚ p1q is not a proper power in π1 pΛq, where
here 1 P Zř“ π1 pS 1 q is a generator. If λ “ \i λi where λi : S 1 í Λ, then write
degpλq “ i degpλi q. Here we are implicitly Ů putting a combinatorial structure on
S 1 . When λ is an immersion of cycles S “ S 1 , we will write |λ| to denote the
number of 1-cells in S.
      A 2-complex will be used throughout to mean a two-dimensional CW-complex
X in which all attaching maps of 2-cells are combinatorial immersions. We will
usually write X “ pΛ, λq where Λ “ X p1q is the 1-skeleton and where λ : S “
   S 1 í Λ is the attaching map for the 2-cells in X.
Ů
      Our maps between 2-complexes will always be assumed to be combinatorial,
that is, they will send open n-cells homeomorphically to n-cells. An immersion of
2-complexes is a locally injective combinatorial map which we shall usually use the
arrow í to denote. If Z í X is an immersion of 2-complexes, it will be useful to
bear in mind that there is an induced diagram of immersions
                                    SΓ ì      SΛ
                                    ì



                                               ì




                                    Γ ì        Λ
where the map SΓ í SΛ is a homeomorphism on each component.
    A natural 2-complex to associate to a one-relator group is its presentation
complex. That is, the 2-complex with a single 0-cell, a 1-cell for each generator
and a single 2-cell whose attaching map spells out the relator. However, for the
statement and proofs of results in this article it will be more useful to consider
arbitrary 2-complexes with a single 2-cell.
Definition 2.2.3. A connected 2-complex X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex if
λ is an immersion of a single cycle.
    In direct analogy with the definition of Magnus subgroups, we define Magnus
subgraphs.
Definition 2.2.4. If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex, say a connected subgraph
Γ Ă Λ is a Magnus subgraph if Γ does not support λ.
   The aim of the next sections is to sketch a proof of topological versions of
Magnus’ original results. The Freiheitssatz has an obvious translation.
                2.2. ONE-RELATOR SPLITTINGS AND THE HIERARCHY                     81


   Theorem 2.2.5 (Topological Freiheitssatz). If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator
complex and Γ Ă Λ is a Magnus subgraph, then the map on π1 induced by inclusion
                                  π1 pΓq Ñ π1 pXq
is injective.
     The Magnus hierarchy and variations do not have an immediate useful trans-
lation to the setting of 2-complexes. However, in [Lin22a], the following theorem
is proven. The statement is heavily influenced by Howie’s towers [How81].
     Theorem 2.2.6 (The Topological Hierarchy). If X “ pΛ, λq is a finite one-
relator complex, then there exists a sequence of immersions
                           XN í . . . í X1 í X0 “ X.
such that
     (1) For all 0 ď i ď N , Xi is a finite one-relator complex.
     (2) π1 pXN q – Z{nZ where n “ degpλq.
     (3) For all 1 ď i ď N , we have
                              π1 pXi´1 q – π1 pXi q˚ψi
          where ψi is induced by an isomorphism between Magnus subgraphs of Xi .
Remark 2.2.7. One could drop the assumption that X be finite in Theorem 2.2.6
at the expense of removing the word ‘finite’ from the first conclusion and modifying
the second conclusion to ‘π1 pXN q – F ˚ Z{nZ for some free group F and where
n “ degpλq’. However, if X is a one-relator complex, all the interesting information
about π1 pXq can be derived by only looking at the smallest one-relator subcomplex,
which is necessarily finite.
     We now note some classical results which were proved using Magnus origi-
nal hierarchy for one-relator groups. The proofs can all be simplified somewhat
by using Theorem 2.2.6 and the explicit description of the splittings provided by
Theorem 2.2.14.
     The first result is known as the Magnus property. A group G has the Magnus
property if for any two elements w, v P G, we have xxwyy “ xxvyy if and only if w
is conjugate to v or v ´1 . Magnus proved in [Mag30] that free groups have the
Magnus property. Below is the restatement in terms of one-relator complexes.
     Theorem 2.2.8 (Magnus property). Let X “ pΛ, λ1 q and Y “ pΛ, λ2 q be two
one-relator complexes such that the identity map on π1 pΛq induces an isomorphism
π1 pXq Ñ π1 pY q. Then the identity map on Λ extends to an isomorphism of one-
relator complexes X Ñ Y .
    Cockcroft characterised when a one-relator complex is aspherical in [Coc54].
His proof made use of Lyndon’s identity theorem [Lyn50], which we shall cover in
Section 2.4.1.
     Theorem 2.2.9 (Asphericity). If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex, then X
is aspherical if and only if degpλq “ 1.
    Karrass–Magnus–Solitar characterised which elements in a one-relator group
have finite order in [KMS60]. The criterion is very easily checked by inspecting
the relator.
82                              2. RECENT PROGRESS


    Theorem 2.2.10 (Torsion). If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex, then π1 pXq
has torsion if and only if degpλq ě 2. Moreover, every element of finite order in
π1 pXq is conjugate to a root of λ˚ p1q.
     Theorem 2.2.10 follows from Theorem 2.2.6 by noting that an element of finite
order acting on a tree (the Bass–Serre tree of the splittings) must have a fixed point.
     Theorem 2.2.10 can be stated in terms of presentations as follows. Let F be a
free group, w P F not a proper power and n ě 1 an integer. Then G “ F {xxwn yy
has torsion if and only if n ě 2. Moreover, all elements of finite order in G are
conjugate into the subgroup xwy.
     To conclude this section, we state a result due to Howie [How82] and, indepen-
dently, Brodskiı̆ [Bro84]. Recall that a group is locally indicable if every non-trivial
finitely generated subgroup admits an epimorphism to Z. The proof of this theorem
is not carried out using the Magnus hierarchy, rather using the method of towers.
    Theorem 2.2.11 (Local indicability). If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex
with degpλq “ 1, then π1 pXq is locally indicable.
    2.2.3. One-relator splittings. Before diving into the details of Theorem 2.2.6,
we now explain further where exactly these splittings come from. Firstly, we need
a notion of complexity. This notion is related to the relator length as used by Mag-
nus, and is also closely related to a notion of complexity used by Howie in [How82].
We are following [Lin22a] in this section and those that follow.
Definition 2.2.12. If λ : S 1 í Λ is an immersed cycle, denote by
                                     |λ|    ˇ         ˇ
                            cpλq “        ´ ˇImpλqp0q ˇ
                                            ˇ         ˇ
                                   degpλq
the complexity of λ. If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex, the complexity of X is
the tuple
                              cpXq “ pcpλq, ´χpXqq
which is given the dictionary order.
Remark 2.2.13. If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex, then cpλq “ 0 if and only
if λ factors through an embedded cycle S 1 ãÑ Λ. In particular, if cpλq “ 0, then
π1 pXq – F ˚ Z{nZ where F is a free group and n “ degpλq. Moreover, cpXq reaches
its minimal value p0, 0q if and only if π1 pXq – Z{nZ.
    The following theorem explicitly identifies the splittings that arise in Theo-
rem 2.2.6. The statement is rather technical, but we will show with some examples
how exactly one can apply it to compute hierarchies. Recall that a Z-cover is a
covering space ρ : Y Ñ X such that Deckpρq – Z. We shall be using the notion of
graphs of spaces, as introduced by Scott–Wall [SW79].

complex and let ρ : Y Ñ X be a connected Z-cover with t P Deckpρq a generator.
       ‚ (Existence of a one-relator Z-domain) There exists a finite connected sub-

          (1) Z is one-relator and Z p1q supports the attaching map of precisely one
              2-cellŤin Y .

              of Z.
          (4) For any i ą 0, we have Z X ti pZq Ă Z X ti´1 pZq.
       ‚ (Graph of spaces/groups decomposition) If Z Ă Y is any one-relator Z-

   The first bullet point is [Lin22a, Proposition 4.9], the second is [Lin22a,
Proposition 4.3] and the third is [Lin22a, Proposition 4.11].
Definition 2.2.15. If X is a one-relator complex, we will call a splitting π1 pXq –
π1 pZq˚ψ as arising from Theorem 2.2.14 a one-relator splitting of π1 pXq.
Definition 2.2.16. If X is a one-relator complex, a one-relator tower for X is a
sequence of immersions of one-relator complexes
                            XN í . . . í X1 í X0 “ X
such that for each i, Xi`1 is obtained from Xi as in Theorem 2.2.14.
    If X is a 2-complex, the Z-covers of X are in bijection with non-trivial ho-
momorphisms π1 pXq Ñ Z, or rather, with non-zero elements of H 1 pX, Zq. The
connected Z-covers are in bijection with epimorphisms π1 pXq ↠ Z, or rather,
with basis elements of H 1 pX, Zq. We will make this bijection more explicit in
Section 2.2.6. When X is a one-relator complex, such epimorphisms exist if and
only if π1 pXq is not isomorphic to a finite cyclic group. In particular, at least one
epimorphism exists precisely when cpXq ą p0, 0q. Theorem 2.2.6 now follows by
Theorem 2.2.14 and induction.
     2.2.4. Explicit examples of the topological hierarchy. Our first exam-
ple will be the example which was the inspiration for Theorem 2.2.14. The pro-
totypical one-relator complex is a closed surface. So let X be a closed surface,
let C Ă X be a non-separating simple closed curve and consider the epimorphism
π1 pXq Ñ H1 pX, Zq Ñ Z ¨ rCs, where here rCs denotes the homology class of C in
H1 pX, Zq. We can always modify the 2-complex structure of X so that C actually
lies in the 1-skeleton of X. Then if ρ : Y Ñ X is the Z-cover associated with this
homomorphism, one can take Z Ă Y from Theorem 2.2.14 to be the closure of
ρ´1 pX ´ Cq. Here Z is a surface with two boundary components corresponding to
two distinct lifts of C. If t P Deckpρq is a generator, then Z0 “ t´1 pZq X Z and
Z1 “ Z X tpZq are precisely these two lifts. The space X from Theorem 2.2.14
is then obtained from Z by attaching a cylinder connecting these two boundary
components and expresses π1 pXq as a HNN-extension over an infinite cyclic group.

Remark 2.2.17. If G “ F pSq{xxwyy is a one-relator group in which some generator
t P S appears with exponent sum zero, there is an epimorphism ϕ : G Ñ Z defined
by sending each generator different to t to zero and by sending t to one. Taking X
to be the presentation complex for G, the Z-cover associated with ϕ has 1-skeleton
a biinfinite line consisting of the lifts of 1-cells corresponding to t and at each 0-cell
there is a loop for every generator in S ´ ttu. The one-relator splitting arising from
a Z-domain of this cover as in Theorem 2.2.14 is precisely the type of HNN-splitting
that Moldavanskiı̆ finds in [Mol67].
     2.2.5. Sketch of the proof of Theorem 2.2.14. We now sketch out the
main ideas of the proof of Theorem 2.2.14. There are essentially three parts to the
proof with each part corresponding to the three points in Theorem 2.2.14.
     2.2.5.1. Part one: HNN-decompositions from Z-domains. The first step in-
volves showing that certain kinds of subcomplexes of Z-covers give rise to HNN-
splittings.
Definition 2.2.18. Let ρ : Y Ñ X be a Z-cover of 2-complexes and let t : Y Ñ Y
be a generator of Deckpρq “ Z. A subcomplex Z Ă Y is a Z-domain if the following
holds:
     (1) The Z-translates of Z cover all of Y . That is, Y “ iPZ ti pZq.
                                                             Ť
     (2) Z X tpZq is connected and non-empty.
     (3) For each i ą 0, we have that Z X ti pZq Ă Z X ti´1 pZq.
     The following result is the motivation behind the definition of Z-domains.
Proposition 2.2.19. Let ρ : Y Ñ X be a Z-cover of 2-complexes, let t : Y Ñ Y be
a generator of Deckpρq, let Z Ă Y be a Z-domain and denote by Z0 “ t´1 pZq X Z
and ι : Z0 ãÑ Z the inclusion. If
        X “ Z \ pZ0 ˆ r´1, 1sq {tιpzq „ pz, ´1q, pt ˝ ιqpzq „ pz, 1q, z P Z0 u,
then the map
                                          h: X Ñ X
given by ρ when restricted to Z or Z0 ˆ tiu for all i P p´1, 1q, is a homotopy
equivalence.
    Moreover, if ι˚ and pt ˝ ιq˚ are injective, then
                                     π1 pXq – π1 pZq˚ψ
where
                  ψ “ pι ˝ t ˝ ι´1 q˚ : ι˚ pπ1 pZ0 qq Ñ pt ˝ ιq˚ pπ1 pZ0 qq.
    Proof. The map h is surjective by property (1) of Z-domains. Moreover,
property 2 says that Z0 is connected and non-empty. Finally, property (3) implies
that the fibers h´1 pxq are all connected (possibly infinite) lines. In particular, this
                2.2. ONE-RELATOR SPLITTINGS AND THE HIERARCHY                        87


implies that h is a homotopy equivalence. See [Lin22a, Proposition 3.2] for further
details and a more general result.
    If the homomorphisms ι˚ , pt ˝ ιq˚ are injective, then X is a graph of spaces with
a single vertex space Z and a single edge space Z0 ˆ r´1, 1s, attached to Z via ι
and t ˝ ι. Hence, we have π1 pX q – π1 pZq˚ψ . Since h is a homotopy equivalence, we
also have π1 pXq – π1 pZq˚ψ as claimed.                                             □
     2.2.5.2. Part two: existence of one-relator Z-domains. The second step involves
proving that Z-domains always exist for Z-covers of one-relator complexes and that
they may additionally be chosen so that they only support a single attaching map
of a 2-cell. Then the Freiheitssatz combined with Proposition 2.2.19 will allow us
to find the desired splitting.
Proposition 2.2.20. [Lin22a, Lemma 4.7] If ρ : Y Ñ Λ is a connected Z-cover
of 1-complexes, then there exists a Z-domain Z Ă Y . Moreover, if Z is minimal
under inclusion of Z-domains, then Z X tpZq is a tree and, if Λ is finite, Z is finite.
    From Proposition 2.2.20 it is not hard to prove the following.
Corollary 2.2.21. If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex and ρ : Y Ñ X is a
Z-cover, then there exists a Z-domain Z Ă Y , finite if X is finite, which supports
precisely one lift of λ.
    Proof. Let Γ1 Ă Y p1q be a Z-domain for the Z-cover Y p1q Ñ X p1q . Let k ě 0
be the smallest integer such that
                                         k
                                         ď
                                    Γ“         tk pΓ1 q
                                         i“0
supports some lift of λ. Let Z Ă Y be the one-relator subcomplex obtained from
Γ by attaching a 2-cell along a lift of λ. Since Γ1 was a Z-domain for Y p1q Ñ X p1q ,
the three properties (1), (2) and (3) required of a Z-domain holds for Z. Since Γ1 is
finite when Λ is, Z is finite when X is. Furthermore, if Z does not contain precisely
one lift of an attaching map of some 2-cell in X, this would imply that t´1 pZq X Z
supports a lift of λ, contradicting our choice of Γ. Thus, Z supports precisely one
lift of λ.                                                                         □
     It will sometimes be useful to have a refinement of Corollary 2.2.21 specifically
for one-relator complexes. This statement is [Lin24b, Proposition 4.4].
Proposition 2.2.22. Let X “ pΛ, λq be a one-relator complex with cpλq ą 0 and
let e Ă X be a 1-cell traversed by λ. There exists a Z-cover ρ : Y Ñ X and a one-
relator Z-domain pΛZ , λZ q “ Z Ă Y with cpλZ q ă cpλq such that ρ´1 pΛ ´ eq Ă Z
and the following holds. If ẽ Ă Z is a lift of e to Z and m, M P Z are, respectively,
the minimal and maximal integers such that tm pẽq Ă Z and tM pẽq Ă Z, then λZ
traverses both tm pẽq and tM pẽq.
    For an arbitrary Z-cover of a one-relator complex ρ : Y Ñ X, by Corollary 2.2.21
we know that there is some 1-cell e Ă X and a one-relator Z-domain Z Ă Y such
that the attaching map for the 2-cell in Z traverses the minimal lift of e and the
maximal lift. The advantage of Proposition 2.2.22 is that it allows us to first choose
a 1-cell in X and then procure a Z-cover and a Z-domain with these properties.
This version is particularly useful when generalising results on one-relator groups
to the setting of one-relator products of locally indicable groups.
88                               2. RECENT PROGRESS


Remark 2.2.23. One should compare Proposition 2.2.22 with the usual method of
finding a splitting of a one-relator group over a one-relator subgroup, as described,
for example, in [LS01].
Remark 2.2.24. With almost the same proof, Corollary 2.2.21 can easily be gen-
eralised to arbitrary finite 2-complexes X. Then using Proposition 2.2.19 one can
prove that for any finitely presented group G and any epimorphism ϕ : G Ñ Z, there
is an element t P G such that ϕptq “ 1, a finitely generated subgroup H ď kerpϕq
                                               ´1
and two finitely generated subgroups A “ H t X H, B “ H X H t of H such that
                                       G – H˚ψ
where ψ : A Ñ B is the isomorphism induced by conjugation by t. In fact, one only
needs G to have type FP2 pRq for some ring R. This is proven by Bieri–Strebel in
[BS78, Theorem A]. We will state a much more general, but much less explicit,
result in Section 2.5.4.
    2.2.5.3. Part three: decrease of complexity. The final step involves showing that
the complexity of the one-relator Z-domain from Corollary 2.2.21 goes down.
Lemma 2.2.25. If Z “ pΛZ , λZ q and X “ pΛX , λX q are one-relator complexes
and σ : Z í X is an immersion, then
                                     cpλZ q ď cpλX q
with equality precisely when σ | ImpλZ q is injective.
    Proof. If λX is the attaching map for X and λZ is the attaching map for Z,
we have that
                               |λZ |         |λZ |
                                        “           .
                             degpλX q      degpλZ q
Moreover, we have          ˇ           ˇ ˇ             ˇ
                           ˇImpλX qp0q ˇ ď ˇImpλZ qp0q ˇ
                           ˇ           ˇ ˇ             ˇ

with equality precisely when σ | ImpλZ qp0q is a bijection. Since σ is an immersion,
this would force σ to be injective on all of ImpλZ q.                             □
Corollary 2.2.26. Let X “ pΛ, λq be a one-relator complex and let ρ : Y í X be
a Z-cover. If Z Ă Y is a one-relator Z-domain, minimal under inclusion amongst
one-relator Z-domains, then
                                  cpZq ă cpXq.
    Proof. Let λZ denote the attaching map of the 2-cell in Z. The cover ρ sends
ImpλZ q surjectively to Impλq. If cpλZ q ě cpλq, then cpλZ q “ cpλq by Lemma 2.2.25
and ImpλZ q maps isomorphically to Impλq. By collapsing each 2-cell to a point in
X, we obtain an induced commutative diagram
                                                 ρ
                                 Z         Y           X

                                                 ρ1
                                Z1         Y1          X1
where vertical maps are collapse maps. In particular, Z 1 , Y 1 , X 1 are all 1-complexes.
Since Z 1 ´ t´1 pZ 1 q X Z 1 contains precisely one lift of each cell in X 1 , we have
´χpZ 1 q “ ´χpX 1 q ´ χpt´1 pZ 1 q X Z 1 q. Hence, by Proposition 2.2.20, since Z was
minimal, it follows that Z 1 X tpZ 1 q is a tree and so ´χpZ 1 q “ ´χpX 1 q ´ 1. This
implies that ´χpZq ă χpXq.                                                              □
                2.2. ONE-RELATOR SPLITTINGS AND THE HIERARCHY                      89


     2.2.5.4. Completing the proof: the Freiheitssatz. Combining Proposition 2.2.19,
Corollary 2.2.21 and Corollary 2.2.26, we have proven Theorem 2.2.14 under the
assumption that the Freiheitssatz holds. This is because in order to apply Propo-
sition 2.2.19, we needed π1 pZ0 q, π1 pZ1 q Ñ π1 pZq to be injective.
     In order to prove the Freiheitssatz for X “ pΛ, λq, we assume it holds for all
one-relator complexes Z with cpZq ă cpXq (the base case cpXq “ 0 is clear). Let
e Ă Λ be a 1-cell traversed by λ and denote by Γ “ Λ ´ e. By Proposition 2.2.22,
there is a Z-cover ρ : Y Ñ Z and a one-relator Z-domain Z Ă Y with cpZq ă cpXq
and such that Z XtpZq does not support any attaching map of a 2-cell and such that
ρ´1 pΓq Ă Z. Thus, the homomorphisms π1 pZ0 q, π1 pZ1 q Ñ π1 pZq are both injective
by the inductive hypothesis and we may apply Proposition 2.2.19 to conclude that
                                π1 pXq – π1 pZq ˚ψ .
In particular, π1 pZq injects into π1 pXq. This implies that two paths in X that
lift to Z are path homotopic if and only if they are path homotopic in Z. Since
ρ´1 pΓq Ă Z, any pair of paths in Γ lift to Z. Since each component of ρ´1 pΓq is a
Magnus subgraph of Z, by induction it follows that any pair of paths in ρ´1 pΓq are
path homotopic in Z if and only if they are path homotopic in ρ´1 pΓq. Thus, any
pair of paths in Γ that are path homotopic in X are path homotopic in Γ. Thus,
the map
                                   π1 pΓq Ñ π1 pXq
induced by inclusion is injective. We have proven the Freiheitssatz and hence,
Theorem 2.2.14.

    2.2.6. The character sphere and describing all one-relator splittings.
If G is a group, the character sphere SpGq is the following space
                                  `                ˘
                          SpGq “ H 1 pG, Rq ´ t0u {Rą0
of equivalence classes of homomorphisms ϕ : G Ñ R, where ϕ „ ϕ1 if ϕ “ r ¨ ϕ1 for
some r P Rą0 .
    Let S be a finite set of cardinality n, let F “ F pSq be a free group, let w P F
be a cyclically reduced word and let G “ F {xxwyy be a one-relator group. Denote
by Γ Ă Rn “ H 1 pF, Rq the Cayley graph for Fab “ Zn over the generating set given
by the image of S in Fab . Let λw : I Ñ Γ denote the path at the origin spelling out
the word w and denote by Λw Ă Rn its image. Let pw denote the endpoint of λw .
We have pw “ 0 if and only if w P rF, F s. The path λw is called the trace of w. See
Fig. 3 for an example.
    There is a natural correspondence:
                  ␣                                                        (

where a line Lϕ Ă Rn is associated with a homomorphism ϕ : G Ñ R (unique in the

the orthogonal projection of Rn onto the line Lϕ . More explicitly, if pϕ P Lϕ is the
point at (positive) unit distance from the origin, then each element in r ¨ pϕ ` LK ϕ
gets mapped to r P R.

Lemma 2.2.28. Let X be a one-relator complex, let π1 pXq – π1 pZq˚ψ be a one-
relator splitting with ϕ : π1 pXq Ñ π1 pXq{xxπ1 pZqyy “ Z the associated epimorphism.
Then, after possibly replacing Z by a translate, the one-relator complex Zϕ associ-
ated with ϕ is a subcomplex of Z. In particular, π1 pZq splits as a free product of a
free group and π1 pZϕ q.
     In this way we may see all base groups of all one-relator splittings of π1 pXq
by looking at appropriate quotients of Λw , λw . In fact, there is always a maximal
such quotient, in the sense that this quotient one-relator complex quotients onto all
other Zϕ . Indeed, we may always choose a homomorphism ϕ : G Ñ R which factors
through an epimorphism G Ñ Z such that two points p, q P Λw Ă Γ in the same
Zn -orbit are identified under the quotient map Λw Ñ Λϕ if and only if p ` R ¨ pw “
q ` R ¨ pw . Let Λmax denote this maximal quotient and let λmax : S 1 í Λmax be the
induced cycle. Then by construction, the one-relator complex Zmax “ pΛmax , λmax q
admits a unique immersion Zmax í Zϕ1 for all epimorphisms ϕ1 : G Ñ Z. We have
proven the following lemma in the case X has a single 0-cell, the more general case
is left as an easy exercise to the reader.
Lemma 2.2.29. If X is a one-relator complex with π1 pXq infinite, then there is
a unique immersion of a one-relator complex Zmax í X such that Zmax í X
factors through all immersions of Z-domains of all Z-covers of X, with at least one
Z-domain containing Zmax as a subcomplex.
     Another way to see Zmax is as a minimal one-relator subcomplex of the maximal
torsion-free abelian cover of X. This is explained in more detail in [Lin22b].
     As an illustration of how to obtain Zmax , in Example 2.2.27 we may take the
line Lϕ to pass through the origin and p1, 3q instead of p1, 2q. In this way, no vertices
are identified under the projection map Λw Ñ Zϕ .

                      2.3. Splittings of one-relator groups
    2.3.1. Magnus subgroups and the Bass–Serre theory of one-relator
splittings. In this subsection, we discuss some properties of the action of a one-
relator group on the Bass–Serre tree associated with a one-relator splitting. In
order to understand the action, we need to understand the interaction between the
edge groups and their conjugates. Thus, in the first part of this subsection we shall
look at intersections of Magnus subgroups.
    2.3.1.1. Intersections of Magnus subgroups. Two results due to Collins are par-
ticularly important for this topic. The first involves exceptional intersections. A
92                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


pair of Magnus subgraphs A, B Ă X of a one-relator complex with AXB are said to
have exceptional intersection if π1 pAq X π1 pBq ‰ π1 pA X Bq. In other words, if the
intersection is not the obvious one. Collins characterised exceptional intersections
in [Col04]. Howie also characterised exceptional intersections for one-relator prod-
ucts and presented an algorithm to compute intersections of Magnus subgroups in
[How05].

   Theorem 2.3.1. Let X “ pΛ, λq be a one-relator complex and let A, B Ă X be
Magnus subgraphs such that A X B is connected. One of the following holds:
       ‚ π1 pAq X π1 pBq “ π1 pA X Bq.
       ‚ π1 pAq X π1 pBq “ π1 pA X Bq ˚ xcy for some c P π1 pXq.
Moreover, if degpλq ě 2, then only the first situation occurs.

    Howie’s result [How05] provides further information: if π1 pAq X π1 pBq have
an exceptional intersection, then there is an immersion of a one-relator complex
Z í X which expresses this intersection in an explicit way. This extra information
was exploited heavily in [Lin24a].
    The second result involves intersections between conjugates of Magnus sub-
groups and was proved by Collins in [Col08]. These turn out to be much more
controlled.

    Theorem 2.3.2. Let X “ pΛ, λq be a one-relator complex and let A, B Ă X be
Magnus subgraphs such that A X B is connected. If g P π1 pXq, one of the following
holds:
       ‚ g P π1 pAq ¨ π1 pBq, in which case π1 pAqg X π1 pBq “ pπ1 pAq X π1 pBqqb for
         some b P π1 pBq.
       ‚ π1 pAqg X π1 pBq “ 1.
       ‚ π1 pAqg X π1 pBq “ xcy for some c P π1 pXq.
Moreover, if degpλq ě 2, then only the first two situations occur.

   The rank rkpGq of a group G is the smallest cardinality of a generating set for
G. The reduced rank is then defined as:

                            rrpGq “ min t0, rkpGq ´ 1u.

A much stronger, but less precise result on intersections of Magnus subgroups of
one-relator groups was proven by Jaikin-Zapirain and the first author in [JZL23].

    Theorem 2.3.3. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group and A ď G is a Magnus
subgroup, then for any finitely generated subgroup H ď G, we have
                              ÿ
                                  rrpA X H g q ď rrpHq.
                             HgA

     Dicks–Ventura introduced the notion of being inert in [DV96]: a subgroup A ď
G is inert if for all finitely generated subgroups H ď G we have rkpAXHq ď rkpHq.
Theorem 2.3.3 says that Magnus subgroups of one-relator groups are strongly inert.
     2.3.1.2. The action on the Bass–Serre tree. Recall that if G – H˚ψ is a HNN-
extension, where ψ : A Ñ B is the identifying isomorphism and t P G is the stable
letter, then the Bass–Serre tree associated with the splitting is the tree T with
                      2.3. SPLITTINGS OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS                         93


vertices and edges:
                                               ğ
                                  V pT q “            gH
                                             gHPG{H
                                              ğ
                                  EpT q “             gA
                                             gAPG{A

and adjacency given by:
                                     opgAq “ gH
                                     tpgAq “ gtH.
A priori it is not obvious why this is a tree: the fundamental theorem of Bass–Serre
theory states that it is, see Serre’s monograph [Ser03] for details. The Bass–Serre
tree has a natural left G action with a single orbit of vertices and a single orbit of
edges.
    If gH P V pT q is a vertex at distance n from the vertex H P V pT q, then we have
                               gH “ h1 tϵ1 h1 . . . hn tϵn H
for some sequence of elements hi P H and ϵi “ ˘1.
     If g1 H, g2 H P V pT q, denote by Sg1 H,g2 H Ă T the unique geodesic segment
connecting g1 H with g2 H. Then we have
                      Stab pSg1 H,g2 H q “ Stabpg1 Hq X Stabpg2 Hq
                                              ´1           ´1
                                       “ H g1 X H g2
                                                      ´1        ´1

                                             ´            ¯g1´1

where Stabp´q denotes the pointwise stabiliser. Note that the length of Sg1 H,g2 H
is precisely the minimal number of t-letters which appear in the reduced form of
g1´1 g2 . Call this the t-length. More precisely, the t-length of an element g P H˚ψ ,
denoted by |g|t , is the smallest number of t˘1 -letters that appear in a word over H
and t˘1 that equals g in H˚ψ .
     If n ě 0 is any integer, denote by
                               Wn “ tg P G | |g|t “ nu.
By definition, we have W0 “ H. More generally, we have:
                             dpH, gHq “ n ðñ g P Wn .
    Using Collin’s results, in [Lin22a] the following result is shown.
    Theorem 2.3.4. If G – H˚ψ is a one-relator splitting, ψ : A Ñ B is the
identifying isomorphism, t P G is the stable letter and n ě 1 is an integer, then
                         ÿ
                              rrpH X H g q ď rrpAq ` rrpBq.
                        gPHWn H

If A X B has no exceptional intersection, then for some k ě 1, we have
                                   rrpH X H g q “ 0,
for all g P HWn H, n ě k.
94                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


     If k ě 0 is an integer, an action of a group G on a tree T is k-acylindrical if
for every segment S Ă T of length at least k, we have StabpSq “ 1. Say the action
is acylindrical if it is k-acylindrical for some k. The following theorem was proven
by Wise for one-relator groups with torsion in [Wis21]. The full statement is one
of the main theorems of [Lin22a].
     Theorem 2.3.5. Let G be a one-relator group and let G – H˚ψ be a one-relator
splitting. If G has torsion or is 2-free, then
                                 ÿ
                                     rkpH X H g q ă 8.
                              HgH

In particular, the action of G on the Bass–Serre tree is acylindrical.
    Recall that a group G is k-free if all k-generated subgroups of G are free.
We will revisit 2-free one-relator groups in detail in Section 2.5.3 and again in
Section 2.5.4.1. Importantly, the property of being 2-free is, in a precise sense,
generic and there is a straightforward algorithm which decides whether a one-relator
group is 2-free. Both of these results can be found in [LW22].
     2.3.2. Free co-abelian subgroups. A co-abelian subgroup of a group G is
a normal subgroup N Ÿ G such that G{N is torsion-free abelian. If G “ F {xxwyy
is a one-relator group, the aim of this section is to provide a simple criterion for
when a co-abelian subgroup of G is a free group. In particular, if G “ H˚ψ is a
one-relator splitting, we have a natural epimorphism
                                 G Ñ G{xxHyy “ Z
and we want to determine when the co-abelian subgroup xxHyy is a free group.
     Let X be the presentation complex for G. Any co-abelian subgroup of G can
be obtained as kerpϕq for some ϕ P H 1 pG, Rq – H 1 pX, Rq. Let ϕ P H1 pX, Rq and
let Y Ñ X be the induced abelian cover. Let µ : Y p1q Ñ R be the map given by
projection to Lϕ , as described in Section 2.2.6. Since kerpϕq “ π1 pY q, we want to
understand the structure of Y . For this we describe two conditions that depend
only on the homotopy type of Zϕ Ă Y .
     Recall that a subcomplex of a combinatorial complex is full if every cell whose
attaching map is supported in the subcomplex, also lies in the subcomplex. If
J Ă R is a connected subset, denote by YJ the full subcomplex of Y containing
all vertices that map within J Ă R via µ. Let amin , amax P R be the minimal and
maximal values that 0-cells in Zϕ map to. Consider the following two conditions:
      (1) (ϕ is freely descending) There is a 1-cell

Remark 2.3.6. A homomorphism ϕ P H 1 pX, Rq is freely descending if, for in-
stance, some minimal edge in Zϕ is a free edge in the sense that it is traversed
precisely once by the attaching map of the 2-cell in Zϕ . By symmetry, the same
can be said about when ϕ is freely descending with minimal replaced with maximal.
    Sapir–Špakulová show in [Sv11] that a random one-relator group on three or
more generators has some epimorphism ϕ : G Ñ Z such that Zϕ has a topmost or
bottommost edge that is free.
     Theorem 2.3.7. Let X be a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell. If ϕ P
H 1 pX, Rq is freely descending and ascending then kerpϕq is a free group.
Example 2.3.8. Let us return to Example 2.2.27. In this example, both the
topmost b-edge and the bottommost b-edge in Zϕ are traversed precisely once by
the attaching map of the 2-cell. Hence, we may collapse the 2-cell through these
free edges to obtain a homotopy equivalence as required by the definitions of ϕ
being freely descending and ascending. Hence, the homomorphism ϕ from Exam-
ple 2.2.27 is both freely descending and ascending so that kerpϕq is a free group by
Theorem 2.3.7. In fact, kerpϕq is a finitely generated free group.
Example 2.3.9. For another type of example, consider the one-relator group
                            G “ xa, b | a2 babca3 a2 bababc´1 y.
Let ϕ : G Ñ Z be the epimorphism defined by sending c to 1 and a, b to 0. See Fig. 5
for the one-relator complex Zϕ . We see that Zϕ does not have any free faces and so
we cannot conclude that kerpϕq is free as in Example 2.3.8. However, since a20 b0 a0 b0
is a primitive element of F pa0 , b0 q (we can see this by applying the automorphism
                                                                             p1q
a0 Ñ a0 b´2           ´1
          0 , b0 Ñ a0 b0 ), we see that Zϕ is homotopy equivalent to Zϕ ´ a0
              p1q
relative to Zϕ ´ pv0 Y a0 Y b0 Y c0 q “ Zϕ X Yp0,1s . Hence, ϕ is freely descending.
Since a21 b1 a1 b1 a1 b1 is a primitive element of F pa1 , b1 q, we may also conclude that
ϕ is freely ascending. Thus, kerpϕq is free by Theorem 2.3.7.

Since Yramin ,amax s is homotopy equivalent to ∆L YYpamin ,amax s , the result follows.   □
     Proof of Theorem 2.3.7. The image of π1 pXq under ϕ is isomorphic to Zn
for some n. Since Zϕ is finite, we may find an appropriate epimorphism Zn Ñ Z
such that, if ϕ1 denotes the composition of ϕ with this map, then there is an
isomorphism Zϕ Ñ Zϕ1 respecting the order on the vertices. This may be done for
instance by choosing a line L Ă Rm (where m “ rkpπ1 pX p1q qq) with rational slope
sufficiently close to the line associated with ϕ. In particular, ϕ1 is freely descending
and ascending because ϕ is and so kerpϕ1 q is free by Proposition 2.3.12. Since
kerpϕq ď kerpϕ1 q, we have that kerpϕq is free as claimed.                            □
Conjecture 2.3.13. If X is a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell and if ϕ P
H 1 pX, Rq, then kerpϕq is free if and only if ϕ is freely ascending and descending.
     In the next section we discuss Brown’s criterion which states that Conjecture
2.3.13 is true when χpXq “ 0. In other words, when π1 pXq is a two-generator
one-relator group.
     Propositions 2.3.10 and 2.3.11 actually imply the following weakening of The-
orem 2.3.7. Recall that a HNN-extension H˚ψ is ascending if the domain of ψ is
all of H, descending if the target of ψ is all of H.
     Theorem 2.3.14. Let X be a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell and let
ϕ P H 1 pX, Rq. If ϕ is freely descending or freely ascending, then kerpϕq is locally
free. If ϕ also factors through Z, then π1 pXq splits as a descending or ascending
HNN-extension of a free group π1 pXq – F ˚ψ such that xxF yy “ kerpϕq.
Remark 2.3.15. A finitely generated ascending HNN-extension of a free group
embeds into an ascending HNN-extension of a finitely generated free group by work
of Chong–Wise [CW24]. Since an ascending HNN-extension of a finitely generated
free group is residually finite by work of Borisov–Sapir [BS05], Theorem 2.3.14 also
yields a criterion for residual finiteness of a one-relator group.
    We may also state a variation of Conjecture 2.3.13.
Conjecture 2.3.16. If X is a one-relator complex with a single 0-cell and if
ϕ P H 1 pX, Rq, then kerpϕq is locally free if and only if ϕ is freely ascending or
descending.
    Baumslag asked in [Bau74b] whether a (one-relator) group with locally free
derived subgroup is residually finite. Answering Conjecture 2.3.16 would solve this
problem by Remark 2.3.15.
     2.3.3. Algebraic fibring, Brown’s criterion and the Friedl–Tillmann
polytope. An algebraic fibring of a group G is an epimorphism ϕ : G Ñ Z with
kerpϕq finitely generated. One can think of this as an algebraic analog of a fibration
of a manifold over a circle. Algebraic fibrations of a group G are encoded in its
BNS-invariant, see Bieri–Neumann–Strebel [BNS87]. We shall not define what
the BNS-invariant of a group G is, but we remark that it is an open subset of the
character sphere Σ1 pGq Ă SpGq and that kerpϕq is finitely generated if and only if
rϕs, ´rϕs P Σ1 pGq. The reader is directed to [Str13] for further details.
     Brown computed the BNS-invariant for all one-relator groups in [Bro87] and
thus characterised when a one-relator group admits an algebraic fibring. Brown’s
theorem, known as Brown’s criterion, completely determines which homomorphisms
98                                   2. RECENT PROGRESS


G Ñ Z of a one-relator group have finitely generated kernel in terms of the relator.
Below we state Brown’s criterion in the language of Section 2.2.6.
    Theorem 2.3.17 (Brown’s criterion). Let G “ F pSq{xxwyy be a one-relator
group with w cyclically reduced, let X be its presentation complex and let ϕ : G Ñ Z
be an epimorphism. Then:
      (1) rϕs P Σ1 pGq if and only if |S| “ 2 and a bottommost edge in Zϕ “ pΛϕ , λϕ q
          is traversed once by λϕ .
      (2) ´rϕs P Σ1 pGq if and only if |S| “ 2 and a topmost edge in Zϕ “ pΛϕ , λϕ q
          is traversed once by λϕ .
In particular, kerpϕq is finitely generated if and only if |S| “ 2 and a topmost and
a bottommost edge in Zϕ is traversed once by λϕ .
Remark 2.3.18. Dunfield–Thurston investigated algebraic fibring of one-relator
groups from a probabilistic perspective in [DT06] using Brown’s criterion. Specif-
ically they showed that the probability that a random two-generator one-relator
group algebraically fibres lies somewhere between 0.0006 and 0.975. They also re-
mark that computer experiments indicate the probability may be limiting towards
0.94.
    As mentioned in Section 2.3.2, Brown’s criterion is a converse to Theorem 2.3.7
for two-generator one-relator groups. Let us provide a sketch proof of the last
statement of Theorem 2.3.17. This part of the statement was proved first by Mol-
davanskiı̆ [Mol67].

    Sketch proof. Let us first assume that a topmost and bottommost edge in
Zϕ is traversed once by λϕ , then by Proposition 2.3.12, we have

Lemma 2.3.19. Let X “ pΛ, λq be a one-relator complex and let M Ă X be a
Magnus subgraph. The inclusion π1 pM q ãÑ π1 pXq is an isomorphism if and only
if there is a 1-cell e Ă Λ ´ M traversed by λ precisely once and Λ ´ e deformation
retracts onto M .

Lemma 2.3.19 to conclude that there are 1-cells eL Ă Z ´ Z1 and eU Ă Z ´ Z0
that are both traversed precisely once by λϕ . By the first part of the proof this also
implies that |S| “ 2 and so the proof is complete.                                   □
    2.3.3.1. The Friedl–Tillmann polytope. In this section we describe the Friedl–
Tillmann of a two-generator one-relator group, as introduced in [FT20]. This is
a marked polytope in H1 pG, Rq “ R2 which encodes the BNS-invariant of a one-
relator group via its marking. It can be constructed directly from a presentation
and it is (up to translation) independent of the choice of one-relator presentation.
    Let us fix a free group F “ F pa, bq and a cyclically reduced word w contained in
the commutator subgroup rF pa, bq, F pa, bqs. Recall from Section 2.2.6 the definition
of Λw . Let Cw Ă R2 be the convex hull of Λw . Mark all vertices in Cw that
are traversed precisely once by λw , the trace of w. The Friedl–Tillmann polytope
Mw Ă H1 pG, Rq “ R2 for F {xxwyy is then defined as the convex hull of the midpoints
of the squares contained in Cw . A vertex of the polytope Mw is marked if all the
vertices of the corresponding square in Cw are marked. See Fig. 6 for an illustration
of the marked polytope Mw for the one-relator group from Example 2.2.27.
100                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


    An element ϕ P H 1 pG, Rq pairs maximally with a vertex v of Mw if ϕpvq ą ϕpwq
for all other vertices w ‰ v in Mw . Friedl–Tillmann prove the following result in
[FT20], relating their polytope to the BNS-invariant of a one-relator group.
    Theorem 2.3.20. Let F “ F pa, bq, let w P rF, F s be a cyclically reduced word
and let G “ F {xxwyy. Then ϕ P H 1 pG, Rq lies in the BNS-invariant Σ1 pGq if and
only if ϕ pairs maximally with a marked vertex of Mw .
    In the same article, Friedl–Tillmann conjecture that Pw is an invariant of the
group F {xxwyy, up to translation. They prove that it is the case for a large class
of one-relator groups, but their conjecture was only confirmed in full by Henneke–
Kielak in [HK20, Theorem 5.12].
    Theorem 2.3.21. Let F “ F pa, bq, let w P rF, F s be a cyclically reduced word
and let G “ F {xxwyy. Then the marked polytope Mw is an invariant of G, up to
translation.
    See Section 2.3.4 for a connection between the thickness of the Friedl–Tillmann
polytope of a one-relator group and its splitting complexity.

    2.3.4. Splitting complexity, JSJ-decompositions and amalgamated
free products. If G is a group and ϕ : G Ñ Z is an epimorphism, a splitting
of pG, ϕq is an HNN-extension decomposition G – H˚ψ such that xxHyy “ kerpϕq.
Recall that if ψ identifies A ď H with B ď H, then A and B are the associated
groups. The splitting complexity of pG, ϕq is
          cpG, ϕq “ min trkpAq | pG, ϕq splits with associated group Au.
The free splitting complexity of pG, ϕq is
        cf pG, ϕq “ min trkpAq | pG, ϕq splits with free associated group Au.
If G is a finitely presented group (or more generally, of type FP2 pZq) and ϕ : G Ñ Z
is an epimorphism, then Bieri–Strebel proved [BS78, Theorem A] that cpG, ϕq ă 8.
Although cf pG, ϕq in general could be infinite, when G is a finitely generated one-
relator group it is finite for all ϕ by Theorem 2.2.14.
     In [FT20], Friedl–Tillmann related the splitting complexity of many pairs
pF {xxwyy, ϕq with the thickness of the polytope Mw with respect to ϕ:
                    thϕ pMw q “ max tϕppq ´ ϕpqq | p, q P Mw u
where here ϕ is defined on Mw via its natural extension to a homomorphism
H1 pG, Rq “ R2 Ñ R. Henneke–Kielak then generalised their results to all two-
generator one-relator groups in [HK20, Theorem 6.4].
    Theorem 2.3.22. Let F “ F pa, bq, let w P rF, F s be a cyclically reduced word
that is not a proper power and let G “ F {xxwyy. For any epimorphism ϕ : G Ñ Z,
we have
                        cpG, ϕq “ cf pG, ϕq “ thϕ pMw q ` 1.
Remark 2.3.23. The upper bound cf pG, ϕq ď thϕ pMw q from Theorem 2.3.22 is
[FT20, Proposition 7.3]. The proof shows that there is a one-relator splitting for
pG, ϕq with associated Magnus subgroups of rank precisely thϕ pMw q ` 1. This can
also be proved directly using Theorem 2.2.14 and the description of one-relator
splittings from Section 2.2.6.
                     2.3. SPLITTINGS OF ONE-RELATOR GROUPS                        101


Question 2.3.24. Is there an analogue to Theorem 2.3.22 valid for all one-relator
groups?
     Let G “ F {xxwyy be a one-relator group as in Theorem 2.3.22. One immediate
consequence of Theorem 2.3.22 is that G splits as a HNN-extension over Z if and
only if thϕ pGq “ 0 for some ϕ, or, equivalently, if and only if the polytope Mw is a
line. In light of Remark 2.3.23, G then splits as a HNN-extension over an infinite
cyclic group if and only if G admits a one-relator splitting with associated Magnus
subgroups isomorphic to Z.
     Gardam–Kielak–Logan applied this to two-generator one-relator hyperbolic
groups in [GKL24] to compute the JSJ-decomposition of G. Logan was able
to understand the case of two-generator one-relator groups with torsion in even
more detail and worked out a complete characterisation of all outer automorphism
groups in [Log16]. The reader is directed towards Guirardel–Levitt [GL17] for
more information on JSJ-decompositions.
Problem 2.3.25. Characterise the JSJ-decompositions of one-relator groups.
    Although a lot is known about HNN-extension decompositions, as evidenced
above and by Section 2.2, not much is known about amalgamated free product
decompositions of one-relator groups. Call an amalgamated free product G “
A ˚C B proper if A, B, C are finitely generated, C is a proper subgroup of A and
B, and C has index at least three in A or B. Wall asked the following question in
[Wa779, Question F2].
Question 2.3.26 (Wall). Which one-relator groups split as proper amalgamated
free products?
     It is not too hard to show that a proper amalgamated free product contains
a free subgroup of rank two. Thus, BSp1, nq is an infinite family of one-relator
groups that cannot admit any proper amalgamated free product decomposition.
Nevertheless, many one-relator groups do andmit such decompositions. Baumslag–
Shalen showed in [BS90] that every group G of deficiency at least two admits a
proper amalgamated free product decomposition. That is, G “ A˚C B with A, B, C
finitely generated, C a proper subgroup of A and B and with C of index greater
than two in A or B. In particular, this applies to one-relator groups generated by
at least three elements. Such decompositions were also shown to hold for all non-
cyclic one-relator groups with torsion by Benyash-Krivets [BK98]. Wall’s question
does not yet have an answer for torsion-free two-generator one-relator groups.
   Theorem 2.3.27. A one-relator group G “ F {xxwyy splits as a proper amalga-
mated free product unless rkpF q “ 2 and G is torsion-free.
     Beyond existence of amalgamated free product decompositions of one-relator
groups, it would also be interesting to know what structure such amalgamated
free products can have. Fine–Peluso proposed several conjectures in [FP99] which
became known as the Amalgam Conjectures. We state the weakest possible such
conjecture.
Conjecture 2.3.28 (The Amalgam Conjecture). If G is a one-relator group with
at least three generators, then G – A ˚C B with C a free group and A, B either
one-relator groups or free groups.
102                            2. RECENT PROGRESS


     Note that in general, if A ˚C B is finitely presented and A, B, C are finitely
generated, it is possible that A, B, C are not finitely presented, see [BS90, Section
6] for an example. In case G “ A ˚C B is a one-relator group, we know that A, B, C
are finitely presented since G is coherent, see Section 2.5.4.

                        2.4. Group algebra properties
    Recall that if G is a group and R is a ring, then the group algebra RG (or
RrGs) is the R-algebra with free R-basis in correspondence with the elements of G
and with multiplication of basis elements given by the multiplication from G. In
other words, it is the ring with underlying set
              #          ˇ                                                  +
                ÿ        ˇ
       RG “         rg g ˇ rg P R and rg “ 0 for all but finitely many g P G .
                         ˇ

and ring operations defined in the natural way. In this section we will be studying
the properties of group rings of one-relator groups.

    2.4.1. (Co)homology and the relation module. Let F be a free group
and let N Ÿ F be a normal subgroup. Then F acts by conjugation on the abelian
group
                              Nab “ N {rN, N s.
This action extends linearly to a (left) action of ZF on Nab . Since N acts triv-
ially on its abelianisation, this action descends to an action of ZG, making Nab
a (left) ZG-module called the relation module. Another way to view the rela-
tion module of a group presentation G “ F pSq{N is as the ZG-module of cycles
Nab “ Z1 pCaypG, Sqq of the Cayley graph with respect to the generating set S. See
Fig. 7.
     Lyndon computed the relation module of a one-relator group in [Lyn50], a
theorem which became known as Lyndon’s identity theorem.
     Theorem 2.4.1 (Lyndon’s Identity Theorem). Let G “ F {xxwn yy be a one-
relator group with w not a proper power and with n ě 1. Denoting by N “ xxwn yy,
we have
                               Nab – ZG{pw ´ 1q.
In particular, if n “ 1 then
                                    Nab – ZG.
    The simplest way to prove Theorem 2.4.1 is to show that xxwn yy Ÿ F has the
Cohen–Lyndon property: there is a set of xxwn yy, xwy double coset representatives
T Ă F such that xxwn yy – ˚tPT twn t´1 . This was first done by Cohen and Lyndon
in [CL63].
    The main use for Theorem 2.4.1 is to compute the (co)homology of a one-
relator group. Indeed, by [Bro94, Proposition 5.4], if G “ F pSq{N , we have an
exact sequence
