Corollary 2.4.3. Let G “ F {xxwyy be a torsion-free one-relator group and let R be
a ring. Then the (left) global dimension of RG is at most the (left) global dimension
of R plus two. In particular, if R “ K is a field, the global dimension of KG is at
most two.
    Note that if R is commutative, the opposite ring RGop is isomorphic to RG
and so left and right global dimension of RG coincide.
Remark 2.4.4. Such a resolution can be constructed in a similar way for arbitrary
groups, see [JZL23, Proposition 2.2].
    2.4.2. Groups with cyclic relation module. A natural question that arises
from Theorem 2.4.1 is whether having a cyclic relation module is limited to one-
relator groups.
Question 2.4.5 (Harlander). If G “ F {N is a group with relation module Nab
cyclic, is N normally generated by a single element?
     This question is a special case of the relation gap problem which asks whether
a finitely presented group G “ F {N can have strictly fewer relation module gen-
erators than N has normal generators. Bestvina–Brady showed in [BB97] that
there exist groups with infinite relation gap. That is, groups that are not finitely
presented but have type FP2 pZq, a property equivalent to the relation module be-
ing finitely generated. Counterexamples to the relation gap problem could lead to
counterexamples to old conjectures on the homotopy type of CW-complexes such
as Wall’s Dp2q conjecture. The following theorem from [Lin24b] makes it unlikely
that counterexamples will come from groups with cyclic relation module.
     Theorem 2.4.6. If G “ F {N is a right orderable group with Nab a cyclic
relation module, then N is normally generated by a single element. In particular,
G is a one-relator group.
    Recall that a locally indicable group is right orderable by the Burns–Hale the-
orem [BH72] and a right orderable group is one with a total order on its elements,
invariant under multiplication from the right. A key step in the proof of The-
orem 2.4.6 involved showing that normal subgroups of one-relator groups which
have right orderable quotients, must be normally generated by a single element.
Indeed, if G “ F {N and Nab is cyclic, then there is an element w P N whose image
in Nab generates the relation module and such that the map F {xxwyy Ñ G has
perfect kernel. This is explained in detail in [Har15].
Question 2.4.7. Are normal perfect subgroups of one-relator groups normally
generated by a single element?
                              2.4. GROUP ALGEBRA PROPERTIES                                    105


      A positive answer to this question would solve Question 2.4.5. Note that perfect
subgroups of one-relator groups are necessarily infinitely generated. In the torsion-
free case this is by local indicability, in the torsion case, the natural quotient map
F {xxwn yy Ñ F {xxwyy onto the torsion-free one-relator group allows us to reduce to
considering finitely generated subgroups in the kernel. But the kernel splits as a
free product of (infinitely many) copies of Z{nZ, a group which contains no perfect
subgroups. This last fact is due to Fisher–Karrass–Solitar [FKS72, Theorem 1].
      Another closely related problem is the relation lifting problem, asked by Wall
in [Wal66]. If G “ F {N is a presentation and r1 , . . . , rn P Nab are ZG-module
generators, then the relation lifting problem asks whether there exist elements
w1 , . . . , wn P N such that
               xxw1 , . . . , wn yy “ N,   w1 rN, N s “ r1 , . . . , wn rN, N s “ rn .
The relations w1 , . . . , wn are called lifts of r1 , . . . , rn . Using the fact that ZG is a do-
main and contains no non-trivial units under the hypotheses of Theorem 2.4.6, one
can show that every generator of Nab can be lifted. On the other hand, Dunwoody
showed in [Dun72] that the following element
                                    p1 ´ a ` a2 qb ¨ a5 rN, N s
cannot be lifted to a normal generator of xxa5 yy “ N Ÿ F pa, bq. The proof uses the
Magnus property for free groups, Theorem 2.2.8, to show that there is no lift and the
fact that p1´a`a2 qb is a non-trivial unit in ZG to show that p1´a`a2 qb¨a5 rN, N s
is a generator.
     Generalisations of both the relation lifting and relation gap problems can be
considered for relation modules over arbitrary rings R. In this case, the R-relation
module of G “ F {N is the left RG-module
                                            R bZ Nab .
The main theorem of [Lin24b] applies to R-relation modules with R an arbitrary
domain. In this slightly more general setting, it is no longer true that every gen-
erator r P R bZ Nab may be lifted to a normal generator of N . However, there
will always be some element w P N and a unit u P Rˆ such that xxwyy “ N and
r “ u b wrN, N s.
Conjecture 2.4.8. Let G “ F {N be a torsion-free group such that Q bZ Nab is
cyclic as a QG-module, then N is normally generated by a single element.
Remark 2.4.9. There exist groups with torsion which are not one-relator groups,
but which have a presentation with cyclic Q-relation module. Such an example is
given in [Lin24b].
    2.4.3. L2 -Betti numbers and the strong Atiyah conjecture for one-

[Lüc02, Chapter 10]. For torsion-free groups, the strong Atiyah conjecture over a
subfield K Ă C is equivalent to the division closure of KG in UpGq being a division
ring. See [LS12, Proposition 1.2] for this fact.

strong Atiyah conjecture over any subfield K Ă C.

Corollary 2.4.12. If G is a one-relator group, then G satisfies the strong Atiyah
conjecture.
    2.4.4. Embedding the group ring of a torsion-free one-relator group
into a division ring. Recall that a division ring is a ring in which each non-
zero element is a unit. Lewin–Lewin proved in [LL78] that the group ring of a
torsion-free one-relator group embeds into a division ring.
    Theorem 2.4.13. If G “ F {xxwyy is a torsion-free one-relator group and k is
a division ring, then kG ãÑ D for some division ring D.
    Theorem 2.4.13 provided the first proof of Kaplansky’s zero divisor conjecture
for torsion-free one-relator groups. Kaplansky’s zero divisor conjecture is also a
consequence of local indicability, proved later by Brodskii and Howie.
    The proof of Theorem 2.4.13 uses the Magnus hierarchy and various ring con-
structions to explicitly and inductively construct the division ring D. These con-
structions include Ore localisation, skew polynomial ring constructions, Cohn’s uni-
versal matrix inverting construction and coproducts of rings. The background and
details on these notions are covered in detail in Cohn’s book [Coh06].
                         2.4. GROUP ALGEBRA PROPERTIES                             107


    One of the main difficulties in Lewin–Lewin’s construction was ensuring that
at each stage in the hierarchy, the division rings were pH, Gq-free for all Magnus
subgroups H of the one-relator group G. Let k be a division ring, G a group and
kG ãÑ D an embedding into a division ring. If N ď H ď G are subgroups and
DN ď D denotes the division closure of kN in D, then D is pN, Hq-free if the
multiplication map
                                 DN bkN kH Ñ D
given by d b h ÞÑ dh is injective.
                               À Note that if T Ă H is a right transversal for N ,
then we have DN bkN kH – tPT DN bt as a left kN -module.
     There is another way to embed a group ring of a torsion-free one-relator group
into a division ring which we shall now explain. Firstly, we define two types of
division ring embeddings which play a particularly important role. Let kG ãÑ D
be an epic division ring embedding. Recall that kG ãÑ D is epic of D coincides
with the division closure of kG. The embedding is Hughes-free if it is pN, Hq-free
for every finitely generated subgroup H ď G and every normal subgroup N Ÿ H
with H{N – Z. A well-known theorem of Hughes [Hug70] states that a locally
indicable group admits at most one Hughes-free embedding into a division ring,
up to isomorphism. The embedding is Linnell if it is pN, Gq-free for any subgroup
N ď G. When the division closure of CG in the ring of affiliated operators UpGq is
a division ring, this division ring embedding is Linnell. Furthermore, if G is locally
indicable, then by Hughes’ theorem, its Linnell division ring in UpGq will also be
Hughes-free. In particular, the division ring embedding one obtains for KG, with
G a torsion-free one-relator group and K Ă C a subfield, from Theorem 2.4.11 is
Linnell.
     Let G be a right orderable group, let ď be a right G-invariant total order and
let k be a division ring. Denote by
                             #                                        +
                                    ÿ        ˇ
                kppG, ďqq “ r “       rg ¨ g ˇ suppprq is well-ordered
                                             ˇ
                                 gPG

the space of Mal’cev–Neumann series. A powerful theorem of Malcev and Neumann
[Mal48, Neu49] shows that if ď is a bi-ordering of G, then kppG, ďqq actually
has the structure of a division ring called the Mal’cev–Neumann completion. In
particular, if G is a free group, then it admits a bi-ordering and so it embeds
in its Mal’cev–Nuemann completion. This is in fact the base case of the Lewin–
Lewin division ring construction. Lewin showed in [Lew74] that in this case the
embedding of kG into its division closure in its Mal’cev Neumann completion is
Hughes-free (in fact, also Linnell).
     When ď is not a bi-ordering, kppG, ďqq does not have a natural ring structure.
In this case one should look at the endomorphism ring of kppG, ďqq, denoted by
EndpkppG, ďqqq. The group ring kG acts naturally on the right, and so kG embeds
in EndpkppG, ďqqq. It turns out that the endomorphism ring EndpkppG, ďqqq is a
division ring in the case that G is locally indicable and ď is a Conradian right
order. Note that admitting a Conradian right order is equivalent to being locally
indicable, a fact which is a consequence of a result of Brodskii [Bro84], explained
in [RR02]. The following is obtained by combining a result of Jaikin-Zapirain–
López-Álvarez [JZLA20, Corollary 1.4] with a result of Gräter [Grä20a, Theorem
8.1 & Corollary 8.3]. See also [JZL23, Theorem 2.5] and the preceding discussion.
108                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


     Theorem 2.4.14. Let G be a locally indicable group, ď a Conradian right order
on G and let K be a field of characteristic zero. Then the ring EndpKppG, ďqqq is a
division ring. Moreover, the division closure of KG in EndpKppG, ďqqq is a Linnell
ring.
Corollary 2.4.15. If G is a torsion-free one-relator group and K is a field of
characteristic zero, then KG embeds into a Linnell division ring DKG . Moreover,
DKG is the unique epic Linnell division KG-ring.
    By a result of Fisher–Sánchez-Peralta [FSP24], the Lewin–Lewin division ring
construction coincides with the division ring from Theorem 2.4.14 for KG where
K is a field of characteristic zero and G a torsion-free one-relator group.
   2.4.5. Coherence of the group ring. Recall that a presentation of a (left)
R-module M is an exact sequence
                           F1        F0         M          0
where F0 and F1 are free (left) R-modules.
Definition 2.4.16. A ring R is (left) coherent if all of its finitely generated (left)
ideals are finitely presented.
     Coherence of rings was introduced by Bourbaki in the 1960’s. In general, it
is important to distinguish between left and right modules as there are examples
of rings which are left coherent but not right coherent. See, for example, [La772,
Example (e), p. 139]. However, for group rings over a commutative ring, the two
notions coincide. One of the main results in [JZL23] is the following.
     Theorem 2.4.17. If G is a one-relator group and K is a field of characteristic
0, then KrGs is coherent.
     A right R-module M is said to have weak dimension at most k if TorR k`1 pM, N q “
0 for all left R-modules N . Say M has weak dimension k if it has weak dimension
at most k, but not at most k ´ 1. The proof of Theorem 2.4.17 makes use of the
following criterion for when a finitely generated R-module is finitely presented. See
[JZL23, Proposition 3.1].
Proposition 2.4.18. Let R be a ring of global dimension at most two and let
R ãÑ D be an embedding into a division ring. If M is a finitely generated right
R-module, then
               M is finitely presented ðñ dimD TorR
                                                  1 pM, Dq ă 8.
In particular, if D has weak dimension at most one, then R is coherent.
     To see that the last claim in Proposition 2.4.18 follows from the first, note that
if I ď R is an ideal, then
                             TorR             R
                                1 pI, Dq “ Tor2 pR{I, Dq

and so if D has weak dimension at most one, then TorR  1 pI, Dq “ 0.
    The idea to prove Theorem 2.4.17 is to use Proposition 2.4.18 on the group
ring of a one-relator group. There are two cases to consider: when G is torsion-free
and when G has torsion.
    Suppose that G is a torsion-free one-relator group and K is a field. As we saw
in Section 2.4.1, KrGs has global dimension at most two and so it suffices to show
                             2.5. SUBGROUP PROPERTIES                            109


that D has weak dimension at most one. Let M be a left KrGs-module. From
Section 2.4.1 we have the following resolution
                            À

which comes from the standard resolution of K as a trivial KrGs-module. By
Theorem 2.4.14, there exists a division ring embedding KrGs ãÑ D, turning D into
a right KrGs-module. Applying D bKrGs ´ we obtain a complex
                    d2   À                  d1               d0

                                KrGs

The delicate part of the proof of Theorem 2.4.17 is now to show that D bK M
is a torsion-free KrGs module, and show that this implies that kerpd2 q “ 0, see
[JZL23, Lemma 5.1 & Theorem 5.3]. In order to carry out this step, one needs to
know that the division ring D into which KrGs embeds is Hughes-free. Since such a
division ring is only known to exist when K has characteristic 0 by Theorem 2.4.14,
Theorem 2.4.17 requires this hypothesis. However, the proof carries over directly
if one were to show that a Hughes-free division ring exists for KrGs with G a
torsion-free one-relator group and K an arbitrary field.
     If G is a one-relator group with torsion, the ring KrGs does not have global
dimension at most two. Dawid Kielak and the first author showed in [KL24] that
one-relator groups with torsion have a finite index subgroup that is free-by-cyclic,
see Section 2.5.5. Let F ¸ψ Z be the finite index free-by-cyclic subgroup of G.
Now KrF s embeds into its Mal’cev–Neumann completion, giving us a division ring
embedding KrF s ãÑ D. Since KrF ¸ψ Zs – pKrF sqrt˘1 , ψs, localising Drt˘1 , ψs,

coherence property passes between finite codimensional ideals, the group ring KrGs
is also coherent.
     We conclude this section by remarking that the proof of group ring coherence
does not carry over to RrGs when R is not a field as the global dimension of RrGs
is no longer at most two. Even the case R “ Z is open and interesting.

                           2.5. Subgroup properties
    2.5.1. Stackings and non-positive immersions. Dani Wise introduced the
notions of non-positive immersions and negative immersions in [Wis03, Wis04]
as tools to understand better the subgroup structure of certain groups with an eye
towards solving Baumslag’s conjecture on the coherence of one-relator groups.
    The independent proofs that torsion-free one-relator groups have presentation
complexes with non-positive immersions by Helfer–Wise [HW16] and Louder–
Wilton [LW17], led to a sequence of beautiful developments in understanding the
subgroups of one-relator groups which we shall explain in the next few sections.
Definition 2.5.1. A 2-complex X has non-positive immersions if for every immer-
sion Y í X with Y compact and connected, either π1 pY q “ 1 or χpY q ď 0.
110                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


Remark 2.5.2. Other variations of the non-positive immersions property replace
the condition in Theorem 2.5.5 that Y be simply connected with the condition
that Y be contractible or collapsible. Yet another variation, weak non-positive
immersions, requires that χpY q ď 1 for all immersions Y í X with Y compact
and connected.
    When Dani Wise introduced the notion of non-positive immersions in [Wis03],
he conjectured that all one-relator complexes X “ pΛ, λq with degpλq “ 1 have non-
positive immersions. Wise had reduced his conjecture to a conjecture about free
groups which he called the w-cycles conjecture: if F is a free group, w P F is not a
proper power and H ď F is any finitely generated free group, then
                              ÿ
                                  rkpW f X Hq ď rkpHq
                            W fH

with equality only if H ď xxwyy. This can also be interpreted as a rank 1 version of
the (strengthened) Hanna Neumann inequality. In [LW17] Louder–Wilton solved
the w-cycles conjecture. Their proof uses the idea of a stacking.
Definition 2.5.3. Let λ : S í Γ be an immersion of a disjoint union of copies of S 1
into a graph Γ. A stacking of λ is an embedding λ
                                                r : S ãÑ Γ ˆ R such that π ˝ λ

where π : Γ ˆ R Ñ Γ is the projection map.

         T “ tx P S | @y ‰ x    pλpxq “ λpyq ùñ ιpλpxqq

The stacking is good if the top and the bottom intersect each component of S non-
trivially. Louder–Wilton then show that good stackings always exist for immersions
λ : S 1 í Λ, where degpλq “ 1. Their argument makes use of the existence of
maximal Z-tower liftings of λ, a fact due to Howie [How82]. The topological
one-relator hierarchy described in Section 2.2.2 yields, in fact, an explicit maximal
tower lifting of λ. Alternatively, as Wise points out in [Wis20], one can use a
result of Farrell [Far76], the right orderability of torsion-free one-relator groups
and Weinbaum’s theorem [Wei72].
     A key insight of Louder–Wilton is that good stackings can be used to compute
Euler characteristics. This led to the following result, whch is a combination of
[LW17, Lemma 12 & 16]. Recall that the core of a graph Γ, denoted by CorepΓq,
is the union of the images of all immersed cycles S 1 í Γ.
Proposition 2.5.4. Let Λ be a graph and let λ : S í Λ be an immersion of a
disjoint union of cycles S 1 . Suppose that λ admits a good stacking (for example,
if S “ S 1 and degpλq “ 1). Let Λ1 í Λ be a graph immersion and consider the
following diagram

λ1´1 peq is a single edge, or we have
                                ´χpImpλ1 qq ě degpσq.
    The w-cycles conjecture follows immediately from Proposition 2.5.4. We ex-
plain in some detail how to obtain the non-positive immersions property from
Proposition 2.5.4.
   Theorem 2.5.5. If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex with degpλq “ 1, then
X has non-positive immersions.
    Proof. Let Z “ pΓ, γq be a 2-complex and let Z í X be an immersion. If
there is a 1-cell e Ă Γ such that γ ´1 peq contains a single 1-cell, then by removing
e and the 2-cell whose boundary cycle traverses e, we obtain a subcomplex of Z
which is homotopy equivalent to Z. Thus, we may assume that no such 1-cell exists.
Consider the following commutative diagram

where γ : SΓ í Γ is the attaching map for the 2-cells in Z and where the fact that

Remark 2.5.6. The proof actually shows something stronger: if Z í X is an
immersion of a compact and connected 2-complex, then either χpZq ď 0 or Z
collapses through free faces to a point (collapsing non-positive immersions).
     Other proofs of Theorem 2.5.5 are also worth mentioning. Helfer–Wise prove
Theorem 2.5.5 at the same time as Louder–Wilton in [HW16], using the notion
of (bi)-slim structures. In [BCGW24] Bamberger–Carrier–Gaster–Wise showed
that the existence of a good stacking for an immersion λ : S í Λ is equivalent to
the existence of a bi-slim structure on X “ pΛ, λq. Since the existence of a slim
structure is strictly more general, the proof in [HW16] applies to a somewhat larger
class of 2-complexes. For example, they show that reducible 2-complexes without
proper powers (in the sense of Howie [How82]) have slim structures and so have
non-positive immersions. A third proof was provided by Howie–Short in [HS23]
using orderability and diagrams. This last proof could be applied in a more general
‘relative’ setting. A similar result was independently obtained by Millard in his
thesis [Mil21] using ‘relative’ stackings. Finally, a completely different proof was
given in [JZL23], using homological algebra.
112                            2. RECENT PROGRESS


    Not many general properties of 2-complexes with non-positive immersions are
known. Wise conjectured in [Wis03] that if X has non-positive immersions, then
π1 pXq is coherent. That is, every finitely generated subgroup of π1 pXq is finitely
presented. Although this conjecture remains open, in Section 2.5.4 we will see that
significant progress has been made. Properties that are known to hold for all 2-
complexes with non-positive immersions are the following, due to Wise [Wis22]
(see [JZL23, Proposition 2.7]).
     Theorem 2.5.7. If X is a 2-complex with non-positive immersions and π1 pXq ‰
1, then:
       ‚ π1 pXq is locally indicable.
       ‚ X is aspherical.
    Note that these properties were known to hold for (presentation complexes of)
torsion-free one-relator groups, see Section 2.2.2.
     2.5.2. (Uniform) Negative immersions. In [Wis04], Dani Wise intro-
duced a strengthening of non-positive immersions which he called negative im-
mersions. After Louder–Wilton introduced stackings in [LW17], they then wrote
[LW22] in which they used stackings to characterise precisely when a one-relator
complex has negative immersions. However, the definition Louder–Wilton used was
slightly different, although morally very similar, to that of Wise. The advantage of
the Louder–Wilton definiton is that it is invariant under Nielsen equivalence. Since
we shall be covering the results of Louder–Wilton, we shall be working with their
definition here.
     Before stating the definition of negative immersions, we shall need to explain
what it means for a 2-complex to be reducible or irreducible. For this we follow
[LW24]. There the notion of branched immersions is also used. However, we stick
to regular immersions.
     Let X be a 2-complex. We say X is visibly reducible if one of the following
holds:
      (1) X contains a vertex of degree at most one.
      (2) X contains a locally separating vertex.
      (3) X contains a free face.
Morally, X is visibly reducible if there is an obvious way to simplfy it: remove
hanging trees (corresponding to (1)), decompose X as a wedge of two subcomplexes
(corresponding to (2)) or collapse a 2-cell through an edge (corresponding to (3)).
A combinatorial map of 2-complexes ϕ : Y Ñ X is an essential equivalence if the
following two conditions hold:
      (1) ϕp1q : Y p1q Ñ X p1q is a homotopy equivalence that factors as a sequence
          of folds (in the sense of Stallings [Sta83])
      (2) ϕ is a homeomorphism on the interiors of 2-cells.
Definition 2.5.8. A 2-complex X is reducible if there exists an essential equiva-
lence Y Ñ X such that Y is visibly reducible, irreducible otherwise.
Definition 2.5.9. A 2-complex X has negative immersions if for every immersion
Y í X with Y compact and connected, either χpY q ă 0 or Y is reducible.
    One of the main theorems of [LW22] characterises when a one-relator complex
has negative immersions in terms of the subgroup structure of the fundamental
group. Recall that a group is k-free if every k-generated subgroup is free.
                            2.5. SUBGROUP PROPERTIES                              113


    Theorem 2.5.10. If X is a one-relator complex, then X has negative immer-
sions if and only if π1 pXq is 2-free.
    Louder–Wilton later introduced a stronger uniform variant of negative immer-
sions in [LW24].
Definition 2.5.11. A 2-complex X has uniform negative immersions if there exists
some ϵ ą 0 such that for every immersion Y í X with Y compact, connected and
irreducible, we have
                                  χpY q
                                               ď ´ϵ.
                              #t2-cells in Y u
Remark 2.5.12. Wise’s definition of negative immersions is more akin to Defini-
tion 2.5.11, except that reducible is replaced with ‘no free faces and not a point’.
In [LW24, Section 3.4] it is shown that these two notions are distinct even for
one-relator complexes.
     The main advantage of the uniform negative immersions property over the
negative immersions property is that it allows for much stronger results about
subgroups. This was exploited in [LW24] in which Louder–Wilton showed that
2-free one-relator groups are coherent. We will cover this result and other further
corollaries in Section 2.5.4. The following is [LW24, Theorem C].
   Theorem 2.5.13. If X is a one-relator complex with negative immersions, then
X has uniform negative immersions.
     The proof of Theorem 2.5.13 is a beautiful application of linear programming
techniques. The authors encode certain types of maps from 2-complexes to a 2-
complex X in a linear system of equations and inequalities, showing that the maps
which maximise “curvature” are rational vertices in a convex polytope. Since these
rational vertices can be realised by some map Ymax Ñ X, the “curvature” of Ymax
provides the ϵ needed to verify that X has uniform negative immersions. The
reader is invited to consult [Wil24a] in which Wilton formally defines these curva-
ture invariants for all 2-complexes, explores their properties and proposes several
interesting directions for further research.
     So far, we have only considered one-relator complexes X “ pΛ, λq with degpλq “
1 and so the reader might be wondering what can one say about the case degpλq ą 1.
In [LW17], Louder–Wilton show that such one-relator complexes have the property
of not-too-positive immersions. Stronger properties were obtained in [LW20] and
[LW24]. We state [LW24, Theorem 5.1] below which combines Proposition 2.5.4
with a result of Fisher–Karrass–Solitar [FKS72] stating that a one-relator group
with torsion is virtually torsion-free. A similar result can be found in [Wis22] (see
also [Wis20]).
    Theorem 2.5.14. If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex with degpλq ą 1, then
there is a finite sheeted cover Y í X and an inclusion ι : Z ãÑ Y inducing an
isomorphism on π1 such that Z has uniform negative immersions.
    In order to get around passing to a finite index subgroup, a one-relator group
with torsion can be considered as the fundamental group of a one-relator orbicom-
plex with a single non-trivial cone point in the centre of the 2-cell. Louder–Wilton
show in [LW20] that such complexes have uniform negative immersions.
114                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


    2.5.3. Primitivity rank and w-subgroups. Recall that an element w in
a free group F is primitive if it is part of a free basis for F . Equivalently, if
F – F 1 ˚ xwy for some F 1 ď F . Call w imprimitive if w is not primitive.
    Primitivity rank was introduced by Puder in [Pud14]. The definition plays a
key role in Louder–Wilton’s results in [LW22].
Definition 2.5.15. If F is a free group and w P F is an element, its primitivity
rank πpwq is the following quantity:
      πpwq “ min trkpHq | w P H ď F, w is imprimitive in Hu P N Y t`8u,
where πp1q “ 0 and πpwq “ `8 if w is primitive.
    The first connection Louder–Wilton discovered between properties of a one-
relator group and the primitivity rank of its defining relation is the following.
    Theorem 2.5.16. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group, then G is k-free if
and only if k ă πpwq. In particular:
     (1) G is torsion-free if and only if πpwq ě 2.
     (2) The one-relator presentation complex for G has uniform negative immer-
         sions if and only if πpwq ě 3.
    Several k-freeness results had been previously established for cyclically pinched,
cyclically conjugated and related one-relator groups, for instance, by Baumslag in
[Bau62b], Rosenberger in [Ros82] and Fine–Gaglione–Rosenberger–Spellman in
[FGRS95]. These are all encompassed by Louder–Wilton’s results.
    If w P F is an element, one might wonder what the subgroups that arise in
Definition 2.5.15 tell us about the subgroup structure of F {xxwyy. This leads us to
the notion of w-subgroups.
Definition 2.5.17. If F is a free group and w P F is an element, the w-subgroups
of F are the subgroups H ď F satisfying the following:
       ‚ w P H and πpwq “ rkpHq.
       ‚ H is maximal for inclusion amongst subgroups satisfying the above.
     The following is due to Puder [Pud14, Appendix A]. See also [LW22, Lemma
6.4].
Lemma 2.5.18. For any given w P F , there are finitely many w-subgroups and
there is an algorithm to compute them, and hence to compute πpwq.
     The upshot of the above and Theorem 2.5.16 is that there is an algorithm
to decide when a one-relator group is k-free for some k ě 1 and hence when its
presentation complex has (uniform) negative immersions.
     We briefly sketch the algorithm, due to Puder [Pud14]. Let λ : S 1 í Λ be
an immersion realising the inclusion xwy ãÑ F . Enumerate all graph immersions
γ : Γ í Λ that λ surjectively factors through:
                                               γ
                                S1        Γ ì       Λ
Using Whitehead’s algorithm [Whi36], determine in which graphs Γ the cycle S 1 ↠
Γ represents a primitive element of π1 pΓq and throw all of these away. Additionally,
throw away all graphs Γ such that there is already a graph immersion Γ1 í Λ in our
list that Γ í Λ factors through and such that χpΓ1 q ě χpΓq. Finally, throw away
all graphs Γ whose Euler characteristic do not attain the maximal value amongst
                            2.5. SUBGROUP PROPERTIES                             115


the graphs remaining. The graph immersions Γ í Λ left over each correspond to
w-subgroups of F “ π1 pΛq (under the π1 -map at the basepoint) and the primitivity
rank can be computed as πpwq “ 1 ´ χpΓq.
    In [LW22] it is shown that if H ď F is a w-subgroup, then H is malnormal
and the homomorphism
                                H{xxwyy Ñ F {xxwyy
is injective [LW22, Lemma 6.3 & Theorem 6.17]. The image subgroups H{xxwyy ď
F {xxwyy are then also called w-subgroups of the one-relator group. Theorem 2.5.16
tells us about subgroups of rank at most πpwq ´ 1, it turns out that one can also
say something about the subgroups of rank πpwq, see [LW22, Theorem 1.5].
   Theorem 2.5.19. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group, then every subgroup
H ď G with rkpHq “ πpwq is either free or conjugate into a w-subgroup of G.
     If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex, there are natural one-relator complexes
Q í X immersing into X which represent the w-subgroups. These are obtained by
taking the unique core graph immersion Γ í Λ which represents a w-subgroup of
π1 pΛq and attaching a 2-cell along the unique lift of λ. The following is [Lin22b,
Theorem 3.3.16].
    Theorem 2.5.20. If X “ pΛ, λq is a one-relator complex and Q í X represents
a w-subgroup, then there is a one-relator tower
                         Q “ XN í . . . í X1 í X0 “ X.
    This has the corollary that one can decide whether a given element of a one-
relator group lies inside a w-subgroup.
    When πpwq “ 2, Louder–Wilton show that there is only one w-subgroup.
Lemma 2.5.21. If w P F is an element with πpwq “ 2, then there is precisely one
w-subgroup H ď F .
    In light of Theorem 2.5.19 and Lemma 2.5.21, we ask the following question.
Question 2.5.22. If F is a free group and w P F is an element with πpwq ě 3, is
it true that there is a unique w-subgroup?
    In [CH23], Cashen–Hoffman used a computer to verify various conjectures
about one-relator groups with relator length that is not too long. In particular,
they answer Question 2.5.22 positively for all words w P F4 of length at most 16
and with πpwq “ 3. Note that if πpwq “ rkpF q, then w trivially has a unique
w-subgroup, namely F . The primitivity rank may also have a relation with the
stable commutator length, see [LW24, Conjecture 1.7]. This was also explored
computationally by Cashen–Hoffman.
    Moldavanskiı̆ asked the following question in the case πpwq “ 1 [KM18, 11.63].
We believe it should be true for any primitivity rank.
Question 2.5.23. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group and K is a w-subgroup.
Is it true that if H ď G is a subgroup such that H X xxKyy “ 1, then H is free?
    If Question 2.5.22 turns out to have a negative answer, then Question 2.5.23
should be modified appropriately.
116                           2. RECENT PROGRESS


    If K is a finite field of characteristic p, Ernst-West–Puder–Seidel define in
[EWPS24] the p-primitivity rank of an element w P F in terms of ideals in the
group ring KrF s of the free group with coefficients in K:
       πp pwq “ min trkpIq | w ´ 1 P I ň KrF s, w ´ 1 is imprimitive in Iu.
They proved that πq pwq ď πpwq for all q and conjectured that πq pwq “ πpwq. One
could also conjecture whether an analog of Theorem 2.5.16 holds for group rings of
free groups. Say a ring R is k-free if all ideals of rank at most k in R are free.
Question 2.5.24. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group and K is a finite field of
characteristic p, is it true that KrGs is k-free for k ă πq pwq?
    Question 2.5.24 is true when πq pwq “ 2 for the following reason. We remarked
that πq pwq ď πpwq and so G “ F {xxwyy is torsion-free. Hence, if πq pwq “ 2, then
G is locally indicable and so KrF s has no-zero divisors by Theorem 2.4.13. Since
cyclic ideals of KrGs being free is the same thing as KrGs being a domain, the
claim follows.
    If true, Question 2.5.24 is likely to be very difficult to prove. Some related
work was done by Avramidi in [Avr22] who proved that the group ring of the
fundamental group of a surface of sufficiently high genus is 2-free. A possibly
simpler and also interesting variation of Question 2.5.24 could be the question as
to whether KrGs is 2-free when G “ F {xxwyy with πpwq ě 3.

    2.5.4. Coherence. We saw in Section 2.4.5 the coherence property for rings,
in this section we cover the analogous notion for groups.
Definition 2.5.25. A group G is coherent if all finitely generated subgroups of G
are finitely presented.
    Although the coherence property for groups had been studied before, the term
appears to have first been used for groups by Serre in [New74]. The first explicit
example of a finitely presented incoherent group was provided by Baumslag–Boone–
Neuman in [BBN59]. The example they gave was F2 ˆ BSp1, 2q.
    Some of the first classes of groups shown to be coherent were the classes of
cyclically pinched and cyclically conjugated one-relator groups, a result due to
Karrass–Solitar [KS70, KS71b]. A group is cyclically pinched if it has a presen-
tation of the form:
                   xA, B | upAq “ wpBqy – F pAq     ˚       F pBq.
                                                  xuy“xwy

That is, if it is the amalgamated free product of two free groups over a cyclic
subgroup. The cyclically conjugated one-relator group are defined analogously, but
with HNN-extensions. A group is cyclically conjugated if it has a presentation of
the form:
                    @                       D
                     A, t | t´1 upAqt “ wpAq – F pAq ˚xuyt “xwy .
Karrass–Solitar mention in [KS70] that their coherence result solved a conjecture
of Baumslag’s. Later, Baumslag conjectured that in fact all one-relator groups
should be coherent in [Bau74b].
    A push to solve this conjecture by Dani Wise, and later Lars Louder and Henry
Wilton, sparked a lot of progress in understanding the subgroup structure of one-
relator groups. We will discuss all this progress in detail in this section. First
                               2.5. SUBGROUP PROPERTIES                                   117


we mention the most general, but weakest, result which was proven by Andrei
Jaikin-Zapirain and the first author in [JZL23].
       Theorem 2.5.26. If G is a one-relator group, then G is coherent.
    In general, how might one prove a finitely generated group H is finitely pre-
sented? Starting with a finitely generated free group G0 and a surjection G0 ↠ H,
we might try and choose elements r1 , r2 , . . . P G0 such that xxr1 , r2 , . . .yy “ kerpG0 ↠
Hq. This gives us a diagram of epimorphisms
(38)                  G0 ↠ G1 ↠ . . . ↠ Gn Ñ Gn`1 ↠ . . . ↠ H
where each Gi “ G0 {xxr1 , . . . , ri yy is finitely presented and so that
                                        lim Gi “ H.
                                       iÑ8

Then H will be finitely presented if and only if for some k the epimorphism Gk Ñ H
is an isomorphism. When H is a subgroup of a group with sufficiently nice proper-
ties, then it might be possible to define a ‘complexity’ on the candidate presentation,
choose relations ri in a clever way so that this complexity goes down, eventually
reaching something of minimal complexity which yields an actual presentation. This
is the main idea in the proof of coherence for instance for three-manifold groups
[Sco73a] or mapping tori of free groups [FH99].
     The ‘sufficiently nice’ properties we need for coherence results will be the uni-
form negative immersions and non-positive immersions properties. As such, we
need a version of Eq. (38) for immersions. The following is due to Louder–Wilton
[LW24] and makes fundamental use of a generalisation of Scott’s Lemma [Sco73b],
due to Delzant (see [Swa04]).
Lemma 2.5.27. Let X be a compact 2-complex and let H ď π1 pXq be a finitely
generated subgroup. There is a sequence of π1 -surjective immersions of compact
connected 2-complexes:
                            Z0 í Z1 í . . . í Zk í . . . í X
such that
                                      lim π1 pZi q – H.
                                     iÑ8
Moreover, if H is non-cyclic and freely indecomposable, then after possibly replacing
H by a conjugate, each Zi can be taken to be irreducible.
     In the next two sections, we shall use Lemma 2.5.27 to understand subgroups
of π1 pXq for X a finite 2-complex with uniform negative immersions or non-positive
immersions.
     2.5.4.1. Strong coherence and uniform negative immersions. If we plug into
Lemma 2.5.27 a 2-complex with uniform negative immersions and a non-cyclic
freely indecomposable subgroup H ď π1 pXq, then there is an ϵ ą 0 such that
                                      χpZi q
                                                    ď ´ϵ.
                                  #t2-cells in Zi u
Rearranging the inequality and using the fact that χpZi q ě 1 ´ b1 pZi q ě 1 ´ b1 pZ0 q,
we may obtain a uniform bound on the number of 2-cells:
                                                   b1 pZ0 q ´ 1
                             #t2-cells in Zi u ď                .
                                                        ϵ
118                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


Since each Zi is irreducible, every 0-cell and every 1-cell in Zi is traversed by the
attaching map of some 2-cell, implying a uniform bound on the number of cells in Zi .
Hence, all but finitely many of the immersions Zi í X are the same. Being careful
with the direct limit, this implies that H is finitely presented. To obtain coherence
of π1 pXq, one needs to combine this fact with Grushko’s theorem. With a more
careful analysis, Louder–Wilton establish a much stronger theorem on subgroups of
one-relator groups with uniform negative immersions, [LW24, Theorems A & B].
     Theorem 2.5.28. Let G be a 2-free one-relator group. Or more generally, let
G “ π1 pXq with X a finite 2-complex with uniform negative immersions. Then G
is coherent and the following properties hold:
      (1) For any integer n, there are only finitely many conjugacy classes of finitely
          generated one-ended subgroups H ď G with b1 pHq ď n.
      (2) Every finitely generated one-ended subgroup H ď G is co-hopfian, in the
          sense that H is not isomorphic to any proper subgroup of itself.
      (3) Every finitely generated non-cyclic subgroup H ď G is large, in the sense
          that it contains a finite index subgroup that surjects a rank two free group.
     By Theorem 2.5.14, Theorem 2.5.28 applies to a finite index subgroup of a one-
relator group with torsion. In particular, Item 1 from the above hold for one-relator
groups with torsion. The proof that one-relator groups with torsion are coherent
was obtained previously by Louder–Wilton in [LW20] and, independently, by Wise
in [Wis22].
     We remark that coherence, Item 1 and Item 2 are properties shared by (one-
ended) locally quasi-convex hyperbolic groups, see work of Sela [Sel97, Theorem
4.4] and Kapovich–Weidmann [KW04, Corollary 1.5]. In fact Wilton has con-
jectured that all finite 2-complexes with uniform negative immersions have locally
quasi-convex hyperbolic fundamental group in [Wil24a, Conjecture 12.9].
     2.5.4.2. Homological coherence and non-positive immersions. The same strat-
egy employed in Section 2.5.4.1 does not work for non-positive immersions. Indeed,
except for possibly the coherence, none of the conclusions from Theorem 2.5.28
hold for finite 2-complexes with non-positive immersions. In order to get around
this, we will need to consider a weaker property than coherence.
     A finitely generated group G “ F {N has type FP2 pZq if any one of the following
equivalent conditions hold:
      (1) Its relation module Nab is finitely generated as a ZG-module.
      (2) The augmentation ideal IZG ď ZG is finitely presented.
      (3) The trivial ZG-module Z admits a projective resolution P˚ Ñ Z with Pi
           finitely generated for i “ 0, 1, 2.
      (4) There is a surjective homomorphism ϕ : H Ñ G from a finitely presented
           group H such that H1 pkerpϕq, Zq “ 0.
Definition 2.5.29. A group G is said to be homologically coherent if all of its
finitely generated subgroups have type FP2 pZq.
    These definitions naturally extend to arbitrary rings R and to dimensions other
than 2. Note that homological coherence of a group G follows from coherence of
the group ring ZG by the second definition of FP2 pZq.
    A priori, it seems that homological coherence should be a weaker property than
coherence. Indeed, there are well known examples of groups that have type FP2 pZq
                             2.5. SUBGROUP PROPERTIES                                119


but are not finitely presented by work of Bestvina–Brady [BB97]. There are also
examples which distinguish FP2 pZq from FP2 pKq where K is any field by Bieri–
Strebel [BS80]. However, it turns out that within a very large class of groups the
two notions coincide. See [JZL23, Theorem 4.5], stated below.
    Theorem 2.5.30. Let CG be the smallest class of groups containing all coher-
ent groups and that is closed under subgroups, finite extensions, amalgamated free
products, HNN-extensions and directed unions. Then within CG, the homologically
coherent groups coincide with the coherent groups.
    Just as in the case of group rings from Section 2.4.5, homological coherence of
a group can be shown via a criterion which depends on an embedding of the group
ring into a division ring. The following is [JZL23, Corollary 3.4].
Proposition 2.5.31. Let R be a commutative ring and let G be finitely generated
group with cdR pGq ď 2. If RrGs ãÑ D is an embedding into a division ring, then
                   G has type FP2 pRq ðñ dimD H2 pG, Dq ă 8.
In particular, if D has weak dimension at most one, then G is homologically coher-
ent.
    Now let us plug into Lemma 2.5.27 a 2-complex X with non-positive immersions
and a finitely generated non-cyclic and freely indecomposable subgroup H ď π1 pXq.
We have a sequence of immersions
                          Z0 í Z1 í . . . í Zk í . . . í X
such that the following properties hold:
     (1) The homomorphisms π1 pZi q Ñ π1 pZi`1 q Ñ H ď π1 pXq are surjective for
         all i.
     (2) limiÑ8 π1 pZi q “ H.
     (3) Zi is compact for all i.
     (4) χpZi q ď 0 and Zi is aspherical for all i (see Theorem 2.5.7).
                               ´      ¯
                                  p1q
In particular, letting F “ π1 Z0        and Ni “ kerpF Ñ π1 pZi qq, we obtain a dual
sequence
                         N0 ãÑ N1 ãÑ . . . ãÑ Nk ãÑ . . . ãÑ F
with the following dual properties:
     (1) The homomorphisms Ni Ñ Ni`1 Ñ kerpF Ñ Hq ď F are injective for all

    Since π1 pXq is locally indicable by Theorem 2.5.7, we may use Theorem 2.4.14
to obtain a division ring embedding QrHs ãÑ D. When X is a one-relator complex,
we may instead use the Lewin–Lewin division ring from Theorem 2.4.13. We have

Since H2 pH, Dq is a D-submodule of the finite dimensional D-module D bQH Nab ,

tion 2.5.31. This is [JZL23, Theorem 1.2].
    Theorem 2.5.32. If X is a 2-complex with non-positive immersions, then
π1 pXq is homologically coherent.
    The coherence of torsion-free one-relator groups now follows by applying The-
orems 2.5.5, 2.5.30 and 2.5.32.
    It remains an open question of Wise as to whether all 2-complexes with non-
positive immersions are coherent, see [Wis20, Conjecture 12.11]. It is also open as
to whether the rational group ring of the fundamental group of a 2-complex with
non-positive immersions is coherent, see [JZL23, Conjecture 6].
    2.5.4.3. Effective coherence. A coherent group G is effectively coherent if there
is an algorithm which, given as input a finite subset S Ă G, computes a finite
presentation for the subgroup xSy ď G. For now, the following is wide open.
Problem 2.5.33. Prove that one-relator groups are effectively coherent.
     Cyclically pinched and cyclically conjugated one-relator groups are known to
be effectively coherent by work of Kapovich–Myasnikov–Weidmann [KWM05].
They show that graphs of effectively coherent groups with virtually polycyclic edge
groups, and some extra conditions, are effectively coherent. Unfortunately, the same
techniques do not work when applied to graphs of groups with free edge groups.
New ideas will be needed for one-relator groups.
     The proof that one-relator groups are coherent is very non-constructive and
so unfortunately does not shed light on further structure of finitely generated sub-
groups of one-relator groups. Thus, in order to approach Problem 2.5.33, it will be
necessary to understand the subgroup structure of one-relator groups more explic-
itly. Even the case of one-relator groups F {xxwyy with πpwq ě 3 is open and was
explicitly proposed by Louder–Wilton in [LW24].
     2.5.5. Virtual algebraic fibring and generalisations. Recall from Sec-
tion 2.3.3 that a group G algebraically fibres if there is a finitely generated normal
subgroup N ŸG such that G{N – Z. We say a group G virtually algebraically fibres
if it has a finite index subgroup H ď G which algebraically fibred. We saw with
Brown’s criterion that we may characterise precisely when a one-relator group alge-
braically fibres. Determining when it is virtually algebraically fibred is significantly
more difficult.
     Dawid Kielak obtained a powerful criterion in [Kie20] for when a group with
the RFRS property virtually algebraically fibres. A group G is RFRS if it admits a
sequence
      Ş of finite index subgroups Ni Ÿ G such that Ni`1 ď kerpNi Ñ H1 pNi , Qqq
and     Ni “ 1. The main class of examples of RFRS groups are right angled
Artin groups and all their subgroups. In particular, fundamental groups of special
cube complexes are RFRS. Kielak proved that an infinite RFRS group G virtually
                                     p2q
algebraically fibres if and only if b1 pGq “ 0.
                             2.5. SUBGROUP PROPERTIES                               121


    An immediate consequence of Kielak’s result and Dicks–Linnell’s computation
of the L2 -Betti numbers of one-relator groups is that If G is a virtually RFRS
torsion-free two-generator one-relator group, then G is virtually algebraically fibred.
Since one-relator groups are coherent, the fibre subgroup will be a finitely presented
subgroup. Using a result of Fel’dman [Fel71, Theorem 2.4], we may conclude that
the fibre subgroup drops in cohomological dimension and so is a free group by a
result of Stallings [Sta68]. These results together imply the following statement.
     Theorem 2.5.34. If G is a torsion-free two-generator one-relator group that is
virtually RFRS, then G is virtually {finitely generated free}-by-cyclic.
     Note that by [Lüc02, Theorem 7.2], a virtually algebraically fibred group G
      p2q
has b1 pGq “ 0. Hence, if G is a one-relator group that virtually algebraically
fibres, then G must be two-generator and be torsion-free by Theorem 2.4.10.
     An interesting variation of the virtual algebraic fibration problem which has no
                 p2q
restriction on b1 is the following. Given a group G with cdpGq “ n, when does
G admit a finite index subgroup which embeds as a subgroup of a group H that
algebraically fibres over a group N with cdpN q “ n ´ 1? For one-relator groups this
amounts to asking when they admit finite index subgroups that embed in {finitely
generated free}-by-cyclic groups. Under strong geometric hypotheses, Kielak and
the first author proved a criterion in [KL24] for when a one-relator group admits
a positive answer to this question, solving a problem of Baumslag from [Bau86].
    Theorem 2.5.35. Let G be a one-relator group. If G is hyperbolic and virtually
special, then G has a finite index subgroup H that is a subgroup of a {finitely
generated free}-by-cyclic group.
     The class of {finitely generated free}-by-cyclic groups form an extensively stud-
ied class of groups with a deep and rich theory, connecting dynamics, geometry and
algebra. Such groups lie within the slightly more general class of free-by-cyclic
groups, where here the free part is not necessarily finitely generated. In this gen-
erality, less is known although most results for {finitely generated free}-by-cyclic
groups can be upgraded to the more general case. Weakening the assumptions from
Theorem 2.5.35, Fisher proved in [Fis24] that any virtually RFRS one-relator group
is virtually free-by-cyclic.
    Theorem 2.5.36. Let G be a one-relator group. If G is virtually RFRS, then
G is virtually free-by-cyclic.
     Replacing the hypothesis that G be a one-relator group with the hypothesis
       p2q
that b2 pGq “ 0 and cdQ pGq ď 2, Theorem 2.5.35 and Theorem 2.5.36 remain
valid. There are also similar statements for groups of arbitrary finite cohomological
dimension in [KL24, Fis24].
     Chong–Wise show in [CW24] that a finitely generated ascending HNN-extension
of a free group is a subgroup of an ascending HNN-extension of a finitely generated
free group. The slight mismatch between the conclusions of Theorem 2.5.35 and
Theorem 2.5.36 would be resolved if a similar statement was proven for free-by-
cyclic groups (see [CW24, Conjecture 1.2]):
Conjecture 2.5.37 (Chong–Wise). If G is a finitely generated free-by-cyclic group,
then G is a subgroup of a {finitely generated free}-by-cyclic group.
122                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


     We do not know to what extent Theorem 2.5.36 characterises which one-relator
groups are virtually free-by-cyclic. Button showed in [But19, Corollary 8] that
there are residually finite one-relator groups which are not virtually free-by-cyclic
(not even virtually an ascending HNN-extension of a free group). Wise made the
following conjecture in [Wis20, Conjecture 17.8].
Conjecture 2.5.38 (Wise). If G is a hyperbolic one-relator group, then G is vir-
tually free-by-cyclic.
    2.5.6. Mel’nikov’s conjecture, finite index subgroups and infinite in-
dex subgroups. A well-known conjecture in the theory of one-relator groups is
due to Mel’nikov, first stated in the 1980 edition of the Kourovka notebook [KM18,
Problem 7.36]: if G is a residually finite group in which all finite index subgroups are
one-relator, then G is either free or the fundamental group of a closed surface. The
entry in the Kourovka notebook notes that a counterexample was quickly provided
by Churkin: the Baumslag–Solitar groups BSp1, nq each have all their finite index
subgroups isomorphic to BSp1, mq for various values of m. However, the following
modification is still open.
Conjecture 2.5.39 (Mel’nikov). If G is a residually finite group in which all finite
index subgroups are one-relator, then G is either free, isomorphic to BSp1, nq for
some n or the fundamental group of a closed surface.
     A group satisfying the assumptions of Conjecture 2.5.39 is known as a Mel’nikov
group. Some progress on Conjecture 2.5.39 has been made. For instance, Curran
studied finite index subgroups of one-relator groups with surface-like presentations
in [Cur89], characterising when they are actually surface groups. Ciobanu–Fine–
Rosenberger confirm Conjecture 2.5.39 for all cyclically and conjugacy pinched
one-relator groups in [CFR13]. Gardam–Kielak–Logan showed in [GKL23] that
2-generator Mel’nikov groups satisfy the conclusion of Conjecture 2.5.39. Jaikin-
Zapirain–Morales showed in [JZM24b] that Mel’nikov groups generated by at least
three elements are 2-free, so in particular they have (uniform) negative immersions
by Theorem 2.5.16. Finally, we mention that the pro-p version of Mel’nikov’s origi-
nal conjecture has been known to hold for a long time by work of Dummit–Labute
[DL83], where the pro-p analogue of surface groups are known as Demušhkin
groups.
     Several variations of Mel’nikov’s conjecture have also been posed. For instance,
Fine–Rosenberger–Wienke asked [KM18, Problem 20.22] whether a one-relator
group in which every infinite index subgroup is free is either free or the fundamental
group of a closed surface. This was also confirmed for two-generator one-relator
groups in [GKL23]. Wilton later confirmed this variation for all one-relator groups,
see [Wil24b, Theorem D].
    Theorem 2.5.40. If G is an infinite one-relator group that is not free or a
surface group, then G contains an infinite index non-free subgroup.
    When G is a one-relator group generated by at least three elements, Wilton
actually shows that there is a finitely generated non-free subgroup of infinite in-
dex. When G is also hyperbolic and cubulated (for instance when G is 2-free, see
Section 2.6.3), this subgroup is also quasi-convex. Whether this stronger prop-
erty holds also for two-generator one-relator groups is an open question of Wilton’s
[Wil24b, Question 5.4].
                             2.5. SUBGROUP PROPERTIES                              123


Question 2.5.41 (Wilton). If G is a two-generator one-relator group which is not
free and is not isomorphic to BSp1, nq for any n, does G contain a finitely generated
non-free subgroup of infinite index?
    A related and well-known conjecture is Gromov’s surface subgroup conjecture
which predicts that every one-ended hyperbolic group contains a surface subgroup.
Wilton made a significant contribution to this conjecture in [Wil18] when he con-
firmed the surface subgroup conjecture for graphs of free groups with cyclic edge
groups. Many special cases were established previously by several authors, the
reader is directed to consult Wilton’s article for details. The following is a special
case of Wilton’s theorem.
    Theorem 2.5.42. If G is a non-free cyclically pinched or cyclically conjugated
one-relator group, then either G contains a BSp1, nq subgroup for some n, or G is
hyperbolic and contains a quasi-convex surface subgroup.
    Whether an analogous statement holds for all one-relator groups is wide open,
although Calegari–Walker showed that random one-relator groups do contain sur-
face subgroups [CW15, Theorem 5.2.4].
    2.5.7. SQ-universality, virtual Betti numbers and largeness. A group
G is SQ-universal if every countable group can be embedded in a quotient of G.
Peter Neumann made the following conjecture in [Neu73].
Conjecture 2.5.43 (P. Neumann). A one-relator group is either solvable or SQ-
universal.
     Note that a solvable one-relator group is either cyclic or isomorphic to BSp1, nq
for some n ‰ 0, see work of Čebotar1 [Che71, Theorem 3].
     Sacerdote–Schupp [SS74a] established a criterion for an HNN-extension to be
SQ-universal (which is almost identical to the Minasyan–Osin criterion for acylin-
drical hyperbolicity from [MO15]) and used it to show that any one-relator group
generated by at least three elements is SQ-universal. Currently, the most general
result regarding SQ-universality of one-relator groups is due to Button–Kropholler
[BK16]. One of their statements is conditional on a conjecture which was resolved
by Mutanguha in [Mut21], so below we state the unconditional statement.
    Theorem 2.5.44. If G is a one-relator group, then either G is SQ-universal or
G is residually finite and b1 pHq “ 1 for all finite index subgroups H ď G.
    The virtual nth Betti number of a group G is
            vbn pGq “ sup tbn pHq | H is a finite index subgroup of Gu.
By Theorem 2.5.44, Neumann’s conjecture reduces to the case of residually finite
one-relator groups G with vb1 pGq “ 1.
Conjecture 2.5.45. A residually finite one-relator group with vb1 pGq “ 1 is either
infinite cyclic or BSp1, nq for some n ‰ 0.
     In [BK16] the authors show that any such group that is not SQ-universal must
split as a strict ascending HNN-extensions of a finitely generated free group and
must also contain some BSp1, nq for n ě 2.
Problem 2.5.46. Characterise one-relator groups G with vbi pGq ă 8 for i “ 1, 2.
124                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


      Fruchter–Morales characterise when a cyclically pinched and cyclically conju-
gated one-relator group G has vb2 pGq ă 8 in [FM24]: either G is free or the
fundamental group of a closed surface.
      A property related to SQ-universality is that of largeness. A group G is large
if it admits a finite index subgroup which surjects onto the free group of rank two.
Edjvet–Pride pose the problem of determining which torsion-free two-generator one-
relator groups are large [EP84, Problem 9]. Baumslag–Pride had shown previously
in [BP78, BP79] that all non-elementary one-relator groups with torsion and all
one-relator groups generated by at least three elements are large. In fact, something
much stronger is true for a one-relator group F {xxwyy when πpwq ‰ 2: all finitely
generated non-elementary subgroups are large by the results in Section 2.5.4.1.
      A large group is SQ-universal, but the converse is not necessarily true. For
example, BSp2, 3q is an SQ-universal group that is not large (see [EP84]). It is not
clear whether there is a natural conjectural picture for which one-relator groups
are large as the property appears far more subtle than SQ-universality. We direct
the reader to the work of Jack Button for many partial results, see for instance
[But10, But11].

                           2.6. Geometric properties
     Geometric group theory is the study of groups via their actions by isometries on
metric spaces. CATp0q cube complexes and hyperbolic spaces are two rich classes
of spaces that have taken centre stage in geometric group theory since Gromov’s
famous essay [Gro87]. If a one-relator group G acts in some non-trivial way on
one of these spaces, then a wealth of new tools become available to study G and
often this leads to significant constraints on its structure. In this section we shall
go into depth on what is known about geometric actions of one-relator groups.
     Metric spaces will always be assumed to be proper and geodesic and our actions
will be geometric, unless stated otherwise. Recall that a geometric action is one
that is by isometries and is properly discontinuous and cocompact.
     2.6.1. Hyperbolic one-relator groups. A group G is hyperbolic if it acts
geometrically on a hyperbolic metric space. Introduced by Gromov in [Gro87],
their structure is now well-understood enough that their automorphism groups have
been completely computed and their isomorphism problem has been solved, two
things which are currently far from true for one-relator groups. A simple obstruction
for a group to be hyperbolic is the presence of Baumslag–Solitar subgroups BSp1, nq.
A famous conjecture of Gersten’s predicts that this is the only obstruction for one-
relator groups.
Conjecture 2.6.1 (Gersten’s conjecture). If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group
containing no Baumslag–Solitar subgroups BSp1, nq for any n ‰ 0, then G is hy-
perbolic.
   In this section we cover recent progress on Conjecture 2.6.1, mostly based on
work from [Lin22a, Lin24a].
Remark 2.6.2. A more general version of Gersten’s conjecture was open until
recently, when Italiano–Martelli–Migliorini showed in [IMM23] that there exist
groups G of type F , containing no Baumslag–Solitar subgroups, but that are not
hyperbolic. Their examples have cohomological dimension four and arise from a
fibration of a hyperbolic 5-manifold over S 1 .
                             2.6. GEOMETRIC PROPERTIES                                125


    It is still open as to whether there exist finitely presented groups of coho-
mological dimension two without Baumslag–Solitar subgroups that are not hyper-
bolic. Groups of cohomological dimension two are quite special: Gersten proved
that finitely presented subgroups of finitely presented hyperbolic groups of coho-
mological dimension two are themselves hyperbolic [Ger96]. Combining [Ger96,
Corollary 7.8] with the coherence of one-relator groups, we have the following.
    Theorem 2.6.3. If G is a hyperbolic one-relator group, then every finitely
generated subgroup of G is hyperbolic.
    An early geometric result for one-relator groups is known as Newman’s spelling
theorem [New68a], see Section 1.6.1. A corollary of Newman’s result is that a one-
relator group with torsion G “ F {xxwn yy is hyperbolic. Below is a slight strength-
ening of Newman’s result, and a generalisation of the Freiheitssatz, which was
obtained by Schupp in [Sch76b]. Here when we say a word u mentions a letter s,
we mean that s or s´1 appears in u.
     Theorem 2.6.4. Let n ě 1 be a positive integer, let F pSq be a free group,
w P F pSq a cyclically reduced word that is not a proper power and consider the one-
relator group G “ F pSq{xxwn yy. Let A Ă S be a subset of the generators, v P F pSq
a freely reduced word and suppose that w and v mention a letter in S ´ A.
     If v, as an element of G, is contained in the Magnus subgroup xAy ď G, then
v contains a subword un´1 u0 where u is a cyclic conjugate of w or w´1 and u0 is
a prefix of u which mentions each letter from S ´ A that appears in w.
     Setting A “ H, Newman’s theorem shows that the presentation of a one-relator
group with torsion is actually a Dehn presentation (after symmetrising) and so the
uniform word problem and conjugacy problem can both be solved very quickly in
practice. More generally, Newman’s result shows that we can also solve membership
in xAy using Dehn’s algorithm. In particular, this implies that Magnus subgroups
are actually convex in the sense that the shortest words over the generating set S
that represent elements in a Magnus subgroup xAy, must actually be words over A.
Schupp’s strengthening yields the same consequences when applied to torsion-free
one-relator groups whose relator contains appearances of letters that are sufficiently
far apart. For instance, it applies to surface relators, relators of the form an bn cn dn
and many more.
     Although in general hyperbolic one-relator group might not have a presentation
with the properties from Theorem 2.6.4, it is known that Magnus subgroups are
always at least quasi-convex. That is, geodesics in a hyperbolic one-relator group
between two elements in a Magnus subgroup always remain uniformly close to
elements in the Magnus subgroup. This is proved in [Lin24a], building on work
from [Lin22a]. Note that for hyperbolic groups, quasi-convexity is equivalent to
being undistorted.
    Theorem 2.6.5. Magnus subgroups of hyperbolic one-relator groups are quasi-
convex.
    A hyperbolic one-relator group may have many distorted (free) subgroups, see
[Kap99] for an explicit example.
    The Bestvina–Feighn combination theorem [BF92] is an important tool that
can be used to show when a HNN-extension of a hyperbolic group is again hyper-
bolic. The combination theorem states that if G “ H˚ψ is a HNN-extension with H
126                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


hyperbolic, both associated subgroups quasi-convex in H and such that the HNN-
extension has a property called flaring annuli, then G is hyperbolic. Conversely,
Gersten showed in [Ger98, Theorem 6.4 & Corollary 6.7] that if H is hyperbolic,
both associated subgroups are quasi-convex in H and G is hyperbolic, then the
HNN-extension has flaring annuli.
     By the above discussion, if G – H˚ψ is a one-relator splitting, either H is not
hyperbolic in which case G is not hyperbolic by Theorem 2.6.3, or H is hyperbolic,
both associated subgroups are quasi-convex by Theorem 2.6.5 and so G is hyperbolic
if and only if annuli flare in H˚ψ . As such, if one can understand annuli in one-
relator splittings, then in principal one understands when a one-relator group is
hyperbolic or not. Unfortunately, flaring annuli turns out to be a tricky condition
to check. See the article of Kapovich [Kap99] for an explicit use of the combination
theorem to show that a specific one-relator group is hyperbolic.
     On the other hand, if G acts acylindrically on its Bass–Serre tree, then G
automatically has no flaring annuli. Hence, combining this with Theorem 2.3.5
and an inductive argument on one-relator hierarchies, in [Lin22a] it was shown
that 2-free one-relator groups are hyperbolic. In [Lin24a], a slightly more general
statement was established.
    Theorem 2.6.6. Let G “ F {xxwyy be a one-relator group. If two-generator
one-relator subgroups of G are hyperbolic, then G is hyperbolic.
    A more refined statement than Theorem 2.6.6 reducing Gersten’s conjecture to
two specific families of two-generator one-relator groups was proven in [Lin24a].
We describe these families in the next section. Combining Newman’s theorem and
Theorem 2.6.6 with Theorem 2.5.16, we have a clean condition for hyperbolicity.
Corollary 2.6.7. If G “ F {xxwyy with πpwq ‰ 2, then G is hyperbolic.
    If G “ F {xxwyy with πpwq “ 2, recall that by Theorem 2.5.19 there is a two-
generator subgroup H ď F containing w such that every non-free two-generator
subgroup of G is conjugate into P “ H{xxwyy ď G, the w-subgroup. Thus, in order
to apply Theorem 2.6.6 to such a one-relator group, one only needs to check that
P is hyperbolic. Louder–Wilton conjectured that in fact G should be hyperbolic
relative to P in [LW22, Conjecture 1.9], this remains open.
Conjecture 2.6.8 (Louder–Wilton). If G “ F {xxwyy with πpwq “ 2, then G is
hyperbolic relative to its w-subgroup P ď G.
    A well-known problem asks whether hyperbolic groups are residually finite.
This is not known for hyperbolic one-relator groups.
Question 2.6.9. Are hyperbolic one-relator groups residually finite?
     We close this section with some remarks on small cancellation conditions for
one-relator groups. Pride showed that one-relator groups F {xxwn yy with torsion
satisfy the small cancellation condition Cp2nq [Pri83]. However, it is possible that
they do not satisfy Cp2n ` 1q and may not even satisfy T p4q when n “ 2. More
refined small cancellation conditions were developed by Ivanov–Schupp and applied
to characterise hyperbolicity of certain families of one-relator groups, not necessarily
with torsion, in [IS98]. Blufstein–Minian introduce a small cancellation condition
T 1 in [BM22] and use it to prove even more one-relator groups are hyperbolic.
Cashen–Hoffmann [CH23] implemented several hyperbolicity tests for one-relator
                            2.6. GEOMETRIC PROPERTIES                             127




                  Figure 8. The 2{3-Christoffel word is a2 bab.


groups, including the small cancellation conditions mentioned above, and produced
a database of one-relator groups of relator length up to 17, verifying hyperbolicity
when possible. They found many one-relator groups whose hyperbolicity could not
be confirmed with any known small cancellation conditions. Finally, Ol’shanskiĭ
proved in [Ol’92] that one-relator groups are (exponentially) generically C 1 p1{6q
and hence hyperbolic.
     2.6.1.1. Primitive extension groups. In order to describe our two families of
one-relator groups, we shall need to introduce Christoffel words, so called as they
were first studied by Christoffel in [Chr73].
     Let F “ F pa, bq be the free group of rank two and let p{q P Qě0 Y t`8u. Let
Γ Ă R2 be the Cayley graph for Z2 with generating set ta, bu, the images of a, b
under the abelianisation map F Ñ Z2 . The generator a is the vertex p1, 0q while
the generator b is the vertex p0, 1q. Now let L Ă R2 be the line passing through
the origin with slope p{q. Now let I Ñ Γ be the path connecting p0, 0q with the
first integral point on L, not going above L and such that the region enclosed by
L and the image of this path contains no integral points in its interior. Then the
p{q-Christoffel word, which we denote by
                                     prp{q pa, bq
is the word over ta, bu traced out by this path. See Fig. 8 for an illustration.
     The notation for the Christoffel words comes from the fact that they are all
primitive elements of F pa, bq. Nielsen showed that the abelianisation map F Ñ Z2
induces a bijection between conjugacy classes of primitive elements in F and prim-
itive elements in Z2 [Nie17]. Osborne–Zieschang showed in [OZ81] that the
Christoffel words and their inverses provide a complete set of conjugacy class repre-
sentatives for the primitive elements of F . This means that if w P F is a primitive
128                                   2. RECENT PROGRESS


element, then w is conjugate to some prp{q pa, bq˘1 . Moreover, p{q is precisely the
exponent sum of b in w divided by the exponent sum of a in w.
    The two types of primitive extension groups are the two-generator one-relator
groups with presentations:
(39)                            Ep{q px, yq “ xa, b | prp{q px, yqy
(40)                          Fp{q px, y, zq “ xa, b | prp{q pxy, zqy
where
               Ai,j “ xb´i abi , b´i´1 abi`1 , . . . , b´j abj y   where i ă j P N
                  x P A0,k´1 ´ A1,k´1
                  y P A1,k ´ A1,k´1
                  z P A1,k´1 ´ 1
and txxy, xyyu is a malnormal family in case (39) and xzy is malnormal in case (40).
There are actually a couple of extra conditions on the words x, y, z in [Lin24a],
but these are just to ensure that there is no other way to express the relator as a
primitive word over words which violate the malnormality conditions. The upshot
of these extra conditions is that when we go down one step in the hierarchy for
Ep{q or Fp{q we reach a 2-free one-relator group, see [Lin24a, Theorem 3.7].
    Note that since prp{q p´, ´q is a primitive word, this implies that in Ep{q px, yq
we have xx, yy – Z and in Fp{q pxy, zq we have xxy, zy – Z.
    The motivation behind introducing these groups is the main theorem from
[Lin24a] which reduces the problem of determining which one-relator groups are
hyperbolic to determining which primitive extension groups are hyperbolic.
     Theorem 2.6.10. A one-relator group G “ F {xxwyy is hyperbolic if and only
if all its primitive extension subgroups are hyperbolic. In particular, Gersten’s con-
jecture is true if it is true for primitive extension groups.
       Let us mention some examples of primitive extension groups.
