Problem 2.6.11. Characterise non flaring annuli in the sense of [BF92] of prim-
itive extension groups with respect to the above graph of groups structures.
    2.6.2. CATp0q and cubulable one-relator groups. A group G is a CATp0q
group if it acts geometrically on a CATp0q space. Roughly speaking, a metric space
is CATp0q if its geodesic triangles are not fatter than Euclidean triangles with the
same edge lengths. We refer the reader to [BH99] for a more formal definition
and an in depth treatment of the theory. A group is a CATp0q group if it acts
geometrically on a CATp0q space.
    A class of CATp0q spaces which are easy to construct and have particularly
nice combinatorial properties are CATp0q cube complexes. A cube complex is a
combinatorial complex built out of Euclidean cubes which are glued to each other
isometrically along faces. A group is cubulable if it acts properly and cocompactly
on a CATp0q cube complex. A cube complex is non-positively curved if the link of
every vertex, which is a simplicial complex, is flag. In other words, if the link of
each vertex has no missing faces. The universal cover of a non-positively curved
130                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


cube complex is CATp0q by Gromov’s well-known link condition. The reader is
directed to [Wis21] for details.
     The question of which one-relator groups are CATp0q appears to be more sub-
tle than the hyperbolicity question. A CATp0q group cannot contain unbalanced
Baumslag–Solitar subgroups BSpm, nq where m ‰ ˘n. But a CATp0q group can
contain balanced BSpm, ˘mq subgroups. Gardam–Woodhouse considered the fol-
lowing family of one-relator groups in [GW19]:

                    Rp,q “ xx, t | t´1 x´2q tx2p´1 t´1 x´2q tx2p`1 y

They showed that R1,1 contains as an index two subgroup the {fg free}-by-cyclic
group that Gersten showed was not CATp0q in [Ger94]. In particular, R1,1 does
not contain any unbalanced Baumslag–Solitar subgroups, but is not CATp0q. The
groups Rp,q have some further interesting properties we believe are worth noting.

     Theorem 2.6.12. If p, q ą 0 are positive integers, the groups Rp,q have the
following properties:
      (1) Rp,q does not contain any unbalanced Baumslag–Solitar subgroups.
      (2) Rp,q is residually finite if and only if q | 2p.
      (3) If p ă q, then Rp,q is CATp0q.
      (4) If p “ q, then Rp,q is virtually {fg free}-by-cyclic and is not CATp0q.
      (5) If p ą q, then Rp,q has Dehn function » n2 log2 p2p{qq and does not act
          freely on any CATp0q cube complex.

    The first and the third fact are due to Gardam who introduced these groups in
[Gar17, Theorem G]. The second fact is due to Button [But19, Corollary 7]. The
fourth fact follows from a slight generalisation of Gardam–Woodhouses argument
for R1,1 . The claimed finite index subgroup is the kernel of the map Rp,p Ñ Z{2pZ
given by x Ñ 1 and t Ñ 0. Then the statement for when p ą q is [GW19, Theorem
A]. Gardam–Wodhouse deduce this last fact by showing that Rp,q contains as an
index two subgroup a snowflake group, see work of Brady–Bridson [BB00]. Note
that admitting a free action on a CATp0q space is much weaker than admitting a
geometric action.
    Other examples of CATp0q one-relator groups with interesting properties are:
       ‚ Hsu–Wise showed in [HW10] that graphs of free groups with cyclic edge
         groups are CATp0q (cubulable in fact) if and only if they do not con-
         tain unbalanced Baumslag–Solitar subgroups. In particular, all cyclically
         pinched one-relator groups are CATp0q and a cyclically conjugated one-
         relator group xA, t | t´1 ut “ vy is CATp0q if and only if there is no
         w P F pAq such that u and v are conjugate to wm , wn respectively with
         m ‰ ˘n.
       ‚ Infinite one-relator groups with non-trivial centre are CATp0q. Murasugi
         showed in [Mur64] that they are all {fg free}-by-cyclic. Pietrowski showed
         [Pie74] that they all split as graphs of infinite cyclic groups. Since {fg
         free}-by-cyclic groups cannot contain unbalanced Baumslag–Solitar sub-
         groups, Hsu–Wise’s result [HW10] implies the claim.
       ‚ Lauer–Wise showed in [LW13] that any finitely generated one-relator
         group F {xxwn yy acts properly on a CATp0q cube complex if n ě 2 and
         acts cocompactly if n ě 4.
                            2.6. GEOMETRIC PROPERTIES                              131


       ‚ Hydra groups
                           Hk “ xa, t | rr. . . ra, ts, . . .s, tsy
                                                    l jh n
                                                           k
          introduced by Dison–Riley in [DR13] are CATp0q, biautomatic and {fg
          free}-by-cyclic groups which contain free subgroups with distortion func-
          tion » Ak , where Ak is the k th Ackermann function, defined recursively
                                         pnq
          by A1 pnq “ 2n, Ak`1 pnq “ Ak p1q.
        ‚ Brady–Crisp [BC07] proved that the one-relator group xa, b | aba2 “
          b2 y – F3 ¸ Z is CATp´1q (and hence is hyperbolic), but its CATp0q
          dimension is two, whereas its CATp´1q dimension is three.
     In the next section we shall see that any one-relator group F {xxwyy with πpwq ě
3 is virtually cocompactly cubulated. The following conjecture should provide more
cubulated one-relator groups.
Conjecture 2.6.13. Let G “ F {xxwyy be a one-relator group with πpwq “ 2 and
such that the w-subgroup P ď G is virtually (cocompactly) cubulated. Then G is
virtually (cocompactly) cubulated.
     2.6.3. Virtually compact special one-relator groups. A class of non-
positively curved cube complexes known as special cube complexes have played a
particularly important role in several significant developments in geometric group
theory. Special cube complexes were introduced by Haglund–Wise in [HW08]. If X
is a compact special cube complex, Haglund–Wise showed that π1 pXq embeds into
a right angled Artin group, π1 pXq is residually finite and linear and, if hyperbolic,
all its quasi-convex subgroups are separable.
     In his monograph, Dani Wise [Wis21] developed criteria for when a group is
virtually special in terms of hierarchies.
Definition 2.6.14. Define the class VQH of groups with a virtual quasi-convex
hierarchy as the smallest class of groups containing the trivial group and such that
the following hold:
      (1) If A, B P VQH, then G “ A ˚C B P VQH if C is quasi-isometrically
          embedded in G.
      (2) If A P VQH, then G “ A˚C P VQH if C is quasi-isometrically embedded
          in G.
      (3) If A ď VQH and A ď G with rG : As ă 8, then G P VQH.
    One of the main results in [Wis21] determines when a hyperbolic group is
virtually compact special.
     Theorem 2.6.15 (Wise). A hyperbolic group G lies in VQH if and only if it
is virtually compact special.
     Wise used his quasi-convex hierarchy machinery in [Wis21], combined with
Newman’s spelling theorem [New68a], to prove that one-relator groups with tor-
sion are virtually compact special. The first author then proved in [Lin22a] that
2-free one-relator groups are hyperbolic and that all their one-relator hierarchies
are quasi-convex. The proof involved a careful analysis of the action of a one-relator
group on its Bass–Serre tree, leading to Theorem 2.3.5, proving that certain Mag-
nus subgroups are quasi-convex and using a criterion for quasi-convexity in graphs
of hyperbolic groups due to Kapovich [Kap01].
132                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


    Theorem 2.6.16. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group with πpwq ‰ 2, then
every Magnus hierarchy for G is a quasi-convex hierarchy. Hence, G has a finite
index subgroup that is the fundamental group of a compact special cube complex.
    Theorem 2.6.16 provides an answer to Question 2.6.9 for a large class of one-
relator groups.
Corollary 2.6.17. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group with πpwq ‰ 2, then G
is residually finite, linear and quasi-convex subgroups are separable.
    We conclude with a conjecture. This should be compared with a conjecture of
Wise [Wis04, Conjecture 14.2] and a conjecture of Wilton [Wil24a, Conjecture
12.9].
Conjecture 2.6.18. If G “ F {xxwyy is a cubulated one-relator group, then every
finitely generated subgroup of G is undistorted if and only if πpwq ‰ 2 or πpwq “ 2
and the w-subgroup P ď G is virtually Fn ˆ Z for some n ě 1.

                            2.7. Residual properties
    2.7.1. Residually solvable and rationally solvable one-relator groups.
Recall that Gpiq “ rGpi´1q , Gpi´1q s denotes the ith term of the derived series of G
and that the ith term of the rational derived series is the subgroup
                           ! ˇ       ”                ı        )
                     piq                pi´1q   pi´1q
                    GQ :“ g ˇ g k P GQ , GQ             , k ‰ 0 Ĳ G.
                                     Ş
A group G is residually solvable if iPN Gpiq “ 1 and it is residually rationally solv-
       Ş       piq
able if iPN GQ “ 1. The transcendental (rational) derived series is the extension
of the (rational) derived series to arbitrary ordinals.
    A one-relator group is positive if its relator is a positive word over the genera-
tors. A classical result due to Baumslag [Bau71b] states that a positive one-relator
group is residually solvable. In [Lin24c], residually rationally solvable one-relator
groups were characterised in terms of their relator, generalising Baumslag’s result.
Additionally, if G is a one-relator group and H 2 pGq “ 0, it was shown that the ra-
tional derived series and the usual derived series of G coincide using work of Strebel
[Str74], yielding a characterisation of residual solvability amongst such one-relator
groups. We now explain this characterisation and its consequences.
    The usual example of a one-relator group which is not residually (rationally)
solvable is the Baumslag–Gersten group
                         BGp1, nq “ xa, t | rat , as “ an´1 y.
Since the relator expresses a power of a as a commutator of a and a conjugate of
a, we see that xxayy is rationally perfect in BGp1, nq for n ě 2. In other words,
                                                                            p1q
H1 pxxayy, Qq “ 0. When n “ 2, we also have H1 pxxayy, Zq “ 0. Hence, GQ “
 p2q
GQ “ xxayy for each n ě 2 and Gp1q “ Gp2q “ xxayy for n “ 2. The main theorem
in [Lin24c] shows that this phenomenon is not far off from the general case.
    Theorem 2.7.1. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group, then there is some
r P F and some n ě 1 such that w P rn rxxryy, xxryys and
                                pω`1q      pωq
                              GQ        “ GQ “ xxryyG .
Furthermore, if H 2 pGq “ 0, then n “ 1 and Gpω`1q “ Gpωq “ xxryyG .
                             2.7. RESIDUAL PROPERTIES                               133


    With the notation of Theorem 2.7.1, G is residually (rationally) solvable if and
only if r “G 1 and so r is conjugate to w or w´1 by Theorem 2.2.8.
                                                                     pωq
Corollary 2.7.2. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group, then G{GQ is a residually
rationally solvable one-relator group.
    In particular, Theorem 2.7.1 characterises precisely when the only rationally
solvable quotients of a one-relator group F {xxwyy have a certain derived length:
        ‚ If all rationally solvable quotients of F {xxwyy have rational derived length
          1, then F {xxryy would have to be abelian. This means that either rkpF q “ 1
          or rkpF q “ 2 and r is primitive.
        ‚ If all rationally solvable quotients of F {xxwyy have rational derived length
          at most 2 (but not all 1), then F {xxryy would have to be non-abelian
          and metabelian. Since the Baumslag–Solitar groups BSp1, nq with n ‰
          0, 1 are the only non-abelian metabelian one-relator groups, this means
          that F {xxryy – BSp1, nq for some n ‰ 0, 1. Moreover, by a result of
          Brunner [Bru74, Theorem 2.4], r must lie in the same AutpF q-orbit of
          pb´1 aba´n q˘1 .
        ‚ If F {xxwyy admits rationally solvable quotients of rational derived length
          3 but not 2, then F {xxwyy admits rationally solvable quotients of arbitrary
          rational derived length.
    Another corollary obtained in [Lin24c] is that it is algorithmically decidable
whether a one-relator group is residually rationally solvable; one can compute the
element r from Theorem 2.7.1 and decide whether it is trivial in G.
    It is not known whether the derived series of a one-relator group in general has
length at most ω, but in [Lin24c] it is conjectured that this is the case.
Conjecture 2.7.3. Let G “ F {xxwyy be a one-relator group. There is a word r P F
such that w P rrxxryy, xxryys and
                              Gpω`1q “ Gpωq “ xxryyG .
In particular, the maximal residually solvable quotient of G is the one-relator group
F {xxryy.
    A related class of groups is that of amenable groups. Like solvable groups,
amenable groups also cannot contain non-abelian free subgroups. Unlike solvable
groups, amenable groups do not form a variety and so finding a non-residually
amenable group appears to be quite difficult. Arzhantseva has asked the following
general question, see [KM18, Problem 18.6].
Question 2.7.4 (Arzhantseva). Is every one-relator group residually amenable?
     It is not known whether the non residually solvable Baumslag–Gersten group
BGp1, 2q is residually amenable. If the answer to Question 2.7.4 is negative, then
it is likely to be negative for BGp1, 2q.
     2.7.2. Residually nilpotent and parafree one-relator groups. Recall
                                  th
that Gi “ rGi´1 , Gs denotes
                          Ş the i term of the lower central series of G and that G
is residually nilpotent if iPN Gi “ 1. In contrast with the (rational) derived series,
it is known that the lower central series of a one-relator group can have length ω 2
by work of Mikhailov [Mik16]. Thus, a solution to the following problem is likely
to be more subtle than the case of residually solvable one-relator groups.
134                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


Problem 2.7.5. Characterise when a one-relator group is residually nilpotent.
     Azarov proved in [Aza98, Theorem 1] that a cyclically pinched one-relator
group
                                  G “ F1 ˚xuy“xvy F2
is residually nilpotent if and only if either u or v is not a proper power in the free
group F1 or F2 respectively. Outside of this result, only sporadic families are known
to be residually nilpotent.
     A group G is parafree if it is residually nilpotent and if there is a free group F
such that
                            G{Gi – F {Fi        for all i P N
Baumslag provided the first non-free examples of such groups in [Bau64]. The
example he provided was the one-relator group xa, b, c | a5 b3 c2 y. Baumslag then
initiated the systematic study of parafree groups in a series of two papers [Bau67b,
Bau69a]. In [Bau69a], Baumslag proved that a parafree group must be 2-free.
The reader is directed to Baumslag’s survey [Bau05] for further background on
parafree groups and many one-relator examples. Using Theorem 2.6.16, the first
author showed in [Lin22a] that parafree one-relator groups are hyperbolic and
virtually special.
     A major open problem in group theory is Remeslennikov’s conjecture: if G
is a residually finite group with the same finite quotients as a free group, is G
necessarily free? A positive answer is known within several classes of groups, but
no systematic approach has been taken for the class of one-relator groups. Recently,
Jaikin-Zapirain [JZ23] has shown that a residually finite group with the same finite
quotients as a free group must be parafree. In light of this, understanding when a
one-relator group is parafree becomes very interesting.
     Magnus showed in [Mag39] that an n-generator group with the same lower
central series quotients as a free group of rank n is actually a free group of rank n.
Combining this with a result of Stallings [Sta65a], we see that a one-relator group
G that is residually nilpotent is parafree if and only if H 2 pGq “ 0. Hence, solving
Problem 2.7.5 would also yield a characterisation of parafree one-relator groups.
     In particular, combining Azarov’s result with these remarks, a cyclically pinched
one-relator group G “ F1 ˚u“v F2 is parafree if and only if u or v is not a proper
power and H1 pG, Zq “ Zb1 pF1 q`b1 pF2 q´1 . This was greatly generalised by Jaikin-
Zapirain–Morales in [JZM24a] who characterised when a graph of parafree groups
with cyclic edge groups is also parafree.
     2.7.3. Residual finiteness and profinite completions. If G is a group, the
profinite completion of G, denoted by Ĝ, is the inverse limit of the inverse system
of finite quotients G{N of G. The group G is residually finite precisely when the
canonical map G Ñ Ĝ is injective. A classical result of Dixon–Formanek–Poland–
Ribes [DFPR82] states that the profinite completions of two finitely generated
groups G and H are isomorphic (as topological groups) precisely when the set of
finite quotients of G and H coincide.
     A group G is profinitely rigid (within a class of groups) if for any residually
finite group H (within that class of groups), an isomorphism Ĥ – Ĝ implies that
H – G. The profinite genus of a group G is the set of residually finite groups H
such that Ĥ – Ĝ. With this language, Remeslenikov’s conjecture can be stated as
predicting that free groups are profinitely rigid.
                             2.7. RESIDUAL PROPERTIES                               135


     The study of profinite invariants and profinite rigidity of groups is an active
area of research. However, within the class of one-relator groups, very little is
known. Moldavanskiı̆–Sibyakova [MS95] showed that the Baumslag–Solitar groups
BSp1, nq are profinitely rigid amongst one-relator groups and Jaikin-Zapirain–Morales
showed in [JZM24b] that closed surface groups are profinitely rigid amongst one-
relator groups. Experimental work was also carried out in [LL94, BCH04] where
it was shown that certain families of parafree one-relator groups had different sets
of finite quotients.

Problem 2.7.6. Find more examples of residually finite one-relator groups that
are profinitely rigid amongst one-relator groups.

    Baumslag posed the question of distinguishing cyclically pinched one-relator
groups [Bau74b, Problem 1] by their finite quotients after having shown that they
are all residually finite in [Bau63b]. This appears to still be open and is likely to
be a source of examples for Problem 2.7.6.

Question 2.7.7. Are there residually finite one-relator groups with the same finite
quotients that are not isomorphic? Are there finitely many residually finite one-
relator groups with the same finite quotients?

    Baumslag showed in [Bau74a] that even within the class of virtually cyclic
groups, there exist (two-relator) groups which are not profinitely rigid so one cannot
be too optimistic regarding Question 2.7.7.
    By Theorem 2.6.16, a one-relator group G “ F {xxwyy is residually finite if
πpwq ‰ 2. This would be a natural family of one-relator groups to attempt to
answer Question 2.7.7 given how much extra structure is known to hold in these
cases. In particular, since parafree one-relator groups F {xxwyy are 2-free and hence
have πpwq ě 3, it suffices to look at these one-relator groups for Remeslenikov’s
conjecture. Wilton showed in [Wil18] that any cyclically pinched or cyclically
conjugated one-relator group F {xxwyy with πpwq ě 3 does not have the same finite
quotients as a free group.
    The first examples of pairs of finitely presented residually finite groups H, G and
a homomorphism H Ñ G that is not an isomorphism, but induces an isomorphism
on profinite completions Ĥ Ñ Ĝ were discovered by Bridson–Grunewald in [BG04],
after finitely generated ones had been discovered by Platonov–Tavgen’ [PT90].
Such examples should not exist amongst one-relator groups.

Problem 2.7.8. Prove that if G is a residually finite one-relator group and H ď G
is a finitely generated subgroup, then the induced map Ĥ Ñ Ĝ is an isomorphism
only if H “ G.

     One could also wonder whether it is worth studying profinite completions of
non-residually finite groups. However, Baumslag–Miller–Troeger showed in [BMT07]
that, given any one-relator group G, one can construct another one-relator group
H that is not residually finite but has profinite completion isomorphic to G. In
fact, the isomorphism they construct is induced by a surjection H Ñ G. The first
example of such a surjection was the abelianisation map BGp1, 2q Ñ Z, proven by
Baumslag in [Bau69b].
136                            2. RECENT PROGRESS


                          2.8. Algorithmic properties
    2.8.1. The word problem. Although the word problem for all one-relator
groups was solved almost a century ago, the complexity of the word problem has not
seen much improvement from Magnus’ original solution. One way of measuring the
complexity of the word problem of a group G is via its Dehn function δG . An upper
bound on the Dehn function of one-relator groups was obtained by Bernasconi in
[Ber94].
      Theorem 2.8.1. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group, then
                                 δG pnq À A2|w| pnq,

where Ak denotes the k th Ackermann function, defined recursively by A1 pnq “
                pnq
2n, Ak`1 pnq “ Ak p1q.
                                                pnq
     Note that A2 pnq “ 2n and A3 pnq “ exp2 p1q already grows faster than any
finite tower of exponentials. Gersten states in [Ger93] that it is an open question
as to whether for each k, there exists a one-relator group G with δG pnq Á Ak pnq.
We conjecture that no such one-relator groups exist for all k and they perhaps do
not even exist for k “ 3.
     A candidate one-relator group for having the worst possible Dehn function is
the Baumslag–Gersten group
                                       A        t
                                                      E
                            BGp1, 2q “ a, t | aa “ a2 .

which splits as a HNN-extension of the Baumslag–Solitar group BSp1, 2q where
one generator is conjugated to the other. Platonov determined the precise Dehn
function of the Baumslag–Gersten group [Pla04] after Gersten established lower
bounds in [Ger92] and Bernasconi established upper bounds in [Ber94].
      Theorem 2.8.2. If G “ BGp1, 2q is the Baumslag–Gersten group, then

                              δG pnq „ expplog2 pnqq p1q.
     One can attempt to find one-relator groups with worse Dehn functions by per-
forming iterated HNN-extensions to BGp1, 2q, conjugating one generator to the
other, and hoping this yields a growth in Dehn function comparable to that of
BGp1, 2q from BSp1, 2q. Interestingly, Bernasconi also showed that A3 pnq is an up-
per bound for the Dehn function of such groups. Other one-relator groups with
interesting Dehn functions were already discussed in Section 2.6.2.
     Another way of measuring the complexity of the word problem is via the time
function of an algorithm to solve it. The Dehn function of a group provides an
upper bound for the time function of a non-deterministic Turing machine solving
its word problem. This roughly means that we can verify that an input word is
trivial in time bounded above by the Dehn function. In general it is much more
difficult to prove lower bounds on the time complexity of the word problem than
on the Dehn function. Indeed, the following question, which appears as [BMS02,
Question (OR3)], is open.
Question 2.8.3 (A. Myasnikov). Is the word problem in every one-relator group
decidable in polynomial time?
                          2.8. ALGORITHMIC PROPERTIES                            137


    Myasnikov–Ushakov–Won [MUW11] proved the following upper bounds for
the complexity of the word problem of the Baumslag–Gersten group, lending cre-
dence to a positive answer to Question 2.8.3. Their proof used new integer com-
pression data structures known as power circuits.
   Theorem 2.8.4. There is an algorithm which solves the word problem for the
Baumslag–Gersten group BGp1, 2q in polynomial time.
     Diekert–Laun–Ushakov [DLU12] refined the complexity estimates to obtain a
cubic time solution to the word problem in BGp1, 2q and Laun extended this to
apply to any BGp1, nq for n ě 2 [Lau14, Theorem 3.1]. For BGp2, 3q the situation
is more complicated and no time bounds are known (other than those offered by
Magnus’ algorithm), but would be interesting (see [MUW11, Problem 1.8]).
     One obstacle to understanding the complexity of the word problem in one-
relator groups is that there is no general method to obtain a simple finite set of
rewriting rules over the generators to solve the word problem. In [MUW11],
the authors find a particularly nice rewriting system solving the word problem for
BGp1, 2q using the Magnus–Moldavanskiı̆ hierarchy for BGp1, 2q which expresses
it as a double HNN-extension of Z. In general, the Magnus hierarchy is more
intricate. It would be of considerable interest to develop rewriting systems with
nice properties for all one-relator groups, see Problem 1.8.5.
    2.8.2. The conjugacy, membership and normal root problems. Out-
side of a few special cases, the conjugacy problem for one-relator groups has re-
mained largely untouched. Excluding hyperbolic one-relator groups, it is known to
be decidable in cyclically pinched and conjugated one-relator groups (which includes
Baumslag–Solitar groups) by Lipschutz [Lip66] and Larsen [Lar77] respectively,
the Baumslag–Gersten group by Beese [Bee12] and for one-relator groups G with
non-empty BNS-invariant Σ1 pGq by work of Logan [Log23].
    One particular special case of the conjugacy problem which should be more
approachable in general is to determine when an element is conjugate into a Magnus
subgroup.
Problem 2.8.5. If G is a one-relator and A ď G is a Magnus subgroup, find an
algorithm which decides whether an element g P G is conjugate into A.
    Another problem whose solution should be useful for understanding the con-
jugacy problem better would be an effective version of Collins’ Theorem 2.3.2 on
intersections of conjugates of Magnus subgroups. As remarked previously, Theo-
rem 2.3.1 already has an effective version proved by Howie.
Problem 2.8.6. If G “ F pSq{xxwyy is a one-relator group and A, B ď G are
Magnus subgroups generated by subsets of S, determine the double cosets AgB ‰
AB such that Ag X B ‰ 1.
     The membership problem for finitely generated subgroups of one-relator groups
is another problem which is wide open.
Question 2.8.7. Is the subgroup membership problem decidable for one-relator
groups?
    It was shown by Gray [Gra20b, Theorem B] that there are one-relator groups
with undecidable submonoid membership problem. As such, one cannot hope to
go too far with regards to membership problems.
138                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


Problem 2.8.8 (Lyndon). If G “ F pSq{xxwyy is a one-relator group and A, B ď G
are Magnus subgroups generated by subsets of S, find an algorithm which decides
membership in a double coset AgB.
     Lyndon posed this problem for g “ 1 [Lyn62, Problem 3.6], which Newman
solved for one-relator groups with torsion in [New68a, Corollary 3]. The extended
word problem for a group G with presentation xS | Ry is the problem of determining
membership in subgroups generated by subsets of the generators S. Lyndon’s
motivation for this problem came from trying to solve the extended membership
problem in groups with staggered presentations, following Magnus’ ideas for the
extended word problem for one-relator groups.
     The normal root problem was first studied by Magnus in [Mag30]. If F is a
free group and w P F , then an element r P F is a normal root of w if w P xxryy.
Problem 2.8.9 (Normal Root Problem). Let F be a free group and let w P F .
Determine the normal roots of w.
     In [Mag32], Magnus determined the normal roots of the commutator ra, bs.
Some further work on the root problem was carried out by Steinberg in [Ste86] who
characterised normal roots of ak bl when k, l are primes. Remeslennikov conjectured
that if r is a (cyclically reduced) normal root of w and |r| ą |w|, then w is conjugate
to rr, f s or rr´1 , f s for some f P F , see [BMS02, Problem F16]. However, this was
then disproved by McCool in [McC01]. One can also ask a more specific version
of the Normal Root Problem.
Question 2.8.10. If F is a free group, does there exist an infinite sequence of
words w0 , w1 , . . . P F such that xxwi yy ň xxwi`1 yy for all i P N?
    This question is of particular interest because an answer determines whether
or not one-relator groups are closed in the space of marked groups.

     2.8.3. The isomorphism problem, Nielsen generating sets and T -
systems. Despite almost a century of progress since Magnus solved the word prob-
lem for one-relator groups, the conjugacy and isomorphism problem remain very
far from being solved in general.
     Currently the most general result in this direction is that the isomorphism
problem for one-relator groups F {xxwyy with πpwq ‰ 2 is decidable amongst one-
relator groups. This is because they are hyperbolic and one can appeal to deep
work of Sela [Sel95] (in the torsion-free case) and Dahmani–Groves [DG11] (in
the torsion case).
    Theorem 2.8.11. If G “ F {xxwyy is a one-relator group with πpwq ‰ 2, then
the problem of deciding whether another one-relator group is isomorphic to G is
decidable.
   One way to understand the isomorphisms between one-relator groups is by
understanding their generating sets.
Definition 2.8.12. Two sets X1 , X2 Ă G “ F {N are Nielsen equivalent if there
is an automorphism ψ P AutpF q such that ψpX1 q “ X2 . They are in the same
T -system if there is an automorphism ϕ P AutpGq such that ϕpX1 q is Nielsen
equivalent to X2 .
                            2.8. ALGORITHMIC PROPERTIES                              139


     Let F be a free group with free generating set S Ă F . Given two normal
subgroups N1 , N2 Ÿ F and an isomorphism θ : F {N1 Ñ F {N2 , if the two generating
sets S2 and θpS1 q lie in the same T -system, where here Si denotes the image of S
in F {Ni , then there is an automorphism ψ P AutpF q such that ψpN1 q “ N2 .
     When F {N1 and F {N2 are one-relator presentations, we may say something
much stronger. If N1 “ xxwyy and N2 “ xxryy, the Magnus property for free groups
(see Theorem 2.2.8) implies that if S2 and θpS1 q lie in the T -system, then there is an
automorphism ψ P AutpF q such that ψpwq “ r˘1 . Hence, one-relator groups G “
F {xxwyy with a single T -system of (one-relator) generating set admit a particularly
simple solution to the isomorphism problem amongst one-relator groups: given
another one-relator presentation H “ F {xxryy, to determine whether G – H, we
only need to check whether w and r˘1 lie in the same AutpF q-orbit which can be
done in polynomial time using Whitehead’s algorithm [RVW07] (see [Whi36] for
Whitehead’s original algorithm).
     The isomorphism problem for two-generator one-relator groups with torsion was
solved first by Pride [Pri77a] who showed that if G “ F {xxwn yy is a two-generator
one-relator group with n ě 2, then G has only one T -system of generating pair,
see Theorem 1.6.12. Note that G “ xa, b | a5 y has more than one Nielsen class of
generating set since ta2 , bu also generates G. However, the automorphism given by
b Ñ b and a Ñ a2 shows that ta, bu and ta2 , bu lie in the same T -system.
     Although now it is known that the isomorphism problem is decidable for all
one-relator groups with torsion, one still might wonder whether Pride’s T -system
result can be extended to obtain a more intrinsic solution to the word problem.
This question was explicitly raised by Pride in [Pri75].

Question 2.8.13 (Pride). If G “ F {xxwn yy is a one-relator group n ě 2, is it true
that G has one T -system of generating set of minimal cardinality?

    Making explicit the connection between the work of Louder–Wilton on two-
complexes X with uniform negative immersions and Nielsen equivalence classes of
generating sets for π1 pXq would be a worthwhile task. In particular, their work
should imply at least that one-relator complexes X with negative immersions have
fundamental group with finitely many Nielsen classes of generating sets of any
given finite cardinality. Furthermore, we suspect that Pride’s Question 2.8.13 can
be answered positively also for this setting.

Question 2.8.14. If X be a one-relator complex with negative immersion, is it
true that G “ π1 pXq has one T -system of generating set of minimal cardinality?

    Question 2.8.14 is known to have a positive answer for closed surfaces: if G is
the fundamental group of a closed surface, Lars Louder showed in [Lou15] that G
has precisely one Nielsen class of generating set of any finite cardinality, improving
on previous work of Zieschang [Zie70].
    See also [BMS02, Problem (OR13)] for a more general phrasing of Question
2.8.14.
    During the 70’s, a significant number of contributions were made towards un-
derstanding the generating sets of one-relator groups. We summarise below some
of the main results in this direction. The reader is directed to Section 1.8.2 for
more details.
140                             2. RECENT PROGRESS


       ‚ The first known examples of one-relator groups with more than one Nielsen
         class of one-relator generating sets were the torus knot groups xa, b | am “
         bn y. This was proved by McCool–Pietrowski [MP73].
       ‚ Delzant [Del91] showed that torsion-free two-generator hyperbolic groups
         have finitely many Nielsen classes of generating pairs (a result announced
         by Gromov [Gro87]), although even C 1 p1{6q two-generator one-relator
         groups can gave more than one Nielsen generating pair by work of Pride
         [Pri76].
       ‚ If G – BSp1, nq for any n ‰ 0, Brunner showed that G has only one
         T -system of generating pair [Bru74, Theorem 2.4] and thus every two-
         generator presentation of G is a one-relator presentation. In the same
         article, Brunner also showed that BSp2, 3q has infinitely many T -systems
         of generating pairs which are not associated with one-relator presentations
         and at least two T -systems of generating pairs which are associated with
         one-relator presentations.
       ‚ Brunner provided an example in [Bru76] of a two-generator one-relator
         group with infinitely many Nielsen equivalence classes of generating pairs
         associated with one-relator presentations. The group was the Baumslag–
         Gersten group and the one-relator presentations associated with the gen-
         erating pairs were:
                                 A             n        n
                                                               E
                     BGp1, 2q – a, t | t´1 a´2 tat´1 a2 t “ a2
         where n is any natural number. It can be checked that each relator has
         minimal length in its AutpF pa, tqq-orbit and so the associated generating
         sets                                                t
                      tt´1 a´n tat´1 an t, tu Ă F pa, tq{xxaa a´2 yy
         are also in distinct T -systems.
       ‚ Non-cyclic one-relator groups with centre may have many T -systems of
         generating pairs (they are all generated by two elements). However,
         Pietrowski [Pie74] showed that their isomorphism problem is decidable
         amongst all one-relator groups. Indeed, Baumslag–Taylor proved that
         it is decidable whether a given one-relator group has centre in [BT68].
         Pietrowski then showed that any non-cyclic one-relator group with cen-
         tre splits as a graph of infinite cyclic groups in an essentially unique way
         [Pie74] which then yields a solution to the isomorphism problem using
         Tietze transformations.
       ‚ Rosenberger solved the isomorphism problem for cyclically pinched one-
         relator groups in [Ros94b] by an in depth analysis of the Nielsen classes
         of generating sets. Some partial results were also obtained for cyclically
         conjugated one-relator groups by Fine–Röhl–Rosenberger [FRR93].
       ‚ A generic one-relator group has precisely one Nielsen class of (minimal
         size) generating set by work of Kapovich–Schupp [KS05].
Although it appears that the isomorphism problem might be easily solvable amongst
one-relator groups G “ F {xxwyy with πpwq ‰ 2, the above results for two-generator
one-relator groups show that the general case presents several additional difficulties
we do not yet know how to overcome.
